The layout of the deployment (`fedspec.py`) takes `shared`, `manifest_env` (required with `shared`: the variable of the manifest file) and `direct_io` in `import`, each refused where it contradicts another (a shared import that is writable, two spaces on one tree, a manifest inside a tree, a direct mount of a writable import); `Layout.import_manifest` checks the manifest a variable names, and `fedimport.verify_imports(layout, environ, timeout_s=...)` runs the core's import check over the rendered platform file after up, refusing one that leaves the sharing out. On HPC the owner's service binds the tree read-only: `provider.sbatch` takes `--import=SPACE=PATH` after its peers, `bundle/importstorage.py` checks each tree (a plain path, no link on the way, readable, not on node-local memory, outside the work directory) at configuration and at every start, and `importstorage.py mounts`, a control step, proves each mount, and every mount below it, read-only from the mount table of the running service's own container processes. `hpcimport.py` gives the HPC driver what it needs from the layout (`provider_import_args`, `import_storage_extra`, `luma_requests`, `mount_check_argv`, `direct_storages`), and `Bootstrap.import_storage_extra` is the hook that lets its bootstrap make the imported storage for a provider that runs as the job's user. `hpcfed.py up` takes each call at its place (`deploy/hpc-federation/DESIGN.md`, section 8) and no longer refuses a layout with an imported space: the preflight checks every variable, manifest and support before anything is submitted (and refuses an import that is not shared, two trees that overlap, a root or log directory in or over a tree, and `[hpc] core = "step"`, whose allowlist carries no variable); each owner's step is given its `--import`; a control step proves each owner's mounts read-only before the bootstrap starts a scan; the bootstrap makes the `import-<space>` storage before the support, maps the users on the storage the support names, and waits for the scan; the core's phase runs `fedimport.verify_imports` over the rendered file before the link check, and fills `@hpc_direct_storages@` for a template's direct mount. `layouts/pilot-shape-import.toml` is the pilot's shape with its four data spaces imported; `layouts/pilot-shape.toml` stays ingest-based. Written, not run: nothing of it has met a cluster (`deploy/hpc-federation/README.md` lists what a live slot must show).
- The data gate's owner check and the cell's connect-side 443 mapper, for HPC batch nodes whose loopback other users share (O61). The gate's configuration has a new key, `peer_uid_check` (`true` or `false`, `false` when absent): on, the gate serves a connection only if its peer's socket belongs to the gate's own uid, read from `/proc/net/tcp` and `tcp6` by the new sibling `gatepeer.py`, and closes any other, one it cannot attribute and everything when the tables cannot be read, with a reset before a byte is read or written and one `peer_refused` log line with the reason; off, Docker's gate is byte for byte as it was, and a gate directory of the three older files still starts (the sibling is loaded only when the check is on). The bundle gains `gateport.c`, an `LD_PRELOAD` shim that sends a TCP connect to `127.0.0.1:443`, and no IPv6 destination, to the gate's port, read from the cell's `/etc/expcore/gate.json` and, without it, `EXPCORE_GATE_PORT`, and touches no other connection; it passes on to the next `connect()`, so it is listed before the egress shim. Neither runs live yet: the Apptainer backend turns the check on and loads the shim.
- Provisioning a space by storage import (#184, O41; `docs/storage-import.md`). A space whose data provider holds it on an imported storage is declared so, `import = { readonly = true }` in `[space]` or a `[[spaces]]` entry, in the fingerprint only when declared. `expcore_onedata.storageimport.ImportedSpace(provisioner, manifest).provision(timeout_s=...)` checks the owner's Onepanel for the declared storage (imported, read-only as declared, auto mode, continuous scans off), waits for the scan, then shows the space holds exactly the dataset's manifest (`importmanifest.manifest_of_tree`, `load_manifest`, `write_manifest`) on each provider named, every file whole on the owner by its block map and, with `hashes=True`, every SHA-256; `rescan` forces a scan and records whether every id stayed; `SpaceCheck` checks an uploaded copy by the same code. The client reads three panel paths (`OnezoneClient.panel_get`) and sends one gated panel mutation (`force_scan`), all four in `UNVERIFIED_ENDPOINTS` with live tests in `tests/live/test_storage_import.py`, which compare an imported and an uploaded copy of one tree on a live federation and are not run yet. In a space on a read-only imported storage the provisioner refuses every write unsent, the progressive ingest is refused, and the day-0 round trip is unknown. `deploy/compose-federation` declares such a space in a layout (`import`, read-only from a directory named by an `EXPCORE_FED_IMPORT_...` variable, or writable from a copy under the root), makes the imported storage, supports the space from it by name with the import's config, waits for the scan, refuses `reset --clean` on it, and ships `layouts/storage-import.toml` and `make_import_tree.py` for the live slot. The fakes gain `testing.fake_import`.
- `SandboxPlatform` can be built without `slots` and `journal` (#199, O38), for a run that takes no lease, a one-off check of the data path say. Such a lease-less platform leases nothing, mints its data-path token as the fixed `[principals.data_path]` user and never uses a slot's users or tokens, and gives no run a lease group; `lease_keys`, `condition_group`, `probe`, a tool-server condition and a `launch` with a lease are refused with a `ConfigError` that names the missing `slots` and `journal`, before anything is minted. Giving one of the two without the other is a `ValueError`. A platform built with both behaves as before.
- A declared per-call bound on a call to a tool server, so a tool server's hang is not charged to the agent (#235, L17). `[agent] tool_server_call_bound_s` in `campaign.toml` (`AgentConfig.tool_server_call_bound_s`; positive and finite, optional) makes each call to a tool server (an MCP server: `McpToolProvider`, which says so with its `tool_server` attribute) run under the shorter of the bound and the agent's time left: the provider is handed that as its `timeout_s`. A code cell and any other tool that is no tool server's are never handed the bound and never judged by it: they keep the agent's time left, with or without the key. When the bound expires first, `McpToolProvider` raises `CallTimedOut` and the call is `failed` as `knowledge` infrastructure, so the attempt is set aside as for any infrastructure ending and is never the agent's time cap; when the time left is the shorter, L16's cut decides as before (a time cap, or the ceiling's infrastructure). A call to a tool server that the loop cuts at the cap or the ceiling at a reading at or after the bound's deadline had hung first, and is judged by the bound too (`knowledge` infrastructure, recorded `cut`), so the ending does not depend on which of the provider's unwinding and the loop's cut the event loop runs first. Each started call records its `elapsed_s` (`ended_s - started_s`, waits on infrastructure inside it): a new optional field of the tool call in `expcore-record/3`, absent from a call that never started. `agent_config.tool_server_call_bound_s` is a new optional key, absent when the study declares none, and beside it, and only with it, `agent_config.tool_server_tools` names the tools the bound reached, so a reader sees that the code tool was not one and a replay, whose one provider cannot say, judges a cut call by the record. Fixture `tests/data/record-v3/06-tool-call-bound.json` pins them, and a record written before them replays to its own bytes. The bound reaches the tool server as `timeout_s`, so a provider that ignores it is cut only at the time left. A study that declares no bound keeps L16's behaviour, and its configuration hash, its records' `agent_config` and its campaign's caps are the same bytes (its new records gain only the optional `elapsed_s`). Declaring one changes the caps in the fingerprint and campaign.toml's hash, so a store that began without it is not resumed with it: start a new store.
- A tight, sound bound on an abandoned request's prompt (#189, B19). Both SDK adapters bound the prompt of every request when they build it, and `route.abandoned_request` of the record, which said only `{"reason", "usage": "unknown"}`, now also holds the bound of the request that was given up: `prompt_bound_tokens` and `prompt_bound_by` (`bytes`, `window`, `none`), `reply_max_tokens` (the `max_tokens` it carried), `window_tokens`, `bound_checked`, `bound_exceeded`, `bound_unreported` and `bound_overlapped`. The prompt is bounded by the last reply's reported prompt and completion tokens plus the bytes appended since, counted as a chat template writes them (a token is at least a byte), and a margin of 32 tokens per message, and by the window for the first request, after a reply that left a count out, in a conversation where a reply reported more than its bound or a request was built while another was in flight, and for a history whose first messages are not those the last reply described (a digest check); the assumptions are stated and tested per adapter in `expcore.models.request_bound`, with a tripwire that ends the byte bound for the conversation when one fails. `Retrier` takes an `in_flight` function, read at the moment a request is abandoned. `expcore.campaign.abandoned_bound.abandoned_request_bound(spec, limits, entry, reply_capped=held_to_reply_maximum(record.totals))` prices the entry into `request_bound_tokens` and `request_bound_usd` with the basis it used, at the dearest rates of the tiers the bound can reach; an entry from an older record or a backend of a study's own is priced by the window, as before. The basis also says what the tight bound rests on: `AbandonedBound.tripwire_checked` (the requests of that model in that run that were bounded by bytes and tested against their reply), `tripwire_exceeded`, `tripwire_unreported` and `tripwire_overlapped` (kept as separate counts) and `tripwire_fired_before` (true when any of the three is above zero), read from the entry's own counts, so a study can report beside a tight bound the tests it passed; each is None for an entry that lacks it, never 0. It is recorded only: the budget gate and the reservation of C25 and C27 do not read it. The keys are additions to a free-form part of the route, so the schema and `expcore-record/3` are unchanged and an older record stays valid. See [`docs/interfaces.md`](docs/interfaces.md), "The request given up", and [`docs/study-guide.md`](docs/study-guide.md), "An abandoned request".
- A link check of every provider pair (#221, O35): before any run, each ordered (reading provider, source) pair of providers that support a space in common (n·(n-1) pairs, the local provider's among them: a mirror's link to the local provider carries a study's reads too) must fetch a probe under 1 MiB, a file of its own, from the `[link_check]` probe space of `platform.toml` (a space no agent can see) through the reading provider within `timeout_s`, or no run is admitted, the detail naming the pair and the remedy (restart the reading provider's rtransfer link, or its container alone), until a re-check passes. The health gate runs it at its first call and again every `every_s` (600 s), so a link that sticks mid-campaign closes the gate and holds the campaign; a fault injector's restore runs it for every ordered pair, not the restored provider's alone (a live slot left a mirror's link to the local provider stuck after a third provider's outage), and stays unverified until they pass; `deploy/compose-federation`'s `up` runs it last, writes `link-check.json` and is not ready until it passes. `PlatformHealth.links` holds each pair's seconds, bytes and transfers. The check reads the probe's whole block map: a provider of the probe space that `[link_check].providers` does not list and that holds any of the probe fails the pair, named, and a holding that cannot be read never counts as empty. A probe whose fetch failed is retired for good and the next check reads a new file (the next generation of the probe, a new name and id), so a recovered link passes at once instead of joining the stuck fetch of the old file: the retired file id is recorded in the probe space (a marker file) and is never read again, even when the file could not be deleted, and a pair with no readable probe and no writable fresh one fails closed. A federation with provider pairs and no `[link_check]` now admits no run; both committed layouts declare a probe space (`probe_space`).
- `expcore_onedata.linkentries.link_check_entries(listing)` and `is_link_check_entry(name)` (O50): a study's Day-0 or reset check of the link check's probe space leaves out exactly the check's own files and tells a pair's current probe from its leftovers. A listing (the `Entry` objects the core's listings return, as a list or as `Provisioner.children`'s dict, or `(name, file_id)` pairs; no provider id) comes back as a frozen `LinkCheckEntries`: `own` (every entry of the two name shapes, `expcore-link-probe-<16 hex>` with an optional `-g<N>`, N from 1, and `expcore-link-retired-<16 hex>-<16 hex>`, matched whole in lower-case hex), `current` (per pair, the highest generation among its probes that no marker retires, read as the check reads one, up to nine digits), `superseded` (every other probe; a later passing check purges them but for a name past nine digits of generation, which it neither reads nor deletes), `markers` and `other` (everything else, a near-miss name included, for the study to judge). The check itself is unchanged.
- A session gate and a bounded link heal in the fault injector's restore (O35).
  - **The gate:** once a restored provider serves, the restore waits at most `[faults] gate_timeout_s` (240 s) for two things: Onezone shows every provider of the probe space online, and each peer's block map of a probe file made before the injection holds an answer, not an error, for the restored provider. The probe file is `expcore-session-probe`, journalled with the injection as `gate_probe`. Otherwise the restore fails closed (`sessions_not_ready`) before it checks every ordered pair.
  - **The heal:** on `links_stuck`, `[faults] heal` decides; the default `"off"` fails closed as before. `"all_links"`, the primary mode, pings every provider, waits for no active transfer in a study or the probe space, restarts every provider's rtransfer link in quick succession (`ComposeDriver.ping` and `ComposeDriver.restart_link`, through `docker exec` and nodetool's `rpcterms`), settles with no fetch and no run (`heal_settle_s`, 180 s), and checks every pair afresh. A pair that still fails gets its reader's container restarted, journalled as an injection of its own and recovered first. The per-reader design (`"link_then_container"`, `"link"`) is kept.
  - **Never:** the container of the source of a failing pair or of the restored provider; a second link restart of a provider (a `badrpc_timeout` is not retried); a heal without the exclusive hold; a second heal of the same restore, by any process (a `healing` marker on a `clear_failed` line, read back as `healed`); a read through the links before a restart's settle has ended, by any process (the settle's end journalled as `settle_until`); any container restart with `heal_container_fallback = false`, where a pair the link restarts do not mend fails closed; and an injection of any provider but the condition's declared target, whatever `[faults.services]` lists.
  - **Bounds:** at most one link restart and one container restart per provider per restore, 2 passes, and `heal_timeout_s` (derived when undeclared, see Fixed), with every step's declared bounds reserved and refused at load when one round cannot fit. The tool server's stop after the restore examines the run's token (`toolserver._recheck`), which once expired sets the run aside as `tool_plane_integrity`. So a platform with `[faults]` must declare `[tokens] margin_s`, and is refused at load when it declares none or one the restore and its gate could outlast, `restore_token_margin_s` (the restore's declared bounds and pauses, 300 s of slack and the session drain a clear that takes the federation itself waits, 2,134 s with the defaults), and a heal is skipped while a step of the run that uses its tokens still runs (the tool server's and the sandbox's launches count themselves on the run's admission), unless the long margin is declared, `heal_token_margin_s` (that margin, two heals of the effective `heal_timeout_s` and the start's session drain, 10,854 s for four providers with the other defaults and 11,934 s for five at their derived 4,740 s). The skip is recorded (`heal_skipped`), and a pair that stays stuck leaves the restore unverified, never scored. The heal stops closed on any pair a step breaks (`heal_broke_pair`) and on an active transfer (`transfers_active`). Both durations are provisional.
  - **The record:** a restore the heal mends is verified, with the injector's `gate` and `heal` steps (every step by label, its result word and UTC times, the settle window, the matrices, the shared source). One it cannot mend is `RestoreNotVerified` naming what was tried.
- The fault gate's health check heals stuck pairs its own link check found, before any run's tokens exist (O35, `expcore_onedata.fault_gate_heal`). With the executor's documented order a run's tokens are already in play when its injector's start recovery runs, so that heal is skipped, and stuck pairs no restore heals used to wait for an operator.
  - **When:** only when the gate's own all-pairs link check, made in the call that is answering (the report `PlatformHealth` made in its thread, never another call's), found a stuck pair (a fetch that failed), and only in a check that holds no run's admission (never a post-run check, which keeps a share of its run's admission until it ends). A pass, an untested pair alone, an earlier report, an open injection, or a heal whose own fresh matrix shows no stuck pair starts nothing.
  - **How:** it lets its shared hold go and takes the federation exclusively (`RECOVERY`), waiting at most `health_wait_s` for every shared holder (a campaign run or a tool outside the campaign) and then for the drain record, like any admission; when either outlasts the wait the check fails closed with nothing done, as the hold `federation_busy`, which charges no run. Holding the admission, it reads its latch again. It then runs the restore's heal with no provider restored: the mode of `[faults] heal` (`"off"` heals nothing here either), one `restart_link` and one container restart per provider, 2 passes, `heal_timeout_s`, the enforced no-fetch settle (also written to the federation's drain record, so no admission in any process is granted inside it) and fresh pair reads around each action. A mode that may restart a container first arms the session gate's probe.
  - **Never:** the container of a provider that was the source of a stuck pair, in the gate's check or any matrix the heal read, even once that pair passes; a gate that opens before its own link check, made afresh under the exclusive admission (never a recent pass another call made), passes; a second heal before the gate's link check has passed again, in this process or a later one, except one after a heal cut short (below).
  - **A wait that charges no run:** the heal runs in the background, owning the admission. The check that began it, and every check while it runs, answers the uncharged deferral at once, so a heal longer than `health_timeout_s` is never counted as down and a pair it mends charges no run. A heal still running past `heal_timeout_s` plus `heal_settle_s` closes the gate, uncharged. A check that meets a heal under way waits the same way, never taking the running heal's latch, or another process's view of its unverified container restart, for a charged failure; a wait for another process's heal is bounded the same way, from its holder record's start. Whatever the heal did, the next check makes the link check afresh, so no pass from before the heal's end opens the gate, and a check that cannot make it meanwhile waits, uncharged; after a heal that failed that check finds the pair stuck and closes the gate, as for any stuck link (`FaultGate.wait_for_heal`, `PlatformHealth.wait_for_gate_heal`).
  - **A heal cut short:** a clean exit lets the heal finish the step under way and start no other (`atexit`, `FaultGate.stop_heal`), and it then writes no `ended` line, so a clean exit and a kill leave the same record: a `healing` line whose process (boot id, pid and start time) is gone. The first such heal of a stuck episode does not latch, so the next check that finds a pair stuck heals again, within the same bounds; a second one latches, so link restarts never pile up. A reader's container a killed heal stopped and never started is journalled as an injection of its own, and the next process's check restores it under its own exclusive admission, verified (a new identity, serving), then holds the enforced settle on its entry and in the drain record and answers the uncharged wait, reading nothing through the links; only a check after the settle reads the reader's session gate and clears the entry, and the gate's own link check of every pair, next in that check, lets runs in only when they pass, healing again otherwise. A container found back with no settle journalled (a recovery killed after its start) gets one held the same way, a reader whose stop was journalled and never sent is found never down and cleared, a container that cannot be started fails the check closed, and a check that meets a heal's open container restarts while its settle still closes the federation waits uncharged. The fault gate's recovery never sleeps out a held settle inside its check (`RestoreSettling`, `Recovery.recover(defer=True)`); the injector's start recovery waits it out.
  - **The record:** `gate-heals.jsonl` beside the fault journal (`healing` before the first action, with the gate's check and the healing process; `ended`, with the heal's record and each pair still failing; `links_passed`), by label only, and `PlatformHealth.gate_heal`. The gate stays closed after a heal that does not end `healed`, and the campaign holds, so nothing is scored.
  - **A `max_defer_s` the heal outlasts is refused at the start:** while the heal runs every check defers, and after an outage run a session drain comes first, with no check between them that passes, and the heal begins at a check, so a recheck and that check sit between the drain and the heal, and the check that makes the link check afresh after it follows the heal; past the campaign's `max_defer_s` (3,600 s by default) the core turns that wait into a blocking hold, on which `run`, which exits when blocked, stopped. The platform's check now declares its longest deferral to the core (`FaultGate.max_deferral_s()`, `PlatformHealth.max_deferral_s()`: `session_drain_s` plus `heal_timeout_s` plus `heal_settle_s` with a declared heal, 4,700 s with the defaults and 4,790 s for five providers at a declared 4,290 s, 5,240 s at their derived 4,740 s, and how many of the core's waits that includes; None with `heal = "off"` or without `[faults]`), and the core adds its own figures for them: a recheck (`recheck_s` and `poll_interval_s`, 31 s with the defaults) and the check that starts the heal (`health_timeout_s`, 60 s) after a drain, and again a recheck and the check that makes the link check afresh after the heal (the last deferred answer of the heal comes just before it ends), without a drain only the second pair, so **a campaign needs `max_defer_s` of at least 4,882 s with the defaults for four providers (their derived `heal_timeout_s` of 4,200 s) and 5,422 s for five (their derived 4,740 s; 4,972 s at a declared 4,290 s)** (with no drain 4,471 s, 5,011 s and 4,561 s for those three; with a `health_timeout_s` of 150 s, which the link check needs, 5,062 s, 5,602 s and 5,152 s, and 4,561 s, 5,101 s and 4,651 s with no drain), assuming the heal ends within its own bound and every check ends within `health_timeout_s` (the check's contract; the core gives up on one that outlives it but its thread can still start the heal later) and that no deferral began earlier or for another reason (an outage run still running, a run on an ingest baseline), which would count from its first answer, nor follows a heal that failed with a container restart left open, whose recovery by the next check owes a session drain before the gate closes, and the core's scheduler refuses a campaign whose `max_defer_s` is shorter when it is built, before the lock or the store, naming the platform's own seconds, each of the core's waits with its figure and the fix: raise `max_defer_s`, or lower `heal_timeout_s` or `session_drain_s`, or switch the heal off. The hook is the core's and platform-agnostic: a health check may have a method `max_deferral_s()` answering `expcore.campaign.healthgate.DeferralBound(seconds, detail, rechecks=0, checks=0)` or None (the last two count the waits between two checks and the checks' own time that only the core's settings give, which it adds and names) (`healthgate.deferral_bound`, `SchedulerConfig.check_deferral_bound`); a check without it is held to nothing. **A study that declares a heal and keeps the default `max_defer_s` is now refused at start.**
  - **A check that hides the declaration is refused too:** a study's plugin that wraps the platform's health check in a closure of its own leaves the scheduler no `max_deferral_s` to read, so the refusal above would never fire and the campaign would be held to nothing. The platform now declares the same bound a second time, without the health object: its run admission has a method `declared_deferral_s()` (`expcore_onedata.admission.run_admission`, from the same `[faults]` settings, built by `expcore_onedata.fault_deferral.heal_deferral_bound` for both), and the scheduler refuses a campaign, when it is built and before the lock or the store, where the platform declares a deferral and the health check exposes no `max_deferral_s`, answers None, or declares a smaller bound (compared as the `max_defer_s` each needs under the campaign's own waits): "the health check passed to the scheduler doesn't expose max_deferral_s, but the platform declares a deferral of N s; pass the method on", naming both figures when the check declares less (`SchedulerConfig.check_declared_deferral`, `healthgate.DECLARED_HOOK`). A check that declares the same or more agrees; a platform without `[faults]` or with `heal = "off"`, an admission without the method, and a scheduler with no health check are unaffected. **A study that wraps `PlatformHealth` passes `max_deferral_s` on** (and, if it wraps the admission, `declared_deferral_s`).
  - **The declaration is read from the platform's own configuration too, so wrapping both objects hides nothing (#299):** a study's plugin that wraps its run admission hides `declared_deferral_s` as a wrapped check hides `max_deferral_s`, and the refusal above then never fires. The platform's module now declares the bound a third time, through the registry the study already names: a module `[study] platforms` lists may expose `declared_deferral_from_config(path)` (`expcore.study.platform_deferral.PLATFORM_HOOK`), and when a campaign starts, before any study code runs, the core calls it with the platform configuration the study points at (a new optional `[study] platform_config`, a path inside the study directory, a link out of it refused at load; by default the `platform.toml` beside study.toml, which is read only where a listed platform declares from it: a missing one, or a link out of the directory, refuses that start) and hands the answers to the scheduler (`Scheduler(platform_deferrals=...)`, `healthgate.PlatformDeferral`), which holds the health check to each as it does to the admission's, before the lock or the store: "the health check passed to the scheduler doesn't expose max_deferral_s, but the platform 'expcore_onedata.sandbox.kinds' (its configuration, read through [study] platforms) declares a deferral of N s". The Onedata module answers `fault_deferral.declared_from_file`, which loads the file as a run does (`load_platform`, `load_faults`): the figure is that of the file's own backend (the HPC backend's derived `heal_timeout_s` counts the relay, the exec and the stop; the Compose one does not), and the file is held to every check the platform makes of it, the least `[tokens] margin_s` of its backend included (5,340 s on HPC, 2,134 s on Compose). A platform without the function is unaffected, a hook that fails names the platform and the exception's type and never its text, and the admission's own declaration is still compared. **A study that lists `expcore_onedata.sandbox.kinds` must keep its platform.toml beside study.toml or name it in `[study] platform_config`; one that lists a platform with the function and points at no configuration is refused at its start, naming the key.** Naming the file changes study.toml, whose hash is in the campaign fingerprint, so do it before a campaign's first start. **Listing the module is required whenever the platform declares a deferral:** a `DeferralBound` names the module it comes from (`DeferralBound.platform`, a new last field, `expcore_onedata.sandbox.kinds` for the Onedata layer), and a campaign whose run admission or health check declares a deferral that names a module `[study] platforms` does not list is refused at its start, naming the module (`Scheduler(listed_platforms=...)`), so a study cannot leave the platform out of the registry and wrap its objects. A bound that names no module, and a platform that declares none, are unaffected. **The core also sees the platform built, so a plugin that hides both declarations and lists nothing is refused too:** the platform's own loaders record that they ran (`expcore.config.platform_loads.record_platform_load(module, path)`, which `load_platform` and `load_faults` call at their entry with `expcore_onedata.sandbox.kinds` and the file, and which a platform layer other than the Onedata one calls from its own loader), the pipeline collects the records around the study's build, from its plugin's import to the scheduler's start (`watching_platform_loads`, empty at each build, so a second study in one process inherits nothing), and the scheduler refuses, when it is built and before the lock or the store, a record of a module `[study] platforms` does not list, naming the module and the file's name (`Scheduler(platform_loads=...)`, `check_loads_listed`): a plugin can hide what its objects declare, but not that the platform's loader ran. A listed module covers its own platform and the platform a listed module's function loaded to answer (`PlatformDeferral.loaded`: a study's module that re-exports a platform's function covers it even when the heal is off and the answer is None). A loader run by a platform's own tooling outside a study build (a day-0 tool, a federation script) records nothing and is never refused, and a study that builds no platform is unaffected. **A study that builds the Onedata platform lists `expcore_onedata.sandbox.kinds`, a heal declared or not.** The record holds the module and the path only, and no record, fingerprint or state file depends on it. One study build is open in a process at a time (a second thread's build is refused at once with a RuntimeError, never made to wait, and the pipeline's watch ends before the scheduler starts, so no campaign holds it), so every loader that runs while a build is open, a worker thread of the plugin's included, belongs to it, and a build that loads nothing is never refused for another's platform. A platform loaded after the study's build returned (a health check that loads its configuration at its first call), a `PlatformConfig` made by hand without a loader, and one that an installed helper retained from an earlier build of the same process, are outside it. Records and fingerprints are unchanged.
  - **A failed heal's hold says so (C11, O35):** after a heal that left a pair failing, the next check's recovery of the container restart it left open owes a session drain and a settle, so the wait can run past `max_defer_s` and become a blocking hold, and the closing on the pair charges the run that finds it. Either read like a long deferral. Every answer the gate gives in that aftermath now leads with `a failed heal: operator action needed (stuck pairs: ...)`, naming the first two stuck pairs by label (`providers[2] reading from providers[0]`) and how many more, read from the heal's `ended` line in `gate-heals.jsonl`; a heal under way, one cut short, one that mended its pairs and a wait for an outage run keep their wording. The reason leads because the core keeps the first 200 characters of a detail and the status shows 160. The status's alarms for the deferral and the closed gate then say an operator is needed instead of a run holding the platform or a gate that resumes by itself, the hold the run says carries the reason (`healthgate.OPERATOR_ACTION`, `operator_reason`), the export lists the outage under `operator_holds` (only when there is one; a merged export joins them with their shards), and the report has a section "Holds that needed an operator". A held run has no attempt, so no earlier table showed it. An operator inspects the named pairs, restarts the reading provider's rtransfer link (`rtransfer_config:restart_link()`) or its container by hand, and starts `run` again. A run whose pre-run check that heal outdated is refused at its admission (below) with the same reason first: the refusal's detail, and the `run_unstarted` event that keeps it after the run's key, lead with `a failed heal: operator action needed (stuck pairs: ...)` and then say what the epoch did, the same uncharged hold `federation_busy` with no reason added (#303); a heal under way or cut short, one that mended its pairs and a failed heal a passing link check has since ended leave its words as they were.
  - **A run checked before a heal is checked again (`expcore_onedata.admission_recheck`).** A run's pre-run check could pass before a heal, or before a pair failed, and the run then wait for its admission; once the heal let the federation go it was admitted onto a stuck link before the next link check, and so after a heal that ended `links_stuck` with no container restart. The federation now keeps a link epoch (`link-epoch.json`, `fault_epoch.LinkEpoch`), raised before any heal's first action (the gate's and a restore's) and whenever the gate's link check finds a pair failing; a raise that cannot be written spoils the record (removed, or emptied in place), which the next check makes anew under a new id, and when even that fails the gate keeps `link-epoch.lock` held, so no run is admitted, until it records the raise. The gate notes it for the run in a try note the core now opens for each checked try (`expcore.campaign.prerun`), and `run_admission`, holding the federation's lock and `link-epoch.lock` (which a link check holds shared from its reading of the pairs to its raise, so the admission waits for the checks under way, within `admission_timeout_s`), refuses the run as `federation_busy` (no attempt used, nothing scored, the reason in its `run_unstarted` event) when the epoch has moved or its record is missing or new, when the try noted none, when a heal is on record since the last passing link check, or when a check under way outlasts the bound. A link report older than the epoch is no answer, so the gate's next check makes the link check afresh and sees a failure or a heal another process saw.
    - **Never admitted blind: a campaign with `[faults]` and no health check does not start.** A try with no note at all is admitted, since nothing would ever write one, and a campaign with no health check has none. So a platform's run admission may declare that it needs a health check (`expcore.campaign.health_required`: `requiring_health_check(admit, reason)` sets the attribute `requires_health_check`, which `required_reason` reads and which a malformed value never passes as none), and `Scheduler.__init__` refuses, with a `ConfigError` before the dispatcher's lock or the store, a campaign that has such an admission and no health check, naming why and to pass the platform's health check as the plugin's `health`. The core knows no platform: `run_admission` declares it for every platform with `[faults]` (`HEALTH_REQUIRED`: an outage-capable federation needs the gate's health check, or runs would be admitted without a link verdict; pass `PlatformHealth`, which runs the fault gate). Without `[faults]`, and for an admission that declares nothing, a campaign with no health check runs as before. An admission a study wraps in a function of its own should keep the attribute (`functools.wraps` does); one that loses it is caught at run time instead: the try of a campaign with no health check now has a note that says so (`prerun.UNCHECKED`; the note is no longer absent in such a campaign, and is absent only outside a try), and `run_admission`, reached through the wrapper, and `require_admission`, which the sandbox and the tool server call before they start anything under any admission (an executor's own included, for a study that gives the campaign no run admission), refuse it as `AdmissionRefused`, so a run admission's refusal stops the campaign loudly, a start in the run's own try (an executor that took the federation itself) ends the attempt as a `harness_error` naming the refusal, and nothing is scored; the note travels in the try's context, so work run in a context of its own reads none. The declaring admission is called as the one it wraps, keywords included, and an attribute set to `None` or any other non-text is refused, never read as no need.
  - **The link-check record follows the latest check (`expcore_onedata.linkrecord`, O35).** After a heal the federation's `link-check.json` was still the pre-outage one, so the next pre-check, which wants a check under an hour old, had to run a whole `up`. A restore's link check, every read of the pairs a heal makes (its passing re-check included) and the health gate's routine pre-run check (`PlatformHealth`) now write the record (`fault_serves.recorded`, which `ServingProbe.matrix` and the gate both use), in the federation's root, where `up` writes it and a pre-check reads it, whichever platform file the check ran through (the root is the parent of the `[faults] journal` the file names, so a file derived in another directory writes the root's record and leaves nothing beside itself), in exactly the format `up` writes, and `up` writes it through the same function, which is now atomic (a draft of the writer's own, fsynced, renamed, the directory fsynced), so a concurrent reader never sees a torn file and `up`'s bytes are unchanged. Every check writes its own verdict: a failing one replaces an older pass with its failing report, naming the pair, so the latest verdict on disk is the latest check's. One check at a time makes the record (the check and its write run under `link-check.lock` beside it, `linkrecord.recording`, which `up` takes too; a leaf of the lock order), so a check that finishes late never replaces the record of one that started after it. A check that raises, and a record that cannot be written, remove the older record instead, or empty it in place and date it to the epoch when the directory refuses the removal (a warning names the error's type), so a stale pass never stands; neither fails nor passes a restore or a heal. The record is advisory: it takes none of the federation's locks, is not in the fault journal's directory and is read by no admission, and writing it never touches the link epoch, so a run checked before a heal is refused as before. `load_faults` refuses a `[faults] journal` whose parent holds no `platform.toml` (it names the key, rather than guess where the record goes), and a check run without `[faults]` declares no root and writes no record.
- A session drain after an outage run (O11, `[faults] session_drain_s`, `expcore_onedata.fault_drain`). An agent's read of a file only the stopped provider holds leaves an on-demand sync job on the local provider that nothing calls back, and a later read of the same file and range, from any session, joins it and hangs; it ends only when the run's callers end (a REST read's request process dies, a mount's oneclient has been disconnected for about 300 s plus 15 s; source-read of op-worker f118ed9, not measured), and an eviction, a transfer cancel, a delete and a link restart do not end it. So the federation admits no run before `session_drain_s` (320 s by default; 0 declares no drain) after an outage run's mount disconnected and its REST gate stopped, whichever is later. The sandbox records both ends (`DockerSandbox.session_ends`, `session_ends` in its metadata) and `SandboxPlatform.launch` reports them to the admission's `SessionDrain`; the injector arms it before it restores a provider it took down, and the wait is the remainder of one deadline, so it overlaps the restore and a heal's settle. Times that are missing mean the full drain from the restore's start, never zero. The end of the drain is written to a record in the fault journal (`federation.drain`, the wall clock, only ever later) as the admission is let go, and every admission checks it once it holds the lock (`acquire` waits for it inside its bound, `try_acquire` refuses), so it holds for the run's admission, the injector's own, the health gate's recovery and a restore that holds none (which takes the federation exclusively for the restore), and an interrupt of the process that let go cannot reopen it; the health check reports the closed federation as deferred. A recovery of a crashed run's injection owes the full drain from its own start, whatever the current run's sandbox says, and the arming comes before anything is restored, against the injections the recovery has just read; an admission reads the record again after the journal (an exclusive control now refuses an unsettled journal as a shared run does), so a restore the health gate made without the lock (a stuck holder's) cannot be raced past, and a start whose recovery restored a provider an earlier run left down waits out the drain that recovery owes before it injects (a `recovery_drain` step). Only an armed admission writes a record: a baseline run, an exclusive control and an outage run whose provider never went down write none. The injector's snapshot gains a `drain` step (the times used, the wait, and why), `FaultInjector.cancel_wait_s` counts the drain, `FederationLock` has `drain_left` and `hold_for`, `Admission` has `try_retain`, `Recovery.recover` takes `before`, and `FederationLock`, `FaultGate` and `FaultInjector` take an optional `wall` clock beside `clock` and `sleep`. A study that declares `[faults]` now waits 320 s after each outage run by default.
- The record says whether the attempt was held to the reservation R (#193, C27): two optional booleans at the end of the totals of `expcore-record/3`. `totals.reserved_capped` is the bound the budget gate reserved the run by, the value the campaign store keeps in the attempt's row (True: the reply maximum, False: the window), and `totals.reply_cap_unheld` is whether the store had taken the attempt's model off the reply maximum when the loop ended. False means the model was not marked when the loop ended, not that it was shown to hold the reply maximum: a model never probed (a stub, or a plugin's own factory) is also False. `RunRequest` carries both (`reserved_capped`, `reply_cap_unheld`) and `record_run` writes them; each is left out when no gate decided it, as in a campaign without a budget, so a scorer reads the bound from the record and never opens the store. Records written before this have neither and replay to their own bytes; `tests/data/record-v3/05-reserved-capped.json` pins the fields.
- The federation's Onedata build, recorded (#186): the images `deploy/compose-federation` pins are a 25.0 development build, not the released 25.0 tag (op-worker `f118ed9`, 81 commits after the tag; oz-worker `14b5bda`, 46 commits after it). `deploy/compose-federation/day0/2026-11-12-federation-facts-build.md` gives each component's commit and names the earlier dated files whose "25.0" means this build; the "Onedata 25.0" of the live-test paragraph above means it too.
- `expcore.campaign.shard` (#154): the interface for running one campaign as N shards on different hosts. The partition by pair, the shard spec and its operator-chosen label, the shares of caps and budget, the environment identity's form, and the names of what a shard records and a merge writes. Running, merging and reporting shards build on it.
- `run STUDY --shard i/N [--shard-label L [--shard-label-wide]]` (#154) runs shard i of N of one campaign on one host: the pairs of tasks, models and seeds assigned to it, each run at its place in the whole plan, on its share of every unit cap and budget ceiling, with the campaign fingerprint of the whole plan. The store pins the shard spec, its first start, its environment identity (`StudyPlugin.environment_identity`, read once when the shard starts) and its shares; a resume refuses another spec, label or rule, a shard resumed without `--shard` or the reverse, a changed identity or changed shares, and the plan of a shard never grows on resume. The label is 1 to 64 of `[a-z0-9-]` with no dot, so a host name or an address is refused; `--shard-label-wide` allows `.` and `_`. A shard says its environment as it starts: one block of the digest of the fingerprint's environment section and its distributions, so a host that would not merge shows before it runs. A shard's export has a `shard` object. A total below the shard count, or a shard with no run, is refused at start. Without `--shard` the store, records, export and report are byte for byte what they were. The study guide's section "Running one campaign on several hosts" explains the partition, the shares and their trade-off, and the identity.
- `python -m expcore merge OUT SHARD_OUT...` (#154): the outputs of a campaign's shards, merged into one batch under `OUT` (normally the study's `out/`): `OUT/runs` holds the union of the shards' runs, `OUT/shards/<index>/campaign-snapshot.sqlite` each shard's snapshot of its store, and `OUT/merge.json` (`expcore-merge/1`) the shards, their start times, shares and run counts, and the SHA-256 of each snapshot. It refuses, naming the shard and the reason, shards that differ in rule or count, lack an index or repeat a label, have different campaign fingerprints, overlap, hold a run that belongs to another shard or leave a run of the plan out, fail their own replay check, or recorded different environment identities (C29). `check`, `analyze`, `report`, `regenerate` and `package` then work on the merged batch as on any other: `check` binds merge.json by its hash and compares each snapshot with the hash it names, `check` and `analyze` each rebuild merge.json from the snapshots and the study's plan through the merge's own checks and refuse a file that differs, the batch's export is the shards' exports joined in plan order with each run entry tagged `"shard": index` and a top-level `"shards"`, and its scores, analysis and report equal those of one driver of the whole campaign, apart from the fields of a shard's own dispatch sequence and the witness named for a deployment fingerprint two shards both confirmed (the first shard's, where one driver names the earliest). The export of an unmerged batch is unchanged.
- Sharding a campaign by model (#154, C32): `[shards] per_model = { model-a = 20, model-b = 6 }` in `campaign.toml`, for a campaign that runs one platform per concurrent run and so has far more shards than a model has concurrent sessions. N is the sum, and `--shard i/N` must give it; shard i serves one model, the models in declared order, that model's pairs are dealt among its own shards as before, so a pair stays whole, and each shard runs one run at a time, so a model's count is its concurrent sessions. The layout is in the campaign fingerprint, and the stores pin the rule `expcore-shard/3`. A model's `per_model_usd` is split over its shards, and `max_usd` over the priced models' shards in proportion to each one's planned runs times one run's reservation, in micro-dollars rounded down, so the parts never sum past it; a free model's shards get nothing. Refused when the study loads: a count above its unit's cap, a model without a count, and a budget that cannot pay one run's reservation for every priced shard. Under either rule, a shard holding pairs of a model whose concurrency share is 0 is refused at start, naming the model. The merge checks each run by the shards' own rule, so shards made under `expcore-shard/2` still resume and merge, and a campaign split by model and merged gives the scores and analysis of one driver, byte for byte, also when only some of its models declare accepted fingerprints (the shards' exports then differ in `deployment_shares`, and a shard without it counts as no entries). Without `[shards]` nothing changes.
- The by-model shards rehearsed against a real provider outage, offline (C32; `tests/study/test_faults_in_model_shards.py`, `tests/support/faults_shard_plugin.py`, `tests/support/faults_shard_support.py`). The example study's `relay-down` condition declares `provider_outage` on `providers[1]`, and the rehearsal's plugin runs each such attempt inside the Onedata layer's `FaultInjector` (`prepared()`, `inject()`, the restore, finished after the probes) over the fake Compose services, with one task's fact held by that provider and read from the services' own state, so nothing of the outage is scripted. A campaign split by model into three shards, each on a federation of its own (root, journal, services, Compose project), injects and verifies a restore in every shard; each shard's journal holds only the owners of its own attempts; the shards share one fingerprint with `[faults]` in it; the merged batch's `scores.json` and `analysis.json` are one driver's byte for byte; and a shard run against a federation that renders another `[faults]` bound is refused at the merge, naming the shard and the key. No product code changed: the rehearsal found nothing that refused or broke.
- A campaign run in batches by factor levels (#154, C38): `run STUDY --select FACTOR=L1,L2 --shard-label L` runs the plan's runs whose declared factors have those levels (every `--select` matching) as a selection shard of the same campaign, rule `expcore-shard/4`, so a study that runs some conditions now and the rest later keeps its study, factor schema and fingerprint whole. The selection is recorded in the store, the snapshot, the export and merge.json like a shard's spec, and `python -m expcore merge` takes selections as it takes shards: they must have one fingerprint and environment identity, be disjoint and cover the plan, and pass their replay checks; merge.json numbers them by their levels and records each one's levels, mode and window, every run entry of the merged export names its `selection`, and the report's shard table names each with its levels. A pair may cross selections, and the analysis pairs the merged runs by task, model and seed, so the scores and analysis of merged batches equal one driver's byte for byte. Concurrent selections (the default) take parts of every unit cap and budget ceiling in proportion to their planned runs (`max_usd` to their reserved bound), rounded down, so disjoint covering selections never pass a total. `--sequential` gives each selection the full caps, the first the whole budget and each later one the totals less what the earlier ones actually spent, read from their stores with `--after EARLIER_OUT` (each stopped, of the same campaign and disjoint); the merge then refuses a chain whose records of the earlier selections their stores contradict, a later selection started without `--after`, and windows that overlap. With a budget the merge refuses a total spend over `max_usd` or a `per_model_usd` ceiling. Refused at start: an unknown factor or level (named), a selection of no run or of every run, a part of the budget below one run's reservation of a model it runs (named), a part of a unit cap of 0, `--select` with `--shard` or `[shards] per_model` (not in this version), and a resume under another selection. The study guide's section "Running a study in batches" shows both modes.
- The report of a merged, sharded campaign (#154): when the batch's export has `shards`, `report.md` gains a `## Shards` section with a row per shard and condition (label, start, runs planned, analysed, exhausted, unfinished and left out, and the shares of the planned runs that ended analysed, exhausted and unfinished), so a reader can see whether outcomes differ by host, and two more tables: the infrastructure attempts by shard and kind, and the wall time, turn latency and reset time by shard (median and 90th percentile with N). The merged export carries what they need, and only it does: a top-level `shard_retries_by_kind` (each shard's own `retries_by_kind` rows tagged with `shard`), and `wall_s` and `turn_latency_s` in each analysed entry (the attempt's wall time from its shard's snapshot, and each turn's latency in order from the verified record). The reset time is recorded nowhere in the core, so it reads `not recorded`, as does any figure an export lacks. The shards are a check that hosts are interchangeable, not a factor of the design: every figure is descriptive, no test across them is made, and no score is shown by shard. The export and report of an unmerged batch, and the export of a shard on its own, are byte for byte unchanged.
- The generic core, `expcore`: the agent loop with output capping and the closed outcome vocabulary; OpenAI-compatible, Anthropic and stub backends with retries and the served-model check; the MCP stdio client with frozen manifests and allow-lists; prompt templates with named slots; records, offline replay and the replay check; the campaign kernel (factor schema, manifest, store and resume, scheduling on one host, leases, budgets, export); the statistics kernel (named contrasts, the Wilcoxon cluster-bootstrap estimator, Holm, power simulation, preregistration) and kappa for cause coding; code stamps, provenance headers and the study-token check. The interfaces a study implements are in [`docs/interfaces.md`](docs/interfaces.md).
- The platform layer, `expcore_onedata` (the `[onedata]` extra): federation configuration, both token planes with fail-closed examination, the tool-server launch with its allow-list, and health checks against the declared support set. The sandbox with its per-condition data paths (a read-only mount, REST through a data gate, or none), a data gate that holds the REST token and checks every answer before the agent sees it, ordered teardown and kill causes from kernel evidence; provisioning under configured prefixes, placement confirmed by the block map and range placement by reads, bounded consistency waits, platform probes recorded as run-end instruments, leased platform users whose slots are freed only after their tokens end, a content-type check before a campaign, and day-0 checks as code.
- Replication packages: `python -m expcore package STUDY --out DIR` builds what the study's `package.toml` lists and nothing else, with a SHA-256 manifest, a study-token check over every path and content, whole-word anonymisation lists for double-blind submissions, and a deterministic archive. The package's own `make check` (`python -m expcore package-check`) verifies every file and derives the tables and the report anew from the records, byte for byte; the build runs it before it writes anything. A package vendors one wheel per distribution of the environment its replay check ran under, and its `make check` installs them into a fresh environment with no index and no network; `PACKAGE.json` names that environment, and where each distribution came from. The study-token list applies to a vendored wheel by provenance: one whose SHA-256 the study's `uv.lock` records for that name and version from the pypi.org registry is a public release and skips that list alone, and `PACKAGE.json` lists it with the lock's hash; the core's wheel and anything installed from a URL, a repository or a directory never do. `--anonymise` also gives the package a code identity by tree hash alone: no commit id and no source URL of the study or the core, anywhere in it. Files no commit holds are warned about and listed, and refused in an anonymised build unless `--allow-uncommitted`. Every build, the plain one included, writes each `key_file` and `key_env` of `models.toml` as a fixed placeholder, changing no other byte, and refuses a package whose `models.toml` still holds another, one in which any file, or any wheel not from an index but the core's, still names a key location the study's models declare (wherever no part of a path continues it, and a bare file name only quoted or after a `/`; a library that names the same variable by chance is attested in `[[opaque]]`), one that packages a declared key file itself, and a second file named `models.toml`; where a key lives is outside the code identity and the campaign fingerprint, so the shipped file verifies as it is, and moving a key never stops a campaign from resuming. A key location written where that rewrite cannot reach it (an inline table, a dotted key, a multi-line string), and a `models.toml` that is not TOML as its bytes are, are refused when `models.toml` is loaded and when it is hashed. No message of the build, findings, warnings, hints and refusals alike, shows a path, a name or a value that holds a private name or a term: every check names such a path the same way, by the start of its SHA-256, a finding is reported once per occurrence at the file's line, and `--show-private-paths` prints the paths behind the hidden names for the author's own terminal. Any exception the build meets refuses it through the same redaction, `package-check` shows its problems through the core's public list, and no command of the core or of its provenance tools repeats an argument it does not take or prints an exception unredacted.
- The campaign's environment: the core and what `[study] requires` names, with everything they require, each with its version and, for an install from a URL, its source. It is in the campaign fingerprint, so a resume under another one is refused, and the replay check and the analysis are bound to it.
- The example study ships in the wheel: `python -m expcore example DEST` writes it into a new directory, with its README pointing at the documentation of the installed core: a link on GitHub at the commit it was installed from, when it came from a GitHub repository, and otherwise each page's path in the core's repository at that commit or at its version's tag. The core holds no URL of its own repository, which would refuse every anonymised build (K11), so the link is built from the URL the install records. `scripts/fresh_repo.py`, run by the `fresh-repo` workflow after each push to `main`, makes a new repository that depends on the core by commit and runs the example end to end from the installed package, its replication package included.
- [`docs/study-guide.md`](docs/study-guide.md): how to build a study on the core, from starting one to its replication package, and how to write a platform of your own. Its code runs as part of the tests. Its last section lists what a platform shipped as a distribution of its own needs (`[study] requires`, `[study] platforms` with `register_infra_kind`, `StudyPlugin.platform`, `health_kind`, and vendoring its wheel) and points at `examples/sparql_platform/`.
- [`docs/interfaces.md`](docs/interfaces.md) states what a tool author otherwise learns from the examples: the calling convention of `FunctionProvider` (`(ToolSpec, function)` pairs, `f(arguments, *, timeout_s)`, `ctx` only when declared, a `str` or a `ToolOutput`, a worker thread), `tools_sha256` and how it differs from the hash a record carries, how `record_run` combines several providers (a tool name offered twice is refused, never shadowed), and what each field of `ToolOutput` means, including how a platform's own cut differs from the core's character cap.
- The platform probes record every transfer of the space that started in the run's window, whoever started it: the harness, the agent through the tool plane, or a QoS requirement. Every tracked provider's listing is read until two observations in a row agree, on a window moved onto the global clock, the one a job's times are stamped on, which Onezone's public current-time endpoint reads (#207); a job cancelled before it started is never recorded, since it has no finish time and the listing's walk stops at it, and it moved no bytes. Per receiving provider the snapshot splits the gained bytes into the transfers' bytes and `on_demand_lower_bound`, a lower bound on what came on demand. The bound is reported only when every input to it is established, and is otherwise unknown with its reasons. It also rests on the listing being complete and on the transfers' byte counters not under-reporting. A leased slot whose token comes back with a later expiry than it was minted with records a `token_expiry_beyond_request` event, which names the slot by its position.
- Fault injection (`expcore_onedata.faults`, O10 and O11): a sanctioned injector takes a named provider down for a run and restores it, a restore counting only when the provider's identity changed and the provider serves (its container healthy, when it has a health check, and the platform's authenticated REST read through it answered, within `ready_timeout_s` of `[faults]`, 180 s by default; the wait is recorded as `ready_after_s`, and a provider that does not serve in time leaves the injection open), with a durable journal that the next start recovers, which waits for the provider to serve too, and, for an injection a process was killed in while its stop command blocked (the journal says `stopping` before the command), waits for the provider to show down before it judges the restore. Outage runs are exclusive with every other run through a federation reader-writer lock, and the platform's health gate admits no run while an injection is unsettled. Treatment is matched to the target: a condition may declare `{kind, target}`, and an attempt is treatment only when its error names that target, the injector recorded injecting it in that attempt, and the model completed a turn. The injector's kinds, `provider_outage` and the untreatable `injector_failure`, are registered by the platform layer's one module of kinds, `expcore_onedata.sandbox.kinds`, which a study lists in `[study] platforms`. With `[faults]`, the platform's half of the campaign fingerprint (`PlatformConfig.fingerprint()`) gains the sanction and each target's service, by the target's label and the SHA-256 of the service's name, so a resume that adds or drops `[faults]`, changes the sanction or takes another service down is refused (the injector's bounds joined them later, below).
- Matched isolation for fault studies (O11): a condition may declare `exclusive = true` in the factor schema, with no fault, and its runs then hold the federation exclusively, as an outage run does, so a control can run under the same isolation as the outage it is compared with. The setting is validated at load, and a campaign whose platform gives no run admission (on Onedata, a platform.toml without `[faults]`) refuses it, naming the file and the key. Each attempt stores the admission it actually held as it was admitted (`shared`, `exclusive`, or `none` for a study without admission) and the export carries it in every analysed, excluded and exhausted entry (`admission`, `admissions`).
- One TLS setting for every client of a federation, `[tls]` of `platform.toml`, taken by the REST client, the data gate's upstream connection, oneclient in the mount sidecar and the MCP tool server. Verification stays on by default. `ca_file` names a CA bundle the federation's certificate chains to; every client trusts the bytes whose SHA-256 was taken at load, never the configured file, so a bundle swapped afterwards is not trusted. `insecure_tls = true` turns verification off, for disposable test federations only, and every health reading warns while it is on. Each run's sandbox metadata records the mode, with the bundle's SHA-256 under `ca_file`, and a plugin puts it in the campaign fingerprint through `StudyPlugin.platform` (`PlatformConfig.fingerprint()`), so a resume under another mode or bundle is refused. Oneclient takes the bundle through `SSL_CERT_FILE`, and the real tool server's use of it is checked only by a gated test (`EXPCORE_LIVE_TOOLSERVER`); only the live tests confirm either.
- A REST data path can serve complete listings. `[sandbox.data_paths]` takes a table for a level, `level = { kind = "rest", listing = "complete" }`, and that level's data gate answers every listing with all of the directory's entries, in the server's order, as one last page; it follows the pages upstream itself, so a client that reads one page (the official client's `ls`) sees the whole directory. It fails closed: a page that neither ends nor continues the listing, gives a key twice, or gives a token or an entry the listing gave before, or a page after the first that is not a 200, is the platform's 502; a listing whose entries and tokens pass 64 MiB, or that passes 10,000 pages, is refused for the gate's integrity (`listing_too_large`), so its run is set aside; a listing that names where it starts is the agent's 400. A study selects the mode per condition through the level, and the run's metadata (`listing`) and `PlatformConfig.fingerprint()` record it. The earlier study's whole-file mode is not carried over.
- The bytes each run's agent read through its data path. The platform's second instrument, `ServedBytes` (namespace `served_bytes`), records the count the sandbox's collection takes once from the log of the gate it stopped: the body bytes of the platform's answers the gate served the agent, as file content, listings as served and other answers, with the gate's own answers apart. The same count comes per file and per directory, so a study can check its agent's claimed reads file by file: `files` has a row for each file whose content was served, keyed by the file id the request's path names, with that path as the gate logged it, the number of answers, the unique byte ranges served (merged, starting where the platform's answer says) with the bytes in them, and the body bytes; `listings` has a row for each directory listed, keyed by its id. How much the tables list is declared in an optional `[sandbox.served_bytes]` table of `platform.toml` (`max_files`, `max_directories` and `max_ranges_per_file`, 1000, 1000 and 64 by default, each refused at load unless an integer of at least 1), every snapshot records the caps in effect (`caps`), and a declared table is in the campaign fingerprint, so a resume under other caps is refused. A table a cap cut says so, and `served.lookup_file`, `lookup_bytes` and `lookup_directory` read it by one rule: a file a cap left out is unknown, never not served or 0 bytes, and so is a byte past the last range a cut row lists. A mount's reads, which no gate sees, are unknown; a run with no data path read zero; a log that may lack a line makes every count and every table unknown, never zero or a part, and a log counts as whole only when it ends with the gate's own `teardown_complete` mark and the gate's output was read. The live tests (`tests/live/test_sandbox.py`, with `EXPCORE_LIVE_LISTING_PATH`) check this and the complete listing against a federation; they have not been run.
- A study's executor can check the sandbox before the agent's loop without the check entering the run's evidence. `exec(code, timeout_s, record=False)` runs as the agent's user in the agent's container and is recorded under `harness_steps` in the sandbox's metadata with its exit status and the host killer's kills it met, never as one of the agent's cells; its gate refusals and kills are not put on the agent's first cell. It runs before the agent's run only, and is refused once the mark is set or an agent's cell has begun. `DockerSandbox.mark_run_start()` then marks where the agent's run begins: the gate, on SIGUSR1, writes the mark in its own log, naming each request it had begun and not yet logged, and the sandbox reads it back before it returns. The count of the bytes served, its tables and the lookups that read them take only the answers after the mark, and those before it are counted apart (`before_run_start`); the snapshot says whether a mark was set (`run_start_marked`). Without a mark everything counts, as before; a mark the log does not confirm leaves the count unknown (`run_start_unconfirmed`). The mark is refused a second time, once an agent's cell has begun (not only ended), while a harness step runs and after the collection, and an agent's cell entered while the mark is set waits for it.
- An agent on a REST data path can use the published Onedata client, `onedatarestfsspec`, as it is. The client refuses to start without a token, so the agent's environment holds `ONEDATA_TOKEN` set to a placeholder, `[sandbox] placeholder_token` (`placeholder` by default; 1 to 128 printable ASCII characters without white space), which is no credential, is in the fingerprint by its SHA-256 alone, and refuses the start if it equals the run's token. The client verifies TLS against the certifi bundle of the agent's image and reads none of the CA variables the sandbox sets, so on a REST data path the sandbox asks the image's interpreter where that bundle is and mounts it with the run's CA appended, as it does for each declared `trust_paths` entry; an image without certifi starts without it and the run's metadata says `not_in_image` (`certifi`), and an image that cannot say where its bundle is refuses the start. The data gate now drops every header in which the platform reads a token: besides `X-Auth-Token`, `Authorization` and `Cookie`, the deprecated `Macaroon` and `X-Onedata-Consumer-Token` and `X-Onedata-Service-Token`, which it forwarded before (the platform still read its own `X-Auth-Token` first). The client is a pinned development dependency, and a test runs it through the gate with the placeholder and the default trust; a live test does the same in a real agent and has not been run.
- A study plugs in a model backend of its own without editing the core: its `models.toml` entries name it with a plain lower-case `backend` value, and `StudyPlugin.backend_factory`, which `run_study` passes to the scheduler, builds it for admission and every run. The factory decides which models it serves and hands the others to `default_backend_factory`. A model whose backend the core does not build is refused when the plugin is loaded, naming `models.toml` and the key, unless the plugin supplies a factory, and the core's own factory refuses to build it. A plugin's factory must carry a `version`; its name and version are in the campaign fingerprint, so a resume under another is refused, and a study on the core's factory keeps its fingerprint. A factory whose module's file lies outside `[study] code` must declare the installed version of the distribution that owns that file (compared as versions), or the campaign is refused naming both, so changed backend code cannot resume under an unchanged version; a file nothing owns is refused, and one in `[study] code` is covered by the code identity.
- A study's conditions can declare a platform's infrastructure kind or the study's own as their treatment. `[study] platforms` in `study.toml` names modules imported before the study is validated, so the kinds they register with `register_infra_kind` are known, and a module that cannot be imported is refused, naming the file, the key, the module and the exception's type, never its text; `[study.kinds]` declares the study's own kinds, held only while that study is loaded or run (`errors.study_kinds`), so they do not outlast it, and one that is a core or a platform kind is refused. Scopes that overlap in one process are counted: each study keeps its kinds, and one name declared differently while they overlap is refused. Neither key is part of the factor schema, so a study that uses neither keeps its campaign fingerprint and schema digest.
- A harness defect's traceback, which the attempt does not keep, goes to a local log, `out/logs/harness-errors.log`: one entry per defect, whether the executor raised it or the loop caught it, headed by the run key, the attempt and the stack hash the marker, the record and the store hold, redacted with the attempt's whole redaction context (`RunRequest.defect_log`, which `record_run` hands to `record_attempt` and the loop's new `on_defect`), and withheld with the reason when a secret is too short to redact. An entry that cannot be written never costs the record or the marker: the failure is kept, and once the result is stored dispatch stops loudly, naming the log. The loop guards any sink the same way: what an executor's own sink raises is carried on the result (`LoopResult.sink_failure`), handed on once the record is built (`record_attempt`'s `on_sink_failure`, which `record_run` points at the attempt's log) and reported the same way. The directory ignores itself for git (a `.gitignore` the author put there is kept, and ends with the line `/harness-errors.log`, which the last-rule-wins order makes ignore the log whatever came before), a replication package never selects it whatever its patterns, and `run` prints the log's path when harness errors stop dispatch. The plugin's "failed on import" message on `run` and `analyze` shows the exception's text redacted as a record is, the keys `models.toml` points at included, redacting a value too short to replace everywhere only where it stands as a token of its own (so it cannot wipe the diagnostic, while a record refuses it), and names the log, where that failure's traceback goes too.
- `run` says each hold of the campaign on stderr as it begins, changes or ends, once and not at every pass of the dispatcher: the subject, the kind, the reason and when dispatch resumes (the time a unit's pause ends, the health gate's recheck interval, or that the hold does not end by itself; a gate closed because the check stayed deferred past `max_defer_s` is said as one that may not end by itself, with the passing check that would end it). A pause extended while it holds is said again, once, with its new end: `Hold` gains `until`, which a change of counts as a change of the hold, and the store's hold event gives it. `Scheduler` takes an `on_hold` callable for this, and `HoldRegistry` an `on_change`.
- A throwaway Onedata federation for the live tests and federation studies, `deploy/compose-federation`: a Onezone and Oneproviders in Docker Compose on the pinned images, as a layout file declares them (`federation.py --layout PATH`; by default one Onezone and three Oneproviders), with its own CA, every port on the host's loopback, brought up and taken down by one command each (`federation.py up|down`), and the `platform.toml` a fresh tester wrote for it from the guides alone. It has two resets, and a bare `reset` is refused naming both: `reset --clean` brings every space back to a synthetic baseline, ingested data removed; `reset --replicas`, for between runs, keeps every owner's data and evicts only what the mirrors hold, verified on the block maps, and refuses to evict anything unless every owner holds each of its files whole. A layout declares the Compose project, the services' ports and memory limits, each space's owner and mirrors, and the fixed users; it is checked key by key, and the Compose file is generated from it. The root lies in an allowed parent, `EXPCORE_FED_ROOT_PARENT`, which a study confined to its own directory may set.
- A second platform as an example, `examples/sparql_platform/`, built only on the documented interfaces: a distribution of its own (`sparql-platform`) with an in-process RDF store (pyoxigraph), read-only SPARQL tools whose results are capped and marked `source_truncated` when cut, a health check, an instrument that snapshots the store at each run's start and end, and an infrastructure kind registered as documented. A small study on it, with two conditions that differ in tool exposure, runs end to end with its replication package in `tests/e2e/test_sparql_platform.py`. The core takes the platform as a development dependency only; it is not in the core's wheel.
- A ratio estimator for outcomes per unit of another, such as tokens per correct result (#138, S13). A contrast declares `estimator = "ratio"` with a `numerator` and a `denominator` outcome in place of `outcome`, and a rule for a zero sum, `zero_denominator = "exclude"` or `"continuity"` with a positive `continuity_correction`; neither is defaulted. In each cluster each arm's sums run over its used pairs, every task, model and seed together, so a failed run's cost still counts; the cluster's value is the natural log of A's ratio over B's, and the estimate, interval, margin and minimum detectable effect are on that log scale, from the kernel's signed-rank test and cluster bootstrap. A cluster the rule excludes is counted by pair under `zero_denominator` or `zero_numerator`, and a pair lost because a run's numerator is None while its denominator is 0 under `numerator_none_at_zero`, which refuses a confirmatory contrast (`RatioNumeratorRefused`, like `TreatmentOutcomesRefused` a `ContrastRefused`). Additive in `analysis.json`: every contrast has `ratio` (null unless it is one) and a ratio contrast's `pairs_missing` has the three causes. The report shows a ratio contrast's outcome as `ln(numerator/denominator)`, adds a "Ratio contrasts" section and the three causes to the pairs table; a report without one is unchanged. An estimator registers the form of contrast it reads (`register(..., form="ratio")`, `estimators.form`); a study's estimator keeps the difference form. A ratio contrast declares its estimation unit, the cell its sums run over, and keeps its `cluster` as the independent unit: `estimation_unit = ["group"]`, the default, pools the models of each value, and `estimation_unit = ["model", "group"]` sums each model's cell of a value apart, so the zero rule excludes and counts cells, a value's log ratio is the mean of its cells', each model weighing the same, a value drops only when every cell of it is lost, and the test, the bootstrap (drawing values with all their cells) and the minimum detectable effect count values, never cells; `per_model` gives each model's estimate over its own cells from the same draws, an exploratory breakdown the report shows as "Ratio contrasts by model (exploratory)". `cluster = ["model", "group"]`, which would count the cells of one value as independent units, is refused on every contrast, naming why. Additive in `analysis.json`: every contrast has `estimation_unit`, `independent_unit`, `n_cells` and `cells_lost` (by cause), and the report's ratio table names both units and the cells. A contrast of one outcome is unchanged: it already estimates per (model, value) cell, averages a value's models with equal weight, and tests and resamples values, and its record now says so.
