Each ratio contrast names its own numerator column, so two bounds of an outcome are two declared contrasts.
- One-arm estimates and each arm's lost runs (#139, S14). A preregistration may declare `[descriptives.<name>]` with `conditions` (one or several, pooled), `cluster`, and an `outcome` (a rate or share, aggregated to clusters as contrasts are) or a `numerator` and a `denominator` (a ratio of totals over every run, a failed run's numerator included). Each has an estimate and the kernel's cluster-bootstrap interval at its own seed, no test and no place in any Holm family; `analysis.json` holds them under `descriptives`, and the report in a section "One-arm estimates (descriptive)", shown only when one is declared. Every contrast result now carries `arm_runs`: per arm, its planned runs of the contrast's planned pairs, finished, measured, and lost by cause, and the report has a table "Runs of each arm" with each arm's finish rate. That table changes every report with a contrast, the example's included (`tests/data/tiny_study_report.md`, regenerated); `analysis.json` gains `arm_runs` on every contrast and `descriptives` at the top.
- Equivalence claims under Holm, and their feasibility (#140, S15). `equivalence_family = [...]` in `preregistration.toml` names members of the Holm family with an `equivalence_margin`; each member's TOST p-value is Holm-adjusted over the family, and a member is `equivalent` only when Holm rejects. For the Wilcoxon cluster bootstrap the TOST is Schuirmann's two one-sided t-tests of the mean cluster difference (`contrasts.tost_t`), NaN with fewer than two clusters or no spread, so one cluster is never evidence of equivalence. Its result carries `in_equivalence_family`, `tost_p`, `tost_p_holm`, `equivalence_level` and `equivalence_interval_adjusted` (the test's dual interval at 1 - 2 times its Holm level), and the analysis `equivalence_holm`; the report has a table "Equivalence family" only when one is declared, and the terminal adds a member's Holm TOST p. An estimator gives its TOST by registering `tost=Tost(p, interval, p_values)` (`estimators.tost_function`). `analysis.equivalence_feasibility(prereg, rows=None)` simulates, for each member, the chance of concluding equivalent at a true 0 and at half the margin, from the declared SDs or a pilot's, at the target clusters and alpha over the family size, judging each study by the member's own estimator's test, and says whether each claim is reachable at the target power (`power.equivalence_feasibility`, independent of the pipeline). A contrast with a margin outside the family is judged by its 1 - 2 alpha interval, as before.
- A smallest effect of interest per contrast (#81, S16). A contrast of one outcome may declare `smallest_effect`, a positive difference on its outcome's scale, and a ratio contrast `smallest_ratio`, a positive ratio other than 1 (2 is a factor of two), compared as its natural log since the ratio estimator works in logs; beside either, an optional `direction = "higher"` or `"lower"`, the claimed sign of A - B. The wrong key for the contrast's form, a non-positive value, a ratio of 1 and a decrease declared as `"higher"` are refused naming the key. The result's `smallest_effect` gives the decision under the study's rule, significant (Holm's decision for a member) with the estimate at or beyond it on the claimed side: `meets`, `meets_either_direction` (no direction declared), `opposite_direction` (significant against the claim, whatever its size), `falls_short`, `not_significant` or `untestable`; where the interval lies against it (`beyond`, `includes`, `short_of`); and whether the design detects it, the minimum detectable effect at most it, with a ratio contrast's minimum detectable ratio. The report has a section "Smallest effects of interest" with the signed estimate and interval (and their ratios) against it, and the terminal line adds the decision. Additive and only when declared: a contrast that declares none has no `smallest_effect` key in `analysis.json`, and its `analysis.json` and report keep their bytes. An equivalence margin is another declaration; a contrast may hold both.
- Several named Holm families in one preregistration (#76, S17). A `[holm_families]` table in `preregistration.toml`, in place of `holm_family`, names each family and its contrasts (`phase1 = ["h1a", "h1b", "h1c"]`), and each family is adjusted on its own at alpha: a member's level, adjusted p-value and decision are its own family's, its minimum detectable effect is judged at alpha over its own family's size, and an equivalence claim is adjusted with the other equivalence claims of its own Holm family only (`analysis.equivalence_groups`), as `equivalence_feasibility` judges it. A family may hold one contrast, a stand-alone confirmatory test. Both forms at once, the table beside `exploratory_only`, an empty table or family, a blank name, an unknown or repeated contrast and a contrast in two families are refused, naming `[holm_families]` and the family. `Preregistration` gains `holm_families`, `families()` and `family_of()`, with `holm_family` the union of the families; the result gains `holm_families`, each a `HolmFamily` with its members, its `HolmStep`s and its equivalence decisions. `analysis.json` adds `holm_families` and the report a table and the Holm steps per family, the exploratory contrasts outside every family and each member's family on the terminal, only for named families; a study with one flat `holm_family` keeps its outputs byte for byte. The power table's corrected row is at alpha over the largest family's size.
- Declared equivalence families (S18). A `[equivalence_families]` table in `preregistration.toml`, in place of the flat `equivalence_family`, names families of equivalence claims (`h1 = ["h1a", "h1b", ...]`), and each is Holm-adjusted over its own claims' TOST p-values at alpha, whatever Holm families their significance tests are in: twelve claims registered as one family across four Holm families of three face alpha / 12 first, where the flat list, split by Holm family since S17, faced alpha / 3. `equivalence_groups` is the one switch, so the decisions and `equivalence_feasibility`'s levels follow it. Each member must be in a Holm family, with a margin and an estimator that registers a TOST p-value, and a claim is in one equivalence family at most; the table beside `equivalence_family` (naming both) or `exploratory_only`, an empty table or family, a blank name, an unknown or repeated contrast, a member in two families or outside every Holm family are refused, naming `[equivalence_families]` and the family. `Preregistration` gains `equivalence_families`, with `equivalence_family` the union of the families; the result gains `equivalence_families`, each an `EquivalenceFamily` with its members and its `HolmStep`s over the TOST p-values, and no `HolmFamily` then holds an equivalence decision. `analysis.json` adds `equivalence_families`, and the report a table naming each claim's equivalence family (and Holm family), each family's TOST steps and each claim's equivalence family on the terminal, only when the table is declared; a study without it keeps its outputs byte for byte (`tests/data/analysis-equivalence-*.json` and `report-equivalence-*.md`, minted before the table existed, pin both existing layouts).
- A preregistration that makes no confirmatory claim, such as a pilot's, says so with `exploratory_only = true` and no `holm_family`. Every contrast is then exploratory, `analysis.json` says `"exploratory_only": true` and holds no Holm decision, the report shows every contrast in one exploratory table with no family table, and its summary and the terminal say that no confirmatory claim is made. An empty `holm_family` is still refused, now naming `exploratory_only`; a family beside the flag is refused too. The power study gives power at alpha only when there is no family.
- Price tiers by prompt length in `models.toml`: two or more `[[models.<key>.price.tiers]]` tables in place of the flat prices, each with its input, output and cache prices, and every tier but the last with `up_to_prompt_tokens`, the longest prompt it takes. A request, its reply included, takes the prices of the tier its prompt fell in. The store keeps each turn's usage (`attempts.turn_usage`, and the marker's `turn_usage`), and the cost measure prices a tiered model request by request from it, so a run's cost is what its record gives. A model without tiers is priced from its totals as before, with an unchanged entry and campaign fingerprint.
- The budget reserves for each run the most its caps let it cost, which is tighter than before for a backend that caps its replies and now covers the request a run can make past its token cap. The cap counts each reply's input and output, and the loop checks it before each request, so a run can pass it by one request, whose prompt the context window bounds. For a model held to its reply maximum (below), the requests within the cap are reserved at most the rounds times the reply maximum as output and the rest of the cap as input, at the dearest rates of any tier, and the request past the cap the context window at the input rate and one reply at the output rate. For any other model, the stub and a study's own backend among them, the whole cap at the dearest rate, as before, and the whole window at the dearest rate; a stub's scripted usage beyond the window is outside the guarantee. An attempt whose usage is unknown is charged the same bound, and a read-only snapshot of a store older than `turn_usage` is costed without it. With a budget, `run` refuses to start or resume with a planned model that is not free and declares no `context_tokens`, naming `models.toml` and the key; checking, analysing, reporting and a package's check of a finished study do not need it.
- The reply maximum bounds a run's reservation only where the campaign has shown the endpoint holds replies to it (C27): the core's own factory builds the model's OpenAI-compatible or Anthropic adapter, and with a budget, at each start, admission sends a request with a `max_tokens` of 16 that asks for a long reply, which must come back cut at that limit. Any other model, one a plugin's factory builds whatever its `backend` included, is reserved by its context window, and a model the probe does not show is admitted with a `reply_cap_not_shown` event. A reply that later reports more than the maximum is a `reply_over_maximum` event and takes the model off it for the rest of the campaign: its runs are reserved by the window from then on, those in flight too, and a later request of a run reserved by the maximum goes out only while the campaign is within its ceilings. Which bound a run was reserved is the decision of the assessment that admitted it, carried into its spend guard and recorded on its attempt's row (`attempts.reserved_capped`), so an attempt whose usage is unknown is charged that bound at every later start, before admission has run too, unless its model was taken off the maximum since; and every resend is cleared again right before it goes out, after the retrier's wait: a top-up reserved by the maximum before the wait reserves the rest of its bound by the window if the model was taken off it meanwhile, or the resend is not sent.
- Top-ups for the sends a run's token cap cannot account for (C26): a resend after a connection error or a 5xx, and every request after a reply that left its input or output count out. Each reserves one request's bound against the ceilings before it goes out, under the dispatcher's locks, and is charged at once and never refunded; the last failed send of a request is charged too. A top-up that does not fit is not sent, and the attempt ends as a budget hold: infrastructure of the new core kind `budget_hold`, set aside, never a treatment, not charged an attempt, and counted per condition and model in the export's new `budget_holds` and in a table of the report, `Budget holds by condition and model`, with a row of zeros for each pair the plan runs, so the analysis sees whether they cluster by model. A hold is counted by its attempt's own kind, so an outage that overlapped it, which `retries_by_kind` counts it under, does not hide it. A reply without usage is a `usage_unreported` event. Admission's probes are paid from the budget the same way, at every start: each send of the one-token completion and of the probe of the reply maximum, retries included, reserves one request's bound by the window before it goes out and is charged in the store (`probe_spend_usd`), an answered one settled to what its reply's counts cost; a probe the budget cannot cover is not sent and its model is not admitted, with a reason saying the budget is spent, and a start takes a release before its probes ask for room. The loop's counting and `token_cap` are unchanged; without a budget nothing is topped up.
- Admission refuses a model whose one-token completion does not report its input and output tokens (B07), budget or none: the token budget is held from those counts, so the cap would not bind that model alone.

- A helper places a declared side file beside its record, safely (#248, R15): `expcore.replay.side_file_placement.stage_side_file(sandbox, request, study.side_files, name)` collects it from the sandbox's `/work` through `sandbox.collect(dest, outputs=[...])`, so the copy keeps O19's guarantees, writes it into `request.run_dir` under the dot-name `.<pattern>.tmp-<attempt>`, and returns a `StagedSideFile` whose `sha256` goes in the record's `hash_field`; `commit()` renames it to its declared name once the record on disk names that hash, and refuses before. It refuses a name the declaration does not hold and a file over `MAX_SIDE_FILE_BYTES`, and returns `None` when the run made no file. The order, the temporary file then the record then the rename, makes a kill recoverable: `check` (not `merge`) first renames a temporary file whose hash the one record beside it names and removes one no record names, so it never refuses a state the study did not cause, and the pairing still verifies every file in place, so it never accepts a wrong file; it removes a temporary file no record names only while it holds the campaign's lock, since an attempt that is staging has no record yet, and a rename that a record names is finished either way; `commit` overwrites no file. The rule is in `docs/interfaces.md` ("Placing a side file beside its record") and the study guide.
- A study declares the side files of its records in `study.toml`: each `[[study.side_files]]` table names a file a run writes beside its record (`pattern`, one literal file name) and the record field that holds its SHA-256 (`hash_field`, a dotted path), validated at load with errors naming the file and the key. `check` hashes every file of a declared name in the batch it parses into its inputs, never replays one, and pairs each with the one record in its directory: a record naming a missing side file, a side file changed or swapped, one in a directory with no record, one beside a record that names no hash for it, and a hash that is not 64 lower-case hex digits each fail the check at stage `side_file`. The check records the declaration (`side_files`); `analyze`, `report`, `regenerate` and a package's `make check` refuse a check made under another, refuse a side file changed, removed or added since, and pair each side file with its record again. A package includes every side file and `PACKAGE.json` records the declaration. The declaration is in `study.toml`, so the campaign fingerprint refuses a resume under another. A study that declares none is checked as before, and its check file and `PACKAGE.json` hold no `side_files`. `verify_batch`, `require_current_check` and `study.verified.load_verified` gain a `side_files` argument.
- A read-only input for the sandbox (#130, O20): `SandboxPlatform.launch(..., inputs=[ReadOnlyInput(source, target)])`, which the executor passes from the run's condition or its own, delivers files a study supplies, such as scripts the agent may run but not change. Each input is copied into the run's directory, hashed while it is copied, and mounted read-only in the agent's container from that copy at `target`. The start proves from inside the agent that every change to it fails with EROFS while the agent can read it and run its executable files, and refuses an input that changed between its hash and its mount. The sandbox metadata records each under `read_only_inputs`, by its path in the agent, its SHA-256, its files and its bytes, never the host's path, and `inputs.InputsRecord`, which `launch` fills (`inputs_record`), puts the same in the run's record. The plugin declares its inputs with `inputs.declare`, checked against every path the sandbox reserves and hashed, puts them in the campaign fingerprint with `inputs.fingerprint`, so a resume with changed inputs is refused, and builds the platform with them (`SandboxPlatform(..., read_only_inputs=...)`): `launch` refuses any other input, and every start refuses one whose content left its declared hash. The run's copy is root's and read-only on the host too, and `collect` hashes it again. A path that is, holds or lies under another mount of the agent, and a source holding a link, a special file or no file, are refused before anything exists. `SandboxSpec` gains `inputs` and `containers.agent_run_args` an `inputs` keyword, both empty by default; `ReadOnlyInput` carries the `sha256` it was declared with.
- A scorer reads a side file's content as the bytes the loader verified, never by path (#144). A scorer, or a plain function used as one, that declares a `side_files` parameter is passed the record's side files as a read-only mapping from declared pattern to bytes; one that takes only the record is called as before, and the example's outputs keep their bytes. `analyze`, `report`, `regenerate` and a package's `make check` hand over the bytes `load_verified` read once and paired against the replay check, before the plugin is loaded, so a side file that no longer matches is refused before any scorer runs; `rescore_files` takes the study's declaration (`side_files`) and pairs the side file beside the record of an attempt directory the same way. The same batch gives the same scores through each, byte for byte. A record with no side file gives an empty mapping, and a caller that hands over none refuses such a scorer. A side file holds at most 64 MiB (`replay.side_files.MAX_SIDE_FILE_BYTES`): `check` fails a larger one at stage `side_file`, and the loader refuses one that grew past it, reading no more than one byte beyond. Neither `check` nor the comparison of a batch with its check reads a side file whole: by its declaration it is no record, so neither looks into it for one, and every hashed-only input is hashed in chunks. Any other hashed-only file is read whole, to tell whether it holds a record, only up to 64 MiB (`replay.check.RECORD_PROBE_BYTES`); a larger one is looked at by a 4 KiB prefix and refused when that prefix opens a JSON object. `rescore` gains a `side_files` argument, and `VerifiedBatch` gains `side_files` and `side_files_for`.
- The mount sidecar can reach a federation on that federation's own Docker network. `[sandbox.federation]` in `platform.toml` names the network, which the oneclient sidecar joins instead of Docker's default bridge, and the local provider's name on it, keyed by `[support] local`, so a changed local provider is refused at load and never mounted through another (O03). A federation the host reaches at addresses a container cannot use, such as one whose ports are published on the host's loopback, needs it. The agent never joins that network. A mount run whose network is not on the host is refused before anything exists, and the sandbox never changes or removes the network. A REST path's data gate reads the table too (#203, the next entry). The study guide's platform section shows the whole path from the agent to the federation. A gated live test, `tests/live/test_mount_path.py`, runs one sandboxed attempt on the mount path against the Compose federation; it has not run yet.
- The REST data path works on a federation reachable only on its own Docker network, such as the Compose federation (#203, O30). With `[sandbox.federation]` the data gate starts on that network, is then connected to the sandbox's internal one, and serves and dials the zone and the local provider by their names there on port 443, the names the federation's certificates hold, checked as `[tls]` says; the agent resolves those names to the gate and never joins the network. The table's new `zone` key gives the zone's name there. Without the table the gate is on Docker's default bridge and dials the URLs' hosts, as before. The gate's TLS keys are given to root, owner-only, before it starts, as its token file is: the gate, root with every capability dropped but `NET_BIND_SERVICE`, could not read keys the driver owned, so every start of the gate container exited before it was ready. [`docs/interfaces.md`](docs/interfaces.md#the-platform-layers-sandbox) says what the image `[sandbox.images.gate]` names must hold: `python` on its path, at least 3.12, its standard library with `ssl`, nothing else. A gated live test, `tests/live/test_rest_path.py`, reads a provisioned file through the gate on the Compose federation, with the federation's own platform file, and checks its hash; it has not run yet.
- The private plain list of study tokens accepts a `whole: <token>` line, which makes that token match only as an entire identifier, literal or line whatever its length, for an ordinary word that would otherwise be found inside unrelated names; the hash file renders it as `whole <scope> <sha256>`.
- The P07 check is a command, `python -m expcore.provenance.history --map MAP --source-repos "NAME=PATH;..."`: each module under `src/` that names a source must, as the commit that started its history holds it, reproduce from that source's blob under the private map. That commit is found without git's copy detection, and a merge counts when it holds the module as no parent does and its first parent lacks it, so a hand-edited copy of an imported module, or one a merge adds back edited, is checked at its own adding commit; a rename is looked for against each parent of a merge, so a module a side branch imported and the merge renamed is traced to that import. The command names a module, never a source repository, and exits 2 when there is no module to check. Section 9 of the study guide documents the sanitising import, its map, the provenance header and this check.
- A turn's record keeps the numeric usage fields a provider reports beyond input and output tokens (an endpoint's own credit count, cache reads and writes, reasoning tokens), under their own names, in `transcript[i].usage.extra`, with `extra_cut` for the fields the cap left out (`ModelTurn.usage_extra` and `usage_extra_cut`; `expcore.models.usage.extra_usage` for a backend of a study's own). They are recorded only and change no budget arithmetic. A field a reply omits is absent, never 0; only integers of 64 bits and finite floats under plain names are kept, at most 32 per turn; an object nested one level down is read as `parent.child`. This is record schema version 3, which adds those two fields to a turn's usage, both optional (a turn whose backend reported none has neither, never a null or a zero), keeps version 2's first-turn rule, and is the first version under the reply-count rule, so a billed reply that became no turn leaves its totals' usage incomplete: every new record is version 3, and records of versions 1 and 2 stay valid, write back to their own bytes and replay without the fields. Until v0.1.0 is tagged every extension of version 3 is strictly additive: `SchemaVersion` gains `optional`, fields a version may leave absent, and `tests/data/record-v3/` holds one fixture per merge point, which `tests/records/test_v3_fixtures.py` validates and replays byte for byte under the current code, beside structural checks over the schema itself: every field version 3 adds is optional, and the fields version 3 had at its first merge point, frozen in `tests/data/record-v3-fields.json`, keep their types, their order and whether they are required, while every later field is optional. An outcome's `defect` and the totals' `malformed_events` are sections of the schema (`defect`, `malformed_event`), versioned like the others, so a required key added to either is caught though the fixtures hold neither. See [`docs/interfaces.md`](docs/interfaces.md), "Usage fields beyond the counts" and "The run record's schema".
- An instrument can read the state at the agent's first turn and can refuse the attempt (#135, C31), with two optional methods that an instrument without them never needs. `at_first_turn(moment)` is called by `first_turn_instruments(instruments, request.timer)`, which the executor calls just before `record_run`, so before the loop starts the agent's clock and before the first request: it takes one reading of the run's clock, logged in the record's clock as `instrument_first_turn` and handed to each hook as `moment["clock_s"]`, and takes none when no instrument has the hook. `check()` is called by `finish_instruments` after every snapshot is in, and also when an instrument's `finish` failed. Either refuses by raising `InfraError` of a kind no condition can declare as its treatment. At the first turn that reaches the executor's caller as infrastructure of that kind (`FirstTurnRefusal`), naming the instrument, before any request is sent, and it costs no tokens unless the backend's `requests_sent` counted one. After the run it is recorded in `RunRecord.instrument_refusal`, `{namespace, infra_kind, detail}`, an optional field of `expcore-record/3` written only when there is one, and the scheduler sets the attempt aside in `AttemptRunner._judge`, after the health check and before the identity check: a scored attempt, a scored treatment attempt included, becomes infrastructure of that kind with an `instrument_refused` event, is charged and runs again, and one already set aside keeps its outcome. A scored treatment attempt the health gate reported an outage for is refused too and never scored; it is discarded as the outage's, not charged, and runs again. A kind a condition may declare, or any other exception, is a defect of the instrument (`InstrumentRefusalError`). The replay serves the reading and the refusal back as recorded and the fixture `tests/data/record-v3/04-instrument-refusal.json` pins them; the check counts a refusal's kind among the kinds a record names, so a platform kind that only a refusal carries still makes the check import that platform; a study whose instruments have neither method takes no reading and writes no field, and the example study's records are unchanged.
- A model served by several deployments can be one model (B18): its entry in `models.toml` declares `accepted_fingerprints`, the serving fingerprints of the deployments the study decided are the same model, and the set goes into the campaign fingerprint, so a resume under another set is refused. A reply served under a fingerprint of the set is accepted; one under any other still sets its run aside as a changed identity, and the served model id is checked as before. The admission probe must be served by the model under a fingerprint of the set. A declared fingerprint is confirmed by the probe or by the first run whose reply shows it, and one never shown is reported as unconfirmed. Each attempt records the turns each fingerprint served (the store's `deployments` column), and the export's `deployment_shares` and the report's "Deployments" section give, per model, each fingerprint's count and share of the analysed turns and runs and the runs served by more than one. A model without the key keeps its checks, its records, its fingerprint digest and its export; the `anthropic` backend records no fingerprint and cannot take the key.
- Several spaces in one platform configuration: `[[spaces]]` in `platform.toml`, in place of `[space]` and `[support]`, each entry with the label code names it by, its supporting providers, its own data provider (`holder`, required, one of them) and the token planes whose caveats reach it (`tokens`). A message names a space by its position, `spaces[i]`. Provisioning, placement, eviction, the probes and the reset are bound to one space by its label, and every mutation a provisioner sends is bound to its space: the gate refuses a target in another space even under a shared prefix. A range-map row names its space. Each token plane's caveats and privilege checks cover every space it is scoped to, so the tool server and the data gate reach exactly the declared set. The health check's token is the data path's, and it compares each space the data path reaches with its own support set. The day-0 checks run in each space they are about: the listing and the placement round trip in every declared space, read-only enforcement in each space the data path reaches, and the tokens as a run mints them; with several spaces a check's verdict is the worst over its spaces (failed before unknown before passed), each named by its position, and the report withholds every space's name and id. The one-space form enters the fingerprint by its support, name and prefixes (`support`); the `[[spaces]]` form enters it by hash, with one entry or several, so a resume under another set is refused. The data provider must support its space in either form.
- A complete reset of a space between runs, `reset.SpaceReset`, in five stages, each verified on every provider of the space and timed. It finds and removes every QoS requirement, cancels every waiting or ongoing transfer, evicts in bulk the files the caller names, checks that no provider other than the owner holds more of the space than the caller allows (by physical size, read only while no transfer runs), and records the ended-transfer history. Before any stage it refuses a plan that names the data provider or a provider outside the space's support set, and verifies the space's live name. A stage that cannot verify what it did fails the reset (`ResetFailed`), and the next run must not start. Bulk eviction (`eviction.py`) moves a directory off a provider in one transfer, or a set of files in parallel under `[placement] concurrency` (16 by default), and verifies them together on the block maps once no transfer runs; what still holds bytes is scheduled again, by the rule `Provisioner.evict` follows on 25.0. Every absence the reset reads, a removed requirement's included, is `answered_absent`, and a cancel the gate refused unsent is never taken for an ended job. Range placement and the probes list each directory once per operation. The mutation gate resolves a transfer's cancel and a QoS requirement's removal through their records. The live tests of the reset's calls need `EXPCORE_LIVE_MUTATE=1`, and the one that resets a whole space also needs `EXPCORE_LIVE_RESET=1`; they have not been run.
- A public testing module for the Onedata platform, `expcore_onedata.testing` (installed with the `[onedata]` extra): the fakes the core's own tests use, so a study tests its instruments, probes and scorers without a live federation. It holds `MockOnedata` (the provider REST surface over a local socket), `FakeFederation` (an in-process Onezone), `FakeData` (Onezone plus every provider's data API: files, per-provider block maps that a range read, a replication and an eviction change, per-reader views with a visibility delay counted on the fake's own clock or in reads of a file, transfers and their delays, and the data API's error shapes: Onedata 25.0's, as measured on the Compose federation, or the older 404 shape for a study that targets an older platform, selected with `error_shape`, each of whose answers a test can still bend on its own), `Ticker` (a clock that moves only when something sleeps), the default `platform.toml` with the federation that matches it (`write_platform`, `write_with_placement`, `make_federation`) and two stand-in MCP servers, run with `python -m`. Each fake's docstring says what it models and what it does not, and [`docs/interfaces.md`](docs/interfaces.md) lists the names, which change only with an entry here. It imports the core, the platform layer and the standard library, never pytest or a test tree. The core's own tests import the fakes from it, so they cannot drift from the code they stand in for; only test-only glue stays in `tests/support/`. No runtime path imports it (the core, the other modules of the platform layer, the CLI and the shipped example), which `tests/test_import_boundary.py` checks from the source, with relative and literal dynamic imports, and by importing every runtime module in a fresh interpreter. The wheel carries it. A transfer that started before a run's window is modelled as the core reports it, carried over and not counted, which is where the first study's own stand-in differed (O08). The study guide has a section, "Testing a study's instruments and scorers", with code that runs as part of the tests.
- The day-0 check `provider_outage` (#108, O28): what reads and the knowledge plane do while a provider is down. For each space, with the federation's lock held exclusively, it takes the space's data provider down through the fault injector (`faults.FaultInjector`: journalled before the stop, recovered when left open, restored only with a changed identity and once the provider serves, a stop of unknown outcome waited out) and records, through the agent's local provider, what a read of a file replicated at a mirror, a read of a file held by the data provider alone and a listing of their directory answer: over REST, and through the mount of a real sandbox in mount mode, its reads run in the agent's container with the errno, the seconds and whether each blocked. It calls the agent's own tools, `get_file_distribution`, `list_space_providers`, `schedule_file_replication` and `get_transfer`, through MCP on the condition's tool server, started on a leased slot's tool-server user and token as a run starts it (the sandbox's and the tool server's tokens minted for the longest the scenario can need them, and a step they no longer cover counted as the check's own failure, never the provider's), recording each call's outcome, timeout and attempts and its wording as a digest with its error terms. Everything is measured with all providers up first, and a baseline that does not serve stops nothing. The check fails unless the provider is shown back (the injection cleared, the identity changed, REST answering again, the reads returning what was written) and the scratch is shown gone, and a space whose injector could not settle an injection an earlier run left open fails the check and halts the spaces after it (that recovery runs before anything is prepared, #178); a declared mount not read, or a tool not called, makes it unknown. Each answer has an outcome (`served`, `partial`, `differs`, `refused`, `no answer`, `blocked`) and its seconds against a bound (`--outage-bound-s`, 60 by default). It stops nothing without `EXPCORE_LIVE_MUTATE=1`, `EXPCORE_LIVE_FAULTS=1` and `sanctioned = true` in `[faults]`, starts the sandbox and the tool server only with `EXPCORE_LIVE_DOCKER=1`, needs three providers in a space's support set (its data provider, a mirror, and the local provider, which differs from the data provider), and names providers, spaces and files by position; it keeps only structured fields, and a tool's answer or wording, which can name what it does not know, only as a digest of it with every known name and id-like token replaced, its length and its error terms. `Context` gains `allow_faults`, `fault_settings`, `fault_driver`, `outage_bound_s`, `agent_host`, `agent_options`, `tool_condition` and `outage_ceiling_s`, and the CLI `--tool-condition`. It has not run against a live federation. [`docs/interfaces.md`](docs/interfaces.md) and the federation's README say what the live run measures.
- A pending-delete knob for `FakeData`, so a study can test that its code waits before it uses a name again: `delete_delay_s` (also an argument of `make_federation`), off by default, so a test that does not set it behaves as before, except that a transfer for a deleted file's id is now accepted (below). With a delay, a DELETE is answered 204 and lands that many seconds of the fake's `clock` later (`land_deletes()` lands every one at once). While it is under way, the provider the DELETE went to answers the entry, and what is under a directory, as a deleted one at once (400 `posix` `enoent`), with its `parent_id` still reading when asked alone, and the other providers keep showing it until it lands, as the 12 Nov live check recorded (`deploy/compose-federation/day0/2026-11-12-federation-facts-pending-deletes.md`). A name made again meanwhile is accepted and **loses nothing**: that check re-created a directory within 0.1 s of its delete four times and lost none of 21 files, so the knob's first version, which took a re-created directory's files when the old delete landed, was wrong. **The default changed:** that loss is now the opt-in `lose_recreated=True` (also an argument of `make_federation`), not reproduced live and kept for testing defensive waits, so a study that set `delete_delay_s` to see a too-short wait lose files must also set `lose_recreated=True`. An ingest wait written against the loss is conservative with no loss, not wrong. A transfer scheduled through the deleting provider for a file it answers as deleted is accepted and ends at once with 0 files, as recorded for a replication (the fake refused it with 400 before, and at delay 0 too: that one answer changes for a test that sets no delay), and the same for an eviction and a migration, a file whose delete has landed, a directory's id and a job through another provider after the delete landed there is an extrapolation. Also assumptions, marked as such in the docstring and in `docs/interfaces.md`: the other providers refusing the entry's name (409), a second DELETE through them changing nothing, the loss with `lose_recreated` touching files at any depth and no directory, and a provider that holds a new entry back answering its id as an entry it does not show yet. A provider that still shows the old entry of a name made again shows only that entry until the delete lands, and then only the new one, as the check recorded (the first version listed both beside each other, so a study that resolved a path through a mirror without waiting got the new entry, which live it did not); the provider the DELETE went to shows the new one at once. A new file's lag on a mirror (size 0, or only some of its directory's files) is not modelled. A delay needs a `clock` that moves: with the fake's default clock, which never does, the first DELETE that would pend raises `ValueError` naming `delete_delay_s` and how to bind a clock, instead of a delete that never lands and a test of a reused name that passes for no reason. Provider calls are answered one at a time under the fake's lock, so the core's bulk reads may run at once while a due delete lands. `fake_data.DELETE_FACTS` names where the recorded answers are.
- Large files in and out of the platform without holding them (O25). `Provisioner.upload(path, source, *, content_type="application/octet-stream")` sends a local file a chunk (`[placement] chunk_bytes`, 16 MiB by default) at a time, each chunk a write at its offset through the mutation gate, and returns an `Upload` (`expcore_onedata.streamed`): the file id, the size and SHA-256, the bytes sent and what it did. A target that already holds the same size and SHA-256 is `skipped` with nothing sent, whoever created it; one this provisioner created that holds a strict prefix is `resumed` from where it ends, by offset writes; any other it created that is no longer than the local file is `restarted`, written again from its first byte with every write at its offset, and one longer than it is refused with nothing written (O44: no write that truncates is sent); a differing file it did not create is refused, as before. `stop=N` makes the local file's first N bytes the content: the target ends as that prefix, verified by its hash, and a later upload with a larger stop, or none, resumes from it. Success is reported only once the size and the SHA-256 read back equal those of the bytes sent. `Provisioner.read_file(file_id, *, provider=None, dest=None)` and `OnezoneClient.read_content` read a file back by ranges of `chunk_bytes`, each gated as a range read and streamed, hashing as the bytes arrive, to a local path (written as `<dest>.part` and moved into place only when whole) or only to the hash. Every answer must be a 206 of exactly the range asked for, of a file of the size it was read at, with exactly its bytes; the whole read has a deadline (`[placement] transfer_timeout_s` unless given), and no wait for the platform lasts longer than `request_timeout_s` or what remains of it.
- A bounded, logged retry of idempotent plumbing reads (#185, O29): `OnezoneClient.get` takes `retry`, a `ReadRetry` (`expcore_onedata.readretry`: three attempts, pauses of 2 s and 5 s, an injectable clock and sleep, and a count) or `ONE_ATTEMPT`, the default, and a read is tried again only when it timed out (an `httpx` read, connect or pool timeout), each retry on a connection of its own and within the call's `deadline_s`; a PUT, POST, PATCH or DELETE, an error answer, a connection error and a streamed read are never retried. The reads of the mutation gate, which find the target's space before every write and range read, are plumbing too: `mutate`, `read_range` and `read_content` take `gate_retry`, for those reads alone, and `space_name`, `file_space_id` and `file_space_name` take `retry`; the gate stays closed (each attempt that is answered decides, and after three timeouts nothing is sent), and each read of a streamed range's gate is given what remains of the deadline when it begins, so a gate of several reads cannot spend a multiple of it. The polls whose failure ends an operation (`replicate`, `evict`, the bulk eviction and the reset's eviction stage) and `place_ranges`'s block-map reads (`Provisioner.blocks(retry=)`) retry as well. Every call that reaches a GET names its choice, held by a table test that finds any get with a keyword or a path, the client's gated and space reads on any receiver, the waits by name, a method got by getattr, the client's own calls and the provisioner's and the layer's retrying helpers, and fails on a call it cannot classify: the provisioner's listings and metadata reads, `read_file`'s size read, the visibility wait, the gate and the polls, the reset's reads, the ingest's attribute reads and the root a day-0 mutating check writes under retry; the health gate, the placement probes and block maps, the transfer log and day-0's reading checks keep one attempt, so a stall stays a recorded stall. Each timed-out attempt is logged with its seconds, its connection (new, reused with the requests it had served, or unknown) and the next attempt's outcome, and each retry is counted: `Provisioner.reads_retried`, `reads_retried` in the reset's record and in the ingest's snapshot.
- Upload chunks of 16 MiB, each full chunk on a new connection (#186, O25): `[placement] chunk_bytes` defaults to 16 MiB (16777216; it was 64 MiB), and the new `[placement] fresh_connection_bytes`, 16 MiB by default, is the size from which a content write goes on a connection of its own. `OnezoneClient.mutate` takes `fresh`, which a client that built its own HTTP client honours by sending that one request through a one-shot client built as the pool's is (the same `[tls]`), closed when the answer is in, never on an idle connection of the pool, and `Provisioner` passes it for every body of at least `fresh_connection_bytes`, so with the defaults every full chunk of an upload is on a new connection and the remainder keeps the pool. A client given an HTTP client by its caller sends through that one. Reads keep the connection pool, but `chunk_bytes` also sizes the ranges of a whole-file read (`read_file`, `read_content`, range placement), so a read now takes 16 MiB ranges: four times as many ranges, and gate reads, per file as at 64 MiB. Why: on the 25.0 development build the Compose federation runs, a 200 MiB upload stalled at chunks of 64 and 32 MiB on reused keep-alive connections (3 of 289 reused PUTs, none of 336 on new ones; the body started and never finished, the provider closed at about 300 s), and 16 MiB and every smaller size passed every run, reused or not (`deploy/compose-federation/day0/2026-11-12-federation-facts-upload-and-stalls.md`). Each write of content is logged (logger `expcore_onedata.client`) with its size, the seconds it took and how its connection began, `new` or `reused` with the requests it had served, so a live run shows which writes were on a reused connection; the line names the provider by its position and holds no path, id or token. A live test at the default chunk size, with no override, uploads 50 MiB, reads it back by its hash and records each PUT's connection.
- **An ingest that lists each directory once, as the space's sole writer (O67).** `Provisioner.upload` and `make_dir` take `listings`, one operation's cache of directory listings, shared by its uploads and filled with each entry they make, so an ingest into one directory is linear in its files rather than quadratic (15,340 files in one directory were about 125,000 listing pages); an entry the cache holds is read again by its id before it decides a skip, a resume or a restart, and every cached directory on the way before it is descended into. `expcore_onedata.bulkingest` passes one: `ingest_hold(config, label=..., timeout_s=...)` holds the federation's lock exclusively for an ingest (`admission.INGEST`; the health gate names it "an ingest"; refused while an injection is unsettled, and on a platform without `[faults]`), and `ingest_files(provisioner, files, root=..., on_file=...)` refuses to start outside that hold, checks each `ManifestFile` source (a path or a tar member's `Slice`) against its size and SHA-256 before it is sent, uploads through one cache, and ends with a fresh listing of `root` compared with the manifest (`check_listing`): a file missing, not a regular file or of another size, a directory on the way that is not one, or any entry the manifest does not hold fails it (`IngestMismatch`), naming each path. It returns an `IngestReport` (per file: action, bytes sent, the check's and the upload's seconds). A writer that takes no admission is outside the hold; the final listing shows its effect. No fingerprint, hash or record changes.
- **An upload straight from a member of a tar (O25).** `Provisioner.upload` and `Provisioner.write_range` take a `Slice` (`expcore_onedata.streamed`) as their source, as well as a local path: `size` bytes of a local file from its byte `offset`, read as a file of its own, whose offsets are its own and of which no read reaches outside the range (`streamed.chunks` takes a slice too and ends it at its last byte whatever the `stop`). `streamed.tar_members(archive)` gives every member of an uncompressed tar (ustar, pax or GNU) as the slice of the archive that holds its bytes, by name and in the archive's order, reading only the headers, and refuses the archive whole (`ArchiveRefused`, a `ValueError` naming why and the member) when it is compressed (gzip, bzip2, xz, zstd, lz4, lzip, compress) or no tar, or when a member is sparse, a link, anything else that is not a regular file, a name held twice, a name that is no plain path relative to a space's root (`rangemap.safe_path`), a pax size that is no plain count, or bytes reaching past the archive's end; and when any byte up to where the reading stopped belongs to no member returned (or a global pax header), or the archive does not end in its end-of-archive blocks there, since `tarfile` ends its reading quietly at a damaged later header or after a member it failed inside, which would drop members without a word. So a dataset kept as one archive per space is uploaded member by member and never unpacked: creation, skip, resume, restart, `stop`, the read-back by hash and the change log are as for a local file, a resume compares the target with the member's own first bytes, and a slice whose file does not hold its whole range is refused before anything is asked. No fingerprint, hash or record changes. Why: on an HPC cluster's scratch filesystem a dataset staged as one archive per space costs one inode per space against the account's inode quota, where its loose files would cost one each.
- Progressive ingest (#107, O27): `expcore_onedata.ingest.ProgressiveIngest`, an instrument that makes part of a dataset arrive during a run, as if its owner were still ingesting it, so a study can test whether an agent notices incomplete data before computing. A study's plan (`expcore_onedata.ingestplan.load_ingest_plan`, one TOML file per condition) names each file by a label, its path in the space, a staged source the study keeps, when it arrives, whether it is written whole or in parts over a duration, and its baseline: "complete" for a file every other condition reads (removed before the run, written back after) or "absent" for new data. `fingerprint(plans)` puts each plan's hash in the campaign fingerprint. Each part is written through the provisioner bound to the study's space and counts only when its hash, the data provider's block map and every provider that must see it agree; every step is a clock-labelled reading on the loop's clock, and every file id removed, created or restored is recorded with its label, the old and the new id, in the snapshot and a journal kept across runs. The start restores the baseline first, recovering a crashed run, and removes only files the ingest made or whose bytes are their staged source's, the file's staged source checked first in every removal, the ingest's own files included (a damaged copy leaves the file in place and fails that file's restore, which the post-run check refuses as infrastructure), each confirmed absent on every provider before its name is written again. Each file is restored on its own, so one that fails leaves no other missing, and every file id is recorded as soon as it exists, a failed upload's included. A failed start, part or restore is `ingest_failure`, a platform kind no condition may declare: the instrument's post-run check (#135, C31) refuses the attempt, which is set aside as charged infrastructure and run again whatever the executor does (`raise_if_failed` is the same check, as a second guard); a file not yet arrived when the run ends is the manipulation. The instrument claims its provisioner's change log (`Provisioner.claim_log`), refusing one another user wrote, and `teardown` refuses a claimed log. No record field changed: the snapshot is the instrument's, under `RunRecord.instruments["progressive_ingest"]`.
- The progressive ingest's preallocated mode (#107, O44): with `[ingest] mode = "preallocated"` and a `prefix` in bytes per file, each planned file, part of the baseline, exists at its final size from the start with only its prefix written at the data provider, so the namespace looks as in the baseline while the rest is still arriving. The start removes the complete file (its old id confirmed absent on every provider), makes it again with `Provisioner.preallocate(path, size)`, which extends it to its final size by an empty write before the prefix is written at offset 0 with `Provisioner.write_range`, and confirms it. The parts fall due from the agent's first model turn: the instrument has the first-turn hook (`at_first_turn`, C31) in this mode alone, and a run whose first turn never came is refused by the post-run check. Each state is confirmed as an arrival is, by the hash read back (`read_file(stop=)`), every provider that must see the file and, in this mode, the block maps showing exactly the written ranges at the final size and nothing beyond them; a part that would not start where the written ranges end, or meets a byte beyond them, fails closed with nothing written. The reset completes each file in place from its staged source and never removes it, verified by the hash and the data provider's block map. Before the start's restore or its first removal every staged copy must have the plan's size and SHA-256. A file may declare `replicas`, the providers that hold it whole in the baseline (a mirror): before the first removal each must hold it whole by its own block map and no undeclared provider but the data provider may hold a byte of it, or the start is refused with nothing removed, and the reset replicates the completed file to each (`Provisioner.replicate_file`), verified by that provider's block map. The journal carries baseline marks (`expcore_onedata.ingesthealth`): `open` before the start changes anything, `unhealthy` when a start, a reset or a recovery fails after that, `whole` once a restore verifies every file. A start is held back with nothing sent unless the last mark is `whole` (`ingest_held`, which costs no attempt, O45); the start's own recovery after a failure marks it `whole` when it verifies every file, and otherwise `ProgressiveIngest.recover()`, on an instrument that has not started, brings it back. `PlatformHealth(..., ingest_journals=...)` fails, before it asks the federation anything, while a baseline is `unhealthy` or `open` with no live attempt owning it (O45), so a crash at any point leaves the federation unhealthy until the recovery ends with the complete file. The snapshot adds `first_turn`, `start.files`, the state `prefix_only`, `id_map` (each id a planned file had, mapped to the one the ingest gave it in its place, for a study's id map keyed by file id) and `start_s` (the start's seconds, before the first turn), and its `ok` is now what `check()` passes in either mode (a start that did not set the start state, or a schedule that never began, is not ok); the plan's summary adds `mode` and each file's `prefix`, and an arrival plan keeps its hash. A gated live test (`tests/live/test_ingest_preallocated.py`) is written for the next slot.
- No write of content truncates (O44): `OnezoneClient.mutate` refuses a PUT to a file's content without a whole `offset` of 0 or more (`client.TruncatingWrite`) before anything is read or sent, since such a write truncates the file to 0 first (op-worker f118ed9). The upload's restart writes from offset 0 at explicit offsets and refuses a longer target, the link check rewrites its probe at offset 0 and replaces a longer one with the next generation, and the truncating write left `client.UNVERIFIED_ENDPOINTS`, since nothing sends it.
- A hard deadline for every cell of the agent's code (#190, O34). At its cap every process of the cell is killed, from the agent container's cgroup, so a worker in a session of its own goes too; a process still alive after `[sandbox] kill_grace_s` (1 to 60 s, 5 by default), as a read waiting in the kernel on the FUSE mount of a file only a stopped provider holds is, has the mount's FUSE connection aborted from the oneclient sidecar, and when that fails, cannot be done or frees nothing, the sandbox is stopped. Each grace is counted by the clock, and the whole escalation lasts at most `deadline.margin_s` past the cap (two graces and 20 s; when it runs out the escalation stops waiting); the sandbox's stop runs once the outcome is recorded, so its teardown neither delays the run's classification nor counts in the cell's time. Only the init's own `sleep`, identified by its pid and start time when the container started, is spared by the kill. Each step is recorded with its time in `metadata()["cell_deadlines"]`, with the outcome `killed`, `mount_aborted`, `sandbox_stopped` or `survived_kill` (the margin ran out). `launch` and `collect` settle the deadline of a cell still running before they decide, so a cell the agent loop cut at its cap, which is the cell's, still ends the run as `cell_unkillable` rather than the agent's `time_cap`. A clean kill is the agent's own time limit, as before; any other outcome makes the cell `kill_survived` and its attempt `InfraError("cell_unkillable")`, a platform kind no condition may declare, and ends the run: later cells, and the collection of a stopped run, raise that kind. Before, the kill at the cap waited 10 s for a process stuck on the mount, and the cell, the next one and the teardown waited for the read; the outage rerun of 12 Nov measured such a read still running 10 s after its kill. A gated live test (`tests/live/test_cell_deadline.py`) checks it with a provider stopped through the fault injector.
- **Direct I/O for the mount, with a start that proves it (#223, O40).** `[sandbox.mount] io_mode = "direct"` now loads with `[sandbox.mount.storages]`, each storage id the sidecar reads directly mapped to the host directory that holds it, and `direct_check_file`, a file the local provider holds in full (`<space>/<path>` under the mount); both are required with `"direct"` and refused with `"proxy"`. The sidecar's oneclient gets `--force-direct-io`, `--disable-log-buffering` and one `--override <id>:mountPoint:/mnt/expcore-storage/<n>` per storage, and each host directory is bound read-only into the sidecar alone (`--mount ...,readonly`), never into the agent's container; a read-only input from a storage, and a storage under the work root, are refused. The start then has the agent read the check file whole, once the mount says the local provider holds all of it, and reads the sidecar's log: unless it says forced direct and no forced proxy and holds no detection or fallback line, the start is refused (`SandboxError`, "direct I/O is not in effect for the mount"), since a direct read refused with EACCES is silently read again through the provider. For the same reason the collection reads the log again, and a direct run whose log shows a read through the provider since the start, or cannot be read, is set aside (`output_collection`). Each run records `mount.direct` (the number of storages, the check's facts and the lines found at collection, never an id or a path). `extra_args` may no longer carry `--override` or `-r`, in either mode. `deploy/compose-federation/measure_direct_vs_proxy.py` replays the same read sequences on the same files under proxy and under direct, each pass from the same evicted, cold state, and compares what each read places on the local provider, read by read: a study validated under proxy keeps proxy unless that verdict is yes. `interfaces.md` ("A direct mount") says what direct I/O changes for provider-side measurements.
- **A resume says when the platform's settings outside the fingerprint changed (C35).** `[placement]` (the reset's QoS scan, its waits and bounds) is in neither fingerprint, since it changes how the reset works and not what an agent sees, so a campaign resumed after a `[placement]` edit, or after a default moved under it (`qos_scan` became `"listing"` in #258), ran its later resets differently with nothing to say so. `StudyPlugin.reset_settings` is a flat mapping of names to text, numbers, booleans or None that the platform supplies after its defaults, and the core never reads its meaning: the store records it at the first start (the meta key `reset_settings`), and every resume compares. A changed key is named with its old and new value in one warning in the log (`expcore.campaign.reset_settings`), in a `reset_settings_changed` event, and in the report's infrastructure notes (the export's optional `reset_settings`; in a merged batch each entry names its shard), and the resume goes on; an unchanged resume writes and says nothing. A store made before this record has none: the resume records the settings, writes one `reset_settings_unknown_before` event and says once that the earlier settings are unknown. A platform that supplies none changes nothing: no key in the store, the export or the report. `expcore_onedata.rangemap.Placement.reset_settings()` (what `load_placement` returns) gives every `[placement]` field after its default as `placement.<name>` and the range map, which decides which bytes a state places, as `placement.range_map_sha256`: the SHA-256 of `RangeMap.canonical()` (the rows sorted by space, file and provider as compact JSON with sorted keys and merged ranges, so the order of the rows in the file does not change it) and never its rows. The data provider's name is left out. A study passes it as `StudyPlugin(reset_settings=load_placement(...).reset_settings())`. `open_campaign` and `Scheduler` take `reset_settings` too.
- **A study can ask for one full reset without leaving the reset's exclusive section (O51).** `SpaceReset.run(..., full_reason="<why>")`, or `ResetPlan.full_reason` for `SpacesReset`, gives the occupancy and quiet stages the full reads while the caller's section holds its grant as before, so no reader gets in during the reset; leaving the section out, the only way to the full reads until now, also dropped that guard (now every reset holds the lock in any case, O66). The record's `occupancy_reads` says `full`, the core's own `why` (`requested` where it would have given the shortcut) and the study's reason beside it (`full_reason`). The record is published, so the reason is a short code (a lower-case letter, then up to 63 lower-case letters, digits or underscores) holding no name the platform's configuration gives (O12); any other is refused before anything is sent. A reset given no reason records its reads as before.
- **A baseline between the fault injector's admission and its stop (O11).** `FaultInjector.prepared(identity)` splits `start` where its preparation ends: `with injector.prepared(identity) as prepared: <baseline>; prepared.inject()`. The body runs after the admission, the recovery of what an earlier run left open, the drain that recovery owes and the drain record, with the federation held exclusively and nothing of the target touched, so a probe started there (`start_instruments(probes, identity)`) reads the federation admitted, recovered and drained, and still up. `start` is that block with nothing in it but `inject`, unchanged: the same journal lines, steps, driver calls and clock and sleep reads, and its own block re-reads nothing. The exit lets the admission go only when nothing is down, and the block never restores (the caller's `finish` or `clear` does). The injector's lock is not held across the body: while a block is open, `start`, `prepared` and `recover` raise `RuntimeError` at once, even while its `inject` holds the lock, and a `clear` during the body returns at once and makes `inject` take nothing down. `inject` refuses a second call, a call outside its block, and, with nothing touched, a window gone stale (no exclusive admission where it is called, an injection journalled since the recovery, a drain record raised since, or a link epoch not the same `id` and count as at the preparation, read once every link check under way has ended). The baseline spends the run's token life in the documented order, and the least `[tokens] margin_s` counts none (the injector's start falls in its 300 s slack, apart from the start's stop on the HPC backend): a study whose baseline may take longer than the slack leaves declares a larger margin.

### Changed

- `hpcfed.py up` refuses an agent's node without `HPC_CORE_PYTHON` (O63): `down`'s sweep of a node a shard step ran on reaps the sandbox's runs with the core's reaper, so it runs under the core's Python, no longer the nodes' own. Every shard step line also carries `--chdir=/`, `--job` and `--step-name`, which its running mark records.

For readers of the commits before this release:
- **Breaking for every store that recorded the platform's fingerprint: `PlatformConfig.fingerprint()` now holds every declared setting that shapes a run, so platform fingerprints change (C06).** Studies hand the core `config.fingerprint()` rather than the rendered file's bytes, so that shards on two federations of one template merge, and 20 of 23 settings another study's tests tried were missing from it: two shards on different sandbox limits, images, trust paths, mount path, placement, fault or token timings or tool-server commit could have merged. It now holds, each after its default and in one canonical form (`2` and `2.0`, a key at its default and one left out, a set in another order are one value), the token lifetime (`tokens`), the one-space form's support, name and prefixes (`support`, the last two hashed), the sandbox's backend, Docker image ids, limits, mount path, I/O mode and oneclient arguments, trust paths, the agent's user and interpreter, the kill's grace and the placeholder token's hash (`sandbox`), the tool server's commit, command line, start bound and each condition's allow-list, rights and manifest hash (`toolserver`), the data provider, the range map's digest and the bounds a run's probes and progressive ingest read during the run (`placement`), and every bound of the fault injector's restore and heal (`faults.settings`); the fault targets no longer hash the Compose project, each deployment's own name. Nothing per federation is in it: addresses, ids, ports, host, network and project names, paths, secrets, the host's commands, and what is checked before a campaign; a command line that shapes a run (the tool server's, oneclient's extra arguments) is in by the SHA-256 of its federation-neutral form, each of the federation's URLs, hosts, ids and its platform file's directory replaced by its role (`neutral_words`). `[placement]`'s concurrency, QoS scan and reset settings stay out, recorded by C35. There is no legacy form: no store has recorded `fingerprint()` yet, so a store of the earlier form is refused at its resume, naming each new setting, and a merge never joins it with one of this form. `PlatformConfig.legacy_fingerprint()` keeps #326's earlier spaces over this new form. A tool-server condition added during a campaign is now a refused resume, not growth (`expcore_onedata.runsettings`; `tests/support/runsettings_support.py` classifies every key the loaders accept).
- **Check your study's code: a reset that does not run in its run's own context, or that runs beside its own agent, now fails closed** (O66). On a platform with `[faults]` every reset holds the federation's reset lock under the run's admission. A reset made where the run's admission is not visible is refused (`AdmissionRefused`) before anything is sent. That covers a thread pool's `loop.run_in_executor`, a `threading.Thread` started without the run's context, and a script with no `shared_admission`. So is a reset made while the run's sandbox or tool server is up, from its launch to the end of its stop. Until now the first kind ran unheld and the second ran beside the agent; both succeeded. `AdmissionRefused` is no infrastructure kind, so the attempt is recorded as a **harness defect** (`harness_error`, charged to nobody, its class and stack hash kept). Its harness-error stop holds the model, or all dispatch, until a release, so a campaign whose study resets that way stops at its first run instead of resetting unheld. What to change:
  - reset from the executor directly, or through `asyncio.to_thread`, which carries the context;
  - reset before the run's sandbox and tool server start, or after they stop;
  - outside a campaign, wrap the reset in `shared_admission(config, reason=<code>)`, as `docs/interfaces.md` and the study guide show.
- **`shared_admission`'s `reason` is a short code, and a free-text reason is now refused** (O48, O12). It goes into the record of the holds (`shared-holds.jsonl`), which is published with the study, so it now follows the rule of a full reset's reason: a lower-case letter, then up to 63 lower-case letters, digits or underscores (`smoke_warmup`, `reset_between_batches`), holding no name the platform's configuration gives (the zone's name and host, each provider's name, id and host, each space's label, name, id and prefixes), compared lower-cased with every separator dropped. One check serves both (`expcore_onedata.shortcode.checked_code`). A reason such as `"smoke: warm-up of the first space"`, accepted until now, is `ValueError` before anything is taken, naming the argument and never repeating the value; `tool_admission.MAX_REASON` is gone. The form holds without `[faults]` too, and for `shared_admission_async`.
- **The campaign's environment names the core by its code, however it is installed (C06, C29, P01).** The core is 0.1.0 at many commits with identical dependencies, so a wheel or directory install recorded nothing about which code it was, and shards run on different core code could merge; one commit installed editable and from a git URL recorded two entries, so shards that were one campaign did not. The core's entry in the environment's `sources` is now `{kind: core, commit, tree, dirty, python_sha256}` (`expcore.provenance.core_identity`): read live from git for a checkout, and otherwise from `expcore/_build_identity.json`, which a new hatch build hook (`hatch_build.py`) writes into the wheel and the sdist. Editable, git-URL, directory, wheel and sdist installs of one commit record one entry; a resume, check or merge under another commit is refused, the merge naming both commits (`merge.check_core`). Dirt is a change to what a build of the core reads (`core_identity.builds_from`), not to a note or a test elsewhere in its checkout. `run`, `check`, `merge` and `analyze` hold the core to the study code identity's rule (`core_check.require_core`): unknown or stale refused always, dirty unless `--allow-dirty`, and a package build refuses a check recorded under a dirty or unknown core. A store an earlier core recorded keeps the earlier form: it resumes, is checked, analysed and packaged with the core in use read the way that core read it (`environment(..., earlier_core=True)`, `study.core_legacy`), so exactly where that core would have (an editable or git-URL store only under the same code); a new merge refuses its shards, which do not say which core ran them (`merge.refuse_earlier_form`), and a batch an earlier core merged is still checked and analysed. An anonymised package withholds the identity the core's wheel carries, and searches for the URL the core was installed from, which the environment no longer records; a plain package's `PACKAGE.json` names the core's commit and tree instead of that URL. The suite pins the checkout's identity as committed (`tests/support/core_pin.py`), so its outcome does not depend on uncommitted changes.
- **Breaking for a caller that wrote bare names in a release file: a release line names the serial of the stop it ends (C13).** A harness-error stop and a budget hold are each given a serial, `<count>.<random>`, in the transaction that records them, shown in the hold, the event and `HarnessStops.status()`; a line of `HarnessStopConfig.release_file` or `Budget.release_file` is `<name> <serial>` (`m1 3.k7f2qd9a`, `ALL 3.k7f2qd9a`) and ends only the stop with that serial, whatever the file's modification time. A release counted before when the file was newer than the stop, which a coarse file clock or a skewed writer's clock decided either way: a release written a tick after the stop was refused, and a file written before it but stamped ahead ended it. A bare name, the old form, now ends nothing, also for a stop recorded before serials (given one at the next start), and is said once in a warning and an event `harness_error_release_refused` or `budget_release_refused` with the exact line to write. No `campaign.toml` key, deploy script or example sets a release file; only a campaign built in code does. `release.take_listed` takes each name's serial and returns the refused lines too; the scheduler reads a stop's hold from `HarnessStops.hold_reasons()`.
- **A record is known by its name, and a file of a batch that nothing accounts for fails the check (R04).** `check` and `merge` read every `.json` file under `runs/` as a record, so a study could not keep its own evidence in `request.run_dir`: the offline HPC rehearsal's plugin, which collects its sandbox's metadata there, had each file fail the check as a record that does not load, and a file of another suffix was passed over in silence. Now a record is a file named as the core writes one, `<SHA-256 of the run key>.<attempt>.json` (`expcore.records.record.is_record_name`, the one form the core has ever used, so every older batch and record is still read). Every other file of the batch must be a hashed-only input (an outcome marker, the campaign's snapshot, a declared side file), the check file or a dot-name temporary. Anything else fails the check at stage `unexpected`, against its own path, is neither hashed nor replayed, and is counted apart from the records (`N of N records passed`, then `K unexpected files in the batch (...)`). It fails the check because the check vouches for every byte the analysis and a package read from `out/runs`. `merge` refuses a shard that holds one, naming each file. `require_current_check` (so `analyze`, `report`, `regenerate`), `archive_batch` and an anonymised package's build refuse one added after a passing check. `rescore_files` and `archived_records` take records by name only. A study's evidence enters a run directory only as a declared side file (study guide, "Files an agent writes"). The check file's keys are unchanged: an unexpected file is a failure like any other.
- An interrupted attempt is charged what it recorded, not its whole bound (C05). With a 60 USD ceiling and a 32.975 USD bound for one model, one attempt killed in flight was charged its bound at the resume, holding that model's remaining runs on budget though it had spent a fraction; an HPC shard killed mid-run was charged so too. With a budget, each attempt now keeps a call log (`expcore.campaign.interrupted_spend`): its spend guard counts each request in the store before it goes out and adds each reply's counts after it (the `SpendGuard.after_reply` hook takes the cache parts and an `unread` flag, which `RecordingBackend` passes), and the spend journal has a `call` line per request and per reply, with the attempt's id, which a resume sums by attempt (its `attempt` lines name how many it holds, `calls_sent` and `calls_closed`), so it grows linearly in the calls. Recovery records how an interrupted attempt is charged (`spend_basis`): `measured` (the replies' counts at the model's price), `measured_plus_inflight` (plus one request's bound for a request sent and not answered), or `bound` (no log, an empty or unreadable or inconsistent one, a reply that left a count out or was not read). A store whose ledger names a spend journal records `bound` until the start's count of its own journal confirms the log (a snapshot's may be short), and a lost attempt the journal never saw end is charged by its journaled log. The budget gate, `selection_ledger.spend_of`, the export (`interrupted_spend`, and `basis` in `lost_in_kill`) and the report ("Interrupted attempts' spend", and a Basis column in "Spend lost in kills") follow. Nothing recorded is charged less; an attempt without a log is charged as before. A budget hold after a kill is released by resuming: the gate re-reads the measured spend at every assessment and ends a hold that is no longer over (`budget_hold_over`); the study guide says what to do when it stays over.
- The reset waits for what can still change, once (O51). A live reset of four spaces took about 16 minutes against 197 s planned. A space whose every transfer observation shows nothing, and that cancelled nothing, now settles on two observations one `transfer_settle_s` apart (30 s, where four in a row took 90 s); anything seen or cancelled keeps the full polls, and the record says which (`transfers.fast`). `resetspaces.SpacesReset` resets several spaces as one: each space's stages in order, the transfer settle's waits, the occupancy wait, the quiet wait and the listings' late look each taken once for every space, and the rest of each stage's work for at most `[placement] reset_concurrency` (4) spaces at once; `SpaceReset.run` is it with one space, and a failure in one space fails the reset with every space's record. The eviction no longer waits for the reset's own job that a listing still shows ongoing after its record said it ended, and records every wait it takes, by why (`eviction.waits`). `SpaceReset.plan_scan` gives a planner the files and each file's holdings from the recursive listing the QoS scan makes, and a reset given it (`run(planned=)`) lists the space no second time. Estimated from the live stage times, the four-space reset's stages this changes fall from about 950 s to about 210 s, and the whole reset to about 420 s with the per-file occupancy verdict's two readings of the largest space (about 106 s each, O46); occupancy polling is left for later.
- `[agent] tool_call_bound_s` is renamed `tool_server_call_bound_s` (#235, L17), and `AgentConfig.tool_call_bound_s` with it, so that the name says the scope: the bound is for calls to a tool server. The old key is refused at load, naming the new one and why, and is never read under the new meaning: it capped every call, a code cell too, which was not what it was for (see Fixed). The record's `agent_config.tool_call_bound_s` is `agent_config.tool_server_call_bound_s`; a record that holds the old name is refused as an unknown field, since no release held it. A study that declared the old key drops it, or declares the new one if a tool server's hang is to be classified; a store that began with the old key is not resumed with the new one, as the caps in the fingerprint differ.
- The REST gate sends the zone's token-scope inference with no token in any header, its own token staying in the call's body (O30). Onezone serves the inference to anyone, but a header's token is authenticated first, and a data-path token's data-access caveats failed that with 401 live, so the published client failed before its first read on every REST data path. Every other call keeps the gate's token in its header.
- `[sandbox.federation]` beside a rest level of `[sandbox.data_paths]` needs `zone`, the zone's name on the federation's network, since the data gate now reads the table (#203): a platform file that declares the table and a rest level without it is refused at load, naming the key. The table is accepted with a rest level alone, and a `zone` in a table no rest level reads is refused. `FederationNetwork` gains `zone`, None by default.
- The mount sidecar and the data gate take the data-path token from a file bound into the one container alone, read-only: the sidecar's shell reads it into oneclient's environment as it starts oneclient, and the gate reads it at its start from the path its configuration names (the gate's file is root's, the user the gate runs as). Before, the token was an environment variable given to `docker run`, which docker keeps in the container's configuration, where `docker inspect` showed it to anyone with access to the Docker daemon. No container of the sandbox is given a credential that way any more. The host's copy of each file is removed once its holder has started, and again at every teardown, a held or kept run's included. A run killed outright while a holder is still starting leaves its file until the next start on the same work root, which removes the files several times older than a healthy start keeps one (the start's record counts them as `reaped_token_files`), or until the run's directory is reaped. A run whose copy cannot be removed is left where it is, never moved into the kept evidence.
