- `PlatformConfig` holds `spaces`; `space` and `support` are properties of the only space and refuse when several are declared (`space_named(label)` names one). `CaveatSpec.space_id` is one id or a tuple of ids, and `TokenIssuer.check_user` reads the rights in each. With several spaces every provisioner message starts with the space's position, and each space's probes have a namespace of their own, `platform_probes_space<i>`. `client.UNVERIFIED_ENDPOINTS`, empty once the live federation confirmed the earlier entries, holds the reset's calls no live run has made yet (its QoS calls and a transfer's cancel), each with a live test. `OnezoneClient.mutate` and `read_range` take `space`, which binds the gate to that space. A provisioner whose `[placement] holder` does not support its space is refused. `place_ranges` keys its record's `providers` by position (`providers[i]`), no longer by name.
- `export.json` and the campaign snapshot name each attempt's directory as `<digest>/try<N>`, relative to the batch, instead of the absolute path the store keeps; the snapshot is also rebuilt so its free space holds no older copy. Neither carries a path of the machine the campaign ran on any more.
- `analyse_study` writes nothing. `write_analysis` writes `export.json`, `scores.json` and `analysis.json`, `write_report` writes `report.md`, and `regenerate` compares all four with what the records give.
- The report and the terminal summary take a contrast's exploratory label from the statistics kernel's own flag, and refuse a result whose flag disagrees with its Holm decisions. A contrast outside the Holm family is shown as exploratory in every table, never as confirmatory.
- The platform probes' snapshot is version 3. It names every provider by its position, `providers[i]`, never by its configured name: the tracked providers, each file's readings, `pairs` and their `from`, `on_demand`, and each transfer record's `provider`. A record is published in a replication package, and version 2 keyed all of these by name. `gained_bytes` in it is stated as placement gained: the bytes of the blocks a provider's block map shows at run end that it did not show at run start; what it showed at the start and no longer does is `lost_bytes`, kept apart, so it is not a net change, and it is not bytes on the wire. The run's end now waits on the transfer listing (`[placement] transfer_settle_s`, 30 s by default, for up to `transfer_settle_polls` observations).
- The campaign fingerprint and `replay-check.json` gain `environment`, so a store or a check made before it cannot be resumed or analysed. `PACKAGE.json` gains `environment`, `wheels`, `study_tokens_exempt`, `code_identity`, `key_locations`, `vcs` and `uncommitted`, and the generated Makefile's `check` target installs the package's wheels; `check-installed` is the old behaviour.
- The code identity and the campaign fingerprint hash `models.toml` with each `key_file` and `key_env` value replaced by its placeholder (`expcore.config.key_locations`; `CodeScope` gains `canonical`). A study whose `models.toml` names no key location keeps every hash it had, so its campaigns, records and outputs stand. One that names a key location gets a new tree hash, a new hash of `models.toml` and a new digest for each model with a key: its campaign cannot be resumed, and a refusal names the leaves that differ because the core that started it counted where the keys live; its replay check must be made again before it is analysed or packaged, and a stamp written over that `models.toml` reads as stale until it is written again.
- `users.SlotTokens` and `sandbox.provider.SandboxPlatform` take a required `events`, the platform's event sink, with no default, because a silent no-op would lose an event. A caller that built either without one must now pass it.
- Record version 3 gains the target of a fault, two more optional fields added the strictly additive way: `outcome.infra_target`, the position label (`providers[1]`) an infrastructure failure names, and `treatment_targets`, the target the condition declares per kind. Each is absent when there is none, never a null or an empty table, so a version-3 record written before them still validates, writes back and replays byte for byte. Where a target is declared, version 3's target rule scores the attempt as treatment only when the failure names that target and the fault injector's reading shows it injected in this attempt; a record that declares no target is decided as before. The merge-point fixture `tests/data/record-v3/03-fault-target.json` pins the shape; versions 1 and 2 keep their own rules, and run markers are schema 3. A study plugin may supply an `admission` hook, held from before an attempt to after its post-run check. A health check may answer deferred, which closes no gate and charges no attempt, for at most `SchedulerConfig.max_defer_s`. An outcome whose post-run check could not complete is set aside as `post_check_unverified`, a non-treatable core kind.
- `[toolserver] insecure_tls` and `[sandbox.mount] tls` are refused at load with a pointer to `[tls]`, which replaces both; `ToolServer.insecure_tls` and `MountSettings.tls` are gone, and the sandbox metadata's `mount` no longer holds `tls`, now the run's own `tls`. `StudyPlugin` gains `platform`, empty by default, and the campaign fingerprint gains a `platform` section only when a plugin gives one, so campaigns already started keep their fingerprint.
- A contrast in the Holm family that loses a pair to a treatment attempt scored `None` is refused. `treatment_outcomes = "excluded"` in its preregistration leaves every pair with a treatment attempt out instead; `treatment_outcomes = "scored"` claims the scorer returns values for them, and the claim is checked: such a contrast is refused too, past its preregistered `max_unscored_treatment` (0 unless set), which cannot excuse every treatment attempt. A confirmatory contrast that `"excluded"` leaves with no pair is refused too. Each contrast counts its missing pairs by cause (`pairs_missing` in `analysis.json`), and the report puts the declaration and its count in the contrast's row. The example's scorer now returns values for its treatment attempts (no answer: accuracy 0, with the rounds and lookups before the failure), and its `relay-vs-baseline` declares `"scored"`. `Row.from_export` requires the export entry's `treatment` flag.
- The report gives each condition's planned runs, how each ended (analysed, exhausted, unfinished, left out) and its attempts set aside, and `analysis.json` carries the same counts under `conditions`. The infrastructure table lists every condition, with a row of zeros (kind `none`) for one that met no failure, and the report refuses an export whose run lists do not add up to `planned_runs`.
- A fifth conclusion word, `untestable`, for a contrast that used no pair, whatever the cause; such a contrast concluded `not_detected` before. It has no estimate, p-value or minimum detectable effect, even when its estimator returned numbers for no data. In the Holm family it counts as p = 1, as a NaN p-value already did, so no other contrast's adjusted p-value changes. Its report row gives the causes of its missing pairs with their counts, and the report and the terminal summary refuse a result whose word disagrees with its pairs. `conclude` takes an `untestable` flag.
- `models.toml` no longer refuses a `backend` outside `openai_compat`, `anthropic` and `stub` when the table is loaded; such a model is refused when the plugin is loaded, unless the plugin supplies a `backend_factory`. The example's executor checks that its backend has `use(script)` instead of requiring a `StubAdapter`, so a backend that wraps the stub serves it.
- Every run of a campaign is recorded with the study's whole model table (`RunRequest.models`), so the key of every model in `models.toml` is redacted from its records, whatever the backend. A key variable set to a value too short to redact now refuses a run of any model in the table, not only of its own.
- `check` reads only the `[study]` table of `study.toml` and no longer validates the study's other configuration files. It imports the study's `[study] platforms` and plugin module only when a record names a backend no native parser is registered for, and says so in one line. A record that names a kind neither the core nor `[study.kinds]` holds imports the platforms alone, also with one line. A batch that needs neither runs none of the study's code. It does not call `build`, so it needs no model or key; a parser registered only inside `build` is not found. A module that cannot then be imported refuses `check`, naming the module and the exception's type, never its text. The `check` of a merged batch is the exception: it rebuilds `merge.json` from the shards' snapshots and the plan, so it loads the whole study.
- A condition that names an unknown infrastructure kind is refused with the treatable kinds as its choices, instead of every known kind, and the error says how a platform's or a study's kind becomes known. A known kind that is not treatable is refused as one that can never be a treatment, whether the core, a listed platform or the study declared it.
- The example measures with an instrument. Its executor makes a fresh `FactsServed` for every run, starts it before `record_run`, finishes it after, and writes the record `finish_instruments` returns; the scorer reads the instrument's count as a fourth outcome, `served`, which is `None` when the snapshot failed. Every record of the example holds `instruments.facts`, and `scores.json` gains the outcome; the contrasts and the report's tables are unchanged.
- `PlatformConfig.fingerprint()` gains `data_paths`, each level's kind and a REST level's listing mode, whenever `platform.toml` has a `[sandbox]`, so a campaign started before it cannot be resumed. It gains `served_bytes`, the caps of the tables of the bytes served, only when `[sandbox.served_bytes]` is declared, so a campaign that ran under the default caps resumes under the same file. `SandboxSpec` gains `listing` (`paged` by default), and `SandboxSettings` gains `served_caps`, from `[sandbox.served_bytes]`; the sandbox's metadata gains `listing` and `served_bytes`, and a gate log it cannot parse makes its gate summary unknown instead of failing the collection; each line of the gate's log for an answer gains `body`, a 206 of a file's content also `content_range`, and a gate whose stop wrote every close line ends its log with a `teardown_complete` line; the sandbox's `gate_stop` gains `output_read` and `teardown_incomplete`, and its `log_write_failed` is `None` when the gate's output could not be read; `gatelog.unknown()` no longer holds `upstream_bytes`, a counter of a mode the gate does not have.

- The example's prompt names each task's `region`, which its executor reads from `study.tasks`, so every record's user prompt holds a `REGION` slot and each condition's prompt hash changes; the outcomes, the contrasts and the report's tables are unchanged.
- A plugin can be split over several files. The core imports it as a package whose path is its own directory, under a name made from a hash of its path, so it imports a module beside it relatively (`from .scoring import Scorer`), and two studies' modules of one name never meet. The plugin and its modules are compiled from their source on every load, never from cached bytecode, so what runs is what the code identity hashed. The code that runs is held to the identity by where it comes from: while the plugin is imported and while `build` runs, every file of the study directory whose code runs, and every module whose file lies there, must be a file `[study] code` covers and a module of the plugin's package, or loading the plugin is refused, naming `study.toml` and the file; a file imported through the module search path or loaded by path is refused even when listed, and an absolute import of a module beside the plugin (`import scoring`) is refused with the fix. Every module of the study's own code is dropped before each load and after a refused one, so no study is served another's; the core's modules and an installed distribution's code, editable or not, are the recorded environment's and are never refused or dropped, wherever they lie. A backend factory in a module beside the plugin goes into the fingerprint as that module's name and its own. `import_plugin_module` takes the loaded study, or its identity, instead of the plugin's path.
- A key of a condition in `study.toml` is checked as a factor before its value's type: a key that is a built-in factor, or no declared factor at all, is refused as such whatever its value, so `seeds = [1]` on a condition reads "is not a declared factor" rather than "must be a non-empty string", which suggested a string would be accepted.
- The README that `python -m expcore example` writes no longer links `../../docs/`, which leads nowhere outside the core's checkout, and leads with the study's own commands without `--allow-dirty`, saying where that option belongs. The command's hint says to commit the study before running it.
- Redaction also replaces a secret as Python's repr shows it and the repr of its UTF-8 bytes, each JSON-escaped and percent-encoded as the raw form is, and, in plain text that is never sealed (the harness-error log, a printed message, never a record, whose keys a common word could rename), each line of a multi-line secret of four or more characters on its own (an exception group puts `| ` between them), so `KeyError(secret)`, whose repr escapes a quote when the secret holds both kinds, leaves nothing of it in a record or the harness-error log.
- `run` reports what it dispatched as attempts of the campaign's runs (`dispatched 65 attempts of 62 runs`), not as runs, counting the attempts it began (`RunSummary.attempts`, from the store's attempt rows), so a worker that got no lease, was not admitted by the platform's run admission, or found a gate closed once it had either is not counted. A refusal on the command line names the command (`report refused: ...`), and the analysis's refusals no longer begin with "refusing to analyse".
- A replay that finds a recorded reply the model's raw reply does not give names the part in plain words: `the recorded tool call does not match the model's raw reply in that turn (transcript[k].native): the record was changed after it was written, or the backend now reads that reply differently`, in place of a sentence about the "neutral turn" and the "native message". The study guide says what replay compares.
- An entry of `models.toml` whose backend the core does not build, and that has a problem of its own, says first that the core does not build that backend and that the plugin must supply a `backend_factory`, whatever key fails next (one of the wrong type or an unknown one included), so `backend = "toy"` with no `api_id` no longer reads as only a missing `api_id`. A refusal that names a backend close to one of the core's, a model of `campaign.toml` that `models.toml` does not define, or a model name the table cannot resolve adds the closest name (`did you mean ...?`), and the campaign's error lists the models `models.toml` defines. A condition that leaves a factor out is refused with that factor's levels.
- Both contrast tables of `report.md` gain a `Margin` column, the preregistered equivalence margin, and the interval at 1 - 2 alpha it is compared with (`90% equivalence interval` at alpha 0.05, `97.5%` at 0.0125: a level keeps every digit it has, up to ten significant ones, and the reported interval's level is named the same way), beside the interval at the reported level, so `equivalent` and `trivial_difference` can be checked from the report; a contrast without a margin shows `-` in both. Every report changes with them, so a package built before this change no longer regenerates its `report.md`; `analysis.json` is unchanged.
- The replay check's write-back redacts nothing: it checks a record's canonical form only, so a record written under earlier redaction rules writes back to itself. A record that holds a value of the checking environment's secrets stops the check at a stage of its own, `secret`, naming where, instead of failing the write-back. The replay itself redacts nothing, as before.
- `analysis.json` of every study gains `exploratory_only`, false unless the preregistration says true, so an `analysis.json` written before no longer regenerates byte for byte.
- `BudgetGate`, `attempt_costs` and `bound_usd` take the campaign's `Limits` in place of the token budget, and the store's attempts and the outcome marker gain `turn_usage`. A study with a budget adds `context_tokens` to each model that is not free, which changes that model's entry in the campaign fingerprint, so a campaign opened before cannot be resumed and starts afresh; its records still analyse. The example's models declare a context window.
- `ModelBackend.probe` returns `ProbeReply | None`, what its one-token reply reported, in place of `None`: a backend of a study's own returns its reply's counts, or passes on the reply of the backend it wraps, or its model is not admitted. `bound_usd`, `cap_bound_usd` and `overshoot_usd` take `reply_capped` (by default False: the bound by the window, whatever the backend), `attempt_costs` and `BudgetGate` the models held to the reply maximum, and `REPLY_CAPPED_BACKENDS` is gone. The store's attempts gain `topups` and `topup_usd`, and its meta `probe_spend_usd`, what admission's probes were charged. The core's infrastructure kinds gain `budget_hold`, which every export's `kinds` lists, the export gains `budget_holds`, which `read_export` requires, and the report gains its budget-hold table and the kind's line under the infrastructure table, so an `export.json` or a `report.md` written before no longer regenerates byte for byte.
- `python -m expcore.tools.manifest freeze OUT --pass-env NAME -- COMMAND` works as its usage shows: the options may follow the file, and everything after the first `--` is the server's command. A server command must now follow `--`.
- The package's `make check` runs pip with `--no-cache-dir` as well as `--isolated`, so the offline, hash-pinned install neither reads nor writes the reader's own pip cache. Where that cache was not writable (a sandbox), pip warned that the cache was disabled, which read like a failure of the check.
- `health_kind` and `identity_kind` under `[scheduler]` in `campaign.toml` are documented in the study guide, with their defaults (`sandbox`, the kind a failed health check is recorded as, and `endpoint`, the kind of a run whose endpoint changed its served identity), and the example's campaign.toml writes both out; the defaults are unchanged. One that names no known infrastructure kind is refused when the study is loaded, naming campaign.toml and the key, with the kinds known then: the core's, those of the modules `[study] platforms` imports and the study's own `[study.kinds]`. It was accepted at load and crashed `run` with a traceback that named neither file nor key. A kind a condition declares as its treatment is still refused when the campaign starts.
- **The mutation gate is narrower: a file addressed by id must now lie in the configured space (`[space] id`).** This holds for a mutation by file id, a range read, a content read and a transfer job's file. Before, a file in any space whose live name started with one of `[space] prefixes` passed; a request under `/spaces/{id}` is still gated by the space's name and the prefixes alone.
- The mutation gate finds the space of a file addressed by id by walking its `parent_id` links through the provider up to the configured space's root, at most `client.MAX_SPACE_DEPTH` (64) links, instead of reading a `path` attribute, which Onedata 25.0 does not have; it then reads the space's name from Onezone before every request, as before. The roots and links a walk read are kept as the provider's that gave them, at most `client.MAX_KEPT_LINKS` (50,000) links, least recently used dropped first, all dropped by `close()`; no walk that failed keeps anything, and one that passes the bound over kept links walks once more with every link read anew before it refuses. `OnezoneClient` takes `kept_links`. `client.NotSent` is new: `OutOfScope`, `MintNotSent` and the new `ScopeUnread`, raised when one of the gate's own reads fails and keeping that read's status, error id, errno and transport flag, are all `NotSent`. A failed read of a space's name for a mutation under `/spaces/{id}` is now `ScopeUnread` rather than a plain `PlatformError`. `UNVERIFIED_ENDPOINTS` is empty.
- A mutation with a raw body sends `Content-Type: application/octet-stream` (`client.UPLOAD_CONTENT_TYPE`), which Onedata 25.0 requires of a content upload, or the type its caller names: `OnezoneClient.mutate`, `Provisioner.put_file` and `Provisioner.upload` take `content_type`, a plain `type/subtype`, and refuse anything else before a request is sent. Only `application/octet-stream` is confirmed on 25.0, and the fake federation answers 415 to any other type.
- Provisioning waits until a provider sees a file written through another before it reads or schedules a transfer through it (`Provisioner.await_visible`, `consistency.wait_visible`): the file's size is read through the provider every 0.5 s until it equals the data provider's, for at most `consistency.VISIBLE_TIMEOUT_S` (120 s), past which `NotVisible`, a `WaitTimeout`, names the provider by its position. A 404 or 400 `posix` `enoent` is "not visible yet"; any other error fails closed. Placement, `fetch` and the scheduling of a replication or an eviction wait; the probes' reads never do.
- `Provisioner.evict` judges an eviction by the block map alone: while the provider still holds bytes after the transfer ended, it schedules the eviction again after `provision.EVICTION_RETRY_PAUSE_S` (10 s), up to `EVICTION_ATTEMPTS` (5), and after the last waits the settle bound and raises `NotPlaced`. On 25.0 an eviction made right after a provider fetched the bytes completes with `filesEvicted` 0. Its record gains `attempts`, and `require_empty` takes `timeout_s`.
- The reset's eviction stage records what an eviction's counters show and names the cause when files still hold bytes (O01). The block maps still decide: op-worker counts a file in `filesProcessed` and in neither `filesEvicted` nor `filesFailed` when it skips it without a failure, which it does for a file the provider holds nothing of, an open file, a file whose other copy it does not know of yet (25.0's eviction that evicts nothing, right after a placement) and a file cancelled per file because a QoS requirement still covers it there (a requirement whose deletion has not reached that provider through dbsync). The first three are normal and the second attempt clears the third, so a gap alone fails nothing. Each provider's record gains `files_processed`, `files_evicted` and `files_failed`, the last attempt's counts, None when a record lacks one. When files still hold bytes after the last attempt, the failure (`eviction.EvictionIncomplete`, a `NotPlaced`) names the provider's position, the counts and, when files were skipped without a failure, the likely cause, and the failed stage's record carries the same counts. `FakeData` gains `qos_covered` (a file whose eviction on a provider is cancelled per file) and its transfer records `filesProcessed`.
- Placement's record gains `per_file`: per file the ranges and bytes asked for beside the ranges and bytes the receiver's block map holds. On 25.0 a range read places at least 1 MiB from its offset, clipped to the file. The day-0 round trip passes when the held ranges cover the range it read, and its facts gain `held_ranges`, `held_covers_range` and `eviction_attempts`.
- `PlatformError` gains `errno`, the POSIX code Onedata gives with the error id `posix`. `client.answered_absent` is the one test of absence: a 404 or exactly 400 `posix` `enoent`, as 25.0 answers a deleted entry, and never a request that was not sent. Teardown, its absence polls and its listing of a logged directory, `read_only`'s clean-up and the gate's walk use it, so they stop as soon as an entry reads gone; so do the consistency waits for a fresh space root and for a transfer its provider does not show yet (`consistency.NOT_FOUND` is gone). Any other 400 is neither gone nor coming: teardown then polls to its bound, keeps the entry logged and reports it left, and the waits raise at once. The absence is the provider's that was read.
- The day-0 check `content_type` is unknown when it measured nothing, with no REST data path among those cases; it passed then before. `precampaign.measure_content_type` gives the verdict with whether a type was measured, and `content_type_check` gives the health gate what it gave before. `read_only` counts exactly 400 `posix` `eacces`, as 25.0 refuses the write, as a refusal beside 401 and 403, and gives each provider's row `sent`, and `code` and `errno` beside a status; a write that was never sent makes it unknown with the reason, never a write "not refused".
- A listing that ends without the API's last-page marker is refused, never taken as complete (O26). A children page ends a listing only with a boolean `isLast` that is true, whatever its `nextPageToken`, and a page of the transfer listing only with `nextPageToken` null, with no `isLast` asked for; a page without the marker raises `PlatformError` (the provisioner, the pre-campaign check and the day-0 listing check) or `Unreadable` (the probes' transfer log, which records the listing as unknown), and the Compose federation's baseline writer refuses it too. A page without `isLast` was read as the last one before, and so was a transfer page without `nextPageToken`, or with an empty one. `provision.next_page_token` reads a children page for every reader. The rules are those of the listings measured on Onedata 25.0 (`deploy/compose-federation/day0/2026-11-12-federation-facts-listing-end.md`).
- The testing module's fakes end their listings as Onedata 25.0 was measured to (`fake_data.LISTING_END_FACTS`). A children page of `FakeData` and `MockOnedata` has `nextPageToken`, `isLast` and `children`, in that order, with a token on the last page too, where it had none (`FakeData`) or a null one (`MockOnedata`); a page the directory's last entries fill now says `isLast` false and is followed by an empty last page; and the page after a last page answers 400 `posix` `eagain`. `FakeData`'s transfer listing gives a full page a token, so an empty page with a null token follows it. A study's test that counted pages, or stopped on the token, sees the 25.0 shapes now.
- A writing condition's tool-server token carries no data-access caveat (#177): its caveats are its expiry and the `rest` interface alone, and its examination refuses one that carries `data.path`, `data.objectid` or `data.readonly`. On Onedata 25.0 any of them limits a token to data access, and the token it had, with `data.path`, was refused by `POST /transfers` with `tokenCaveatUnverified`. Its reach is now its leased tool-server user, and four reads hold it to the tool-server plane before the mint (the scope and the clusters just after it), at the tool server's launch and at its teardown: the user's rights in each of the plane's spaces must be the condition's; Onezone's scope of the token (`infer_access_token_scope`), which lists every space the user is an effective member of, must be exactly the plane's spaces, so a space of another study, or one the study declares for the data path alone, refuses it, named by its position and never by its id or name; the user must hold no Onezone admin privilege; and the user must be an effective member of no cluster, because one who is a member of a provider's cluster can act through that provider (with `cluster_update` mint its tokens and change its configuration, with `cluster_delete` delete it) beyond the plane's spaces (`tokenscope`). Onezone has no read of another user's clusters, so they are read as the token itself, `GET /user/effective_clusters`. A refusal at the mint drops the token, and one at the launch or teardown sets the run aside as `tool_plane_integrity`; a read that gets no answer is an outage there, never a pass. `CaveatSpec(readonly=False)` mints no `data.path` and refuses the `oneclient` interface, itself a data-access caveat on 25.0. The data path's token and a read-only condition's are unchanged, byte for byte, and neither reads the scope, the admin privileges or the clusters. `FakeFederation` answers a user's admin privileges (`admin_privileges`) and the clusters of the user a token it issued belongs to (`clusters`), and can fail or replace the scope and the clusters answers alone (`scope_status`, `scope_hook`, `clusters_status`, `clusters_hook`).
- The tool server is pinned at onedata-mcp `8c9b10c34efdad29498362fc4be4e785ff2a102b` (#191), where it was `0cc17ce`. The one commit between them resolves a space name from the Oneprovider's `GET /spaces`, the user's own spaces, instead of Onezone's admin listing, which answers 403 for the tool-server user, so a tool given a space name was refused where the same tool given its id served; it also restores `providerName` in file distributions. The tool manifest is unchanged: the 7-tool writing manifest has the same SHA-256 at both commits. A study that records the pin (`[toolserver] commit`) sets the new one.
- `deploy/compose-federation/federation.py down` runs `docker compose down --volumes`, so any anonymous or project volume goes with the containers; the federation's data is all bind-mounted under its root, so nothing it needs is lost. Its teardown check now also asserts that no Docker volume labelled with the project (`com.docker.compose.project`) remains, and fails naming the volumes if one does, beside the containers, networks and the root it already checked.
- A deleted entry is read as gone only by a read that asks for a strict attribute. On Onedata 25.0, `GET /data/{id}` resolves only the attributes it is asked for: no `attribute` parameter, or `name` or `type` among them, answers 400 `posix` `enoent`, while `parent_id` or `file_id` alone, and `size` alone (a directory's by the source, a file's seen live by another study), keep answering from a tombstone on every provider, for 14 days by default (`document_expiry`, from the source, not measured); a deleted file's `size` alone was not traced and may answer while its file location exists (the id attribute is `file_id`: `?attribute=guid` answers 400 `badValueNotAllowed` for every entry, deleted or not) (`deploy/compose-federation/day0/2026-11-13-federation-facts-deleted-entries-by-id.md`, from the op-worker source at f118ed9 and the 12 Nov live reads). The core's two visibility waits, `consistency.wait_visible` and `Provisioner.await_visible`'s read on the data provider, used to read `size` alone, so a file deleted during the wait could be reported visible; they now read `name`, `size` and `parent_id` together (`consistency.VISIBLE_ATTRIBUTES`) and check the size from that answer, and a deleted file is `NotVisible`, or refused at the data provider with 400 `enoent`. Nothing else in the waits changes. `FakeData` models the tombstone: a deleted entry read for `size`, `parent_id` or `file_id` alone answers 200 with only those, from its last state, for `tombstone_s` seconds of its clock from the delete landing (default `fake_data.TOMBSTONE_S`, 14 days, so no test reaches the expiry and a presence check by one of them fails in a test), and a read with no attribute, or with `name` or `type` among them, answers `enoent`, and a read of `guid` is refused with 400 `badValueNotAllowed` whatever the entry. A deleted file's `size` alone answers as a directory's does, which is an assumption. `ERRORS_LEGACY` keeps no tombstone. `FakeData.deleted_parents` is replaced by `FakeData.tombstones`, and a read of a deleted entry's children, content or block map is no longer answered by `parent_id`.
- **Every slot declares its tool-server user's rights, and a run leases a slot of its condition's rights (#205, O31).** `[slots.<name>.toolserver] rights = [...]` is required, by a condition's rules: an existing platform.toml adds one line per slot, the rights its tool-server user holds, and one without it is refused at load naming `slots[i]`. Each set of declared rights is a lease group, `users.group_of(rights)`, of the slots that declare it, and conditions with the same rights share one; a run with a tool server leases from its condition's (`SandboxPlatform.condition_group`), so one campaign runs a read-only and a writing condition interleaved, in its randomised order, each run on a slot whose user holds its condition's rights, and a run waits only for a slot of its own group. Before, every run leased from the one group `platform_users`, and a run that drew a slot of the other condition's rights failed its token mint; splitting the campaign by condition would have confounded the condition with time. A run with a data path and no tool server still leases from `users.GROUP`, every slot. A plan condition whose rights no slot declares is refused when the campaign is built (`Scheduler.plan_groups`, asked before the store is opened or a model probed), naming the condition by its position; `SlotTokens.mint` refuses a slot of other rights before it journals or mints; `Slots.users_of` refuses a lease whose slot is not of its group; the day-0 outage agent leases from its condition's group. `users.Slot` gains `rights`. Neither the record nor the campaign fingerprint changes.
- The probes' transfer log and the reset's history read a run's window of the ended-transfer listing, never the space's whole history (#207, O32). On the 25.0 build (op-worker `f118ed9`, from the source) ended transfers never expire and cannot be removed, and the listing is newest first: by finish time for `ended`, by schedule time for `waiting` and `ongoing`, with a page token that resumes after the last job a page gave, and no time filter. So a long campaign passed `MAX_LISTED` (2000) within two runs, leaving `on_demand_lower_bound` unknown, and would have failed its reset past `LISTED_MAX` (100,000). Both readers now read the waiting and ongoing listings to their end and walk the ended one newest first (`expcore_onedata.transferorder.newest_first`), stopping at the first job whose record says it ended before the window's start less the clock-skew allowance and `STOP_MARGIN_S` (10 s); each bound counts the jobs of that window, and a window that holds more is still unknown. The order is checked as the walk reads, through the page that holds the stop and across every record of the next page (one page and its records more), and a listing out of order is unknown, naming the assumption; a job of the window two or more pages past the stop is not seen, and rests on the order holding by construction of the listing's keys; so is the global clock more than the margin behind this host's, for the reset's history as for the probes: both place the window on the global clock, the one a job's times are stamped on, which they read from Onezone's public current-time endpoint, and the reset also fails on a reading that fails or cannot be read, where the probes leave every bound unknown (`clock_offset_unknown`). The margin decides where reading stops, never what counts. Changed meanings: `space_transfers.listed` counts the jobs read down to the cutoff (a new `ended_since` gives it), and the reset's `history.ended` counts the jobs that surely ended since `SpaceReset.run(..., since=)`, the run's start, or by default the reset's own start on its new epoch clock `SpaceReset(now=)`: those whose finish lies after the band at the window's start. The new `history.uncertain` counts, apart, those in that band, which may have ended on either side of `since`; the band runs from `since` moved by the least offset less the clock-skew allowance to `since` moved by the greatest plus it, the offsets being those the readings allow (each reading's offset less and plus half its round trip; a new `history.clock` gives them, the half round trip, the Date headers of Onezone's answers as a cross-check, the allowance, the `band` and each provider's Date offsets, a diagnostic). A finish time is stamped on the platform's global clock, to which the providers synchronise theirs, and neither a provider's Date header nor Onezone's gives it, each being a node's system clock: providers 8 s ahead by their Date headers placed a job at 1001 before a window at 1000, and so did a multi-node Onezone's answering node with a system clock 8 s ahead and a global-clock bias of -8 s (source: op-worker `f118ed9` `transfer_changes.erl`, `replication_status.erl`, `replica_eviction_status.erl`; ctool `8ae7baf` `global_clock.erl`; oz-worker `14b5bda` `provider_logic_plugin.erl`, `get_current_time`; cowboy 2.9.0 `cowboy_clock.erl`). The global clock is read from Onezone's public `GET /provider/public/get_current_time` (answers `timeMillis`, no credential; oz-worker `14b5bda` `provider_routes.erl:192-198`, `provider_logic_plugin.erl:304-305,491-492`, `provider_rest_translator.erl:128-129`, `provider_logic.erl:548-554`), which no live run has made yet, so it is in `UNVERIFIED_ENDPOINTS` with a live contract test for the next live slot; a reading's offset is the global time less the midpoint of this host's clock around the read, and half the round trip widens the band. The allowance covers a provider's synchronisation error to Onezone's clock. `space_transfers.clock` gains `zone`, the readings (`min_s`, `max_s`, `half_trip_s` and `date_offsets_s`, the Date headers of the same answers, a cross-check); its `known` says whether the global clock was read, and the providers' offsets, under `offsets`, place nothing. `ended` counts the jobs that ended with a finish time in the window, and is exact in that sense when `uncertain` is 0 and a lower bound otherwise; a consumer that needs an exact count must treat `uncertain > 0` as not exact. A job cancelled before it started carries no finish time (`finishTime` 0) and sorts after every job that has one, so the walk stops at it: it and what follows are never read as part of the window nor counted against the bound, so a history of such cancellations cannot exhaust it, and `ended` never holds it, nor do the probes' `records` or `listed`; a job with a finish time listed after one with none fails the reading closed, naming the order; it moved no bytes. The reset's own cancels are counted in `transfers.cancelled`, so the ones it made while a job was still scheduled are never invisible. `earlier` says older ones are listed, a job read in the margin below the band among them, never one in the band. `OnezoneClient.get_dated` takes `retry=`, one attempt by default, so that the reset's dated reads keep its retry policy, and `anonymous=True`, which sends Onezone's public current-time read with no credential header and refuses any other path. `FakeData` dates Onezone's answers by its clock (`zone_skew`, `zone_date`, the answering node's system clock), serves Onezone's current-time endpoint, the global clock being its `clock` (`time_status`, `time_hook`), and lists transfers newest first by the same rule, its page token the last key a page gave, and `listing_time` lists a job out of that order for a test.
- The reset removes only the run's own QoS requirements and shows the files they touched settled and quiet (#209, O33). `SpaceReset.qos_requirements()` reads the baseline at the run's start and `run(qos_baseline=)` keeps it; by default none is kept, as before. `[placement] qos_scan` chooses how requirements are found: `"listing"` (the default, below) or `"summaries"` (a summary per leaf on every provider, as the reset first did); the listing reads, on every provider that supports the space, one recursive listing of the space root with `effQosInheritancePath`, the entries united, then a summary per entry a requirement is on, read on the provider that marks it, and each requirement's record: about one read per 1,000 entries per provider where the summaries read one per leaf per provider. Every provider is listed because a requirement is visible at once only on the provider it was added through, so one an agent added through another provider just before the reset, which the data provider has not yet received, is found and removed. The listing rests on two facts the op-worker source states, each with a gated live test (`include_directories`, and a requirement found through another provider); the 14 Nov 2026 live slot made it the default (the next entry). The removal itself is ordered (`resetremove.py`): each requirement is read on every provider until it is `fulfilled` or `impossible` there, within the new `[placement] qos_fulfil_timeout_s` (120 s), or the reset fails with nothing deleted; it is then deleted, and a deletion barrier holds until every provider's summary of its entry no longer lists it and its record answers that it is not there, within 60 s (`resetremove.BARRIER_BOUND_S`), before any file is settled or evicted, since a deletion cancels the replication only on the provider that processes it, best-effort, the replicating provider goes on until dbsync brings it the deletion, and an eviction it processes before then is cancelled for every file the requirement still holds there. `pending` does not prove that bytes are moving. The qos stage's record gives `fulfil_s`, `fulfil_polls`, `impossible`, `barrier_s` and `barrier_polls`. Two stages are new, each with its seconds: `qos_settle`, after the removal, waits for the removed requirements' files' block maps to stay unchanged 3 s within 60 s, since a QoS replication creates no transfer to wait for; `quiet`, after the occupancy, reads at one instant T the block maps of the eviction plan's files and of the removed requirements' files, each held to what the eviction (nothing, where it emptied them) or the settle left, waits the space root's longest occupancy lag for the deepest file (10 s plus 5 s per directory level above the file's parent, from the source) plus 5 s from T, and reads the occupancy. **What it proves, exactly: no replica of the plan's files, and no occupancy growth, had landed by T.** A landing after T is not certified: a replication still running at T can place its bytes later, may not show in the occupancy read, and the stage then passes. **The quiet check does not wait for a requirement to be found.** **The residual:** a fulfilment whose cancel missed may still be replicating one file, with no requirement left and no transfer document to show it: one of a requirement the agent added and removed during the run, or of one the reset removed after a reconciliation restarted between its fulfilled status and its deletion reaching the replicating provider. It is at most one file's size per missed cancel, and rare, since it needs a missed cancel; it may land after T, and is then caught by the next start's reset. `run(qos_rights=)` answers *could any run since the last verified reset have set a QoS requirement*: a study's reset runs at the start of each run and clears what earlier runs left, so it is not the rights of the run about to start (a read-only run that follows a writing run would skip the check exactly when it is needed). `True` or `None` (the default, fail safe) runs the quiet stage with no requirement left; pass `False` only when you know no run since the last verified reset had the right to manage QoS. `Condition.manages_qos` describes one condition, so a caller combines it over every run since the last verified reset. The stage reads the occupancy after the wait, whenever it runs, an empty plan included, and the block maps of the eviction plan's own files (the files the reset evicts from a provider other than the data provider, listed by the eviction itself) as well as the removed requirements' files, and records why it ran (`requirements`, `rights` or `default`), the size and source of the files it read (`held_files`, `held_source` `"eviction plan"`), the block maps read (`block_maps`) and its seconds. A replication that lands before T on a file the plan does not cover has no block map read, and only the occupancy catches it. Only `qos_rights=False` with no requirement removed skips the stage; `qos_settle` stays keyed on the affected files. The cost is the wait, 20 s for files one directory below the root (5 s more per further level), and one block-map read per evicted (file, provider) pair of the plan, once: bounded by the eviction plan, never by the size of the space (a 1,500-file space beside six evicted files took 1,544 block-map reads in the whole reset, 1,506 of them before the eviction, and now takes 18). `STAGES` now holds seven names, and the QoS stage's record gives `scan`, `kept`, `affected_files` and `reads` in place of `summaries_read`. `FakeData` models QoS requirements for these tests (#206): add, get, delete, summary, the recursive listing, and a replication that lands with no transfer; through `FakeQos`, an addition's and a removal's dbsync delay to the other providers, `pending` until the replication has landed, a replication that goes on landing on a provider until the removal reaches it, and a cancel that misses one file; `FakeReset`'s own model, including `qos_spawns`, is gone.
- **`[placement] qos_scan` defaults to `"listing"` (O33).** The reset finds a space's QoS requirements by one recursive listing of the space root on every provider that supports it (about one read per 1,000 entries per provider) instead of a summary per leaf on every provider (about 52,000 reads for a large tree); `"summaries"` is still accepted. The 14 Nov 2026 live slot (`deploy/compose-federation/day0/2026-11-14-federation-facts-slot.md`, the 25.0 development build, op-worker `f118ed9`) showed that the recursive listing takes `include_directories` (1,210 entries in 2 pages of 1,000) and that a requirement added through the mirror was found by the data provider's listing (`effQosInheritancePath` marking the entry) after 3.89 s, two polls, measured by hand since the gated test's own POST reached the mirror before it knew the file. Not measured separately: that the provider a requirement is added through shows it in its own listing at once, the premise for listing every provider; the quiet stage catches a replication's effect regardless, apart from its documented residual. **No store is affected:** `qos_scan` is in neither the platform's fingerprint (`PlatformConfig.fingerprint` reads nothing of `[placement]`) nor the campaign's, so a study that does not declare it resumes a store it started under `"summaries"`, and one that declares it can change it between runs without a refusal (a test pins this). A reset's record names the scan it ran (`stages.qos.scan`), and its `reads` the listing's keys (`listings`, `listing_pages`, `summaries`, `records`). A study that wants the old reads declares `qos_scan = "summaries"`. A resume under another effective value is not refused but is said: a study that passes `Placement.reset_settings()` to its plugin has it recorded and compared (C35, above).
- **Breaking: `[sandbox.mount]` must declare `io_mode`, and a store started before it cannot resume (O36).** `io_mode` is required, `"proxy"`, with no default: a platform file whose mount table has none is refused at load, naming the choices and why, and so is `extra_args` that carries a force-I/O option (`--force-proxy-io`, `--force-direct-io`, or an abbreviation), the TLS flag, the config-file option (`--config`, `-c` or an abbreviation) or an `ONECLIENT_...` assignment. `"direct"` loads only with the storage mapping and the check file of O40 (under Added). Before, an unset `io_mode` passed neither option and left the storage's access type to oneclient's own detection; no word stands for that now. Add `io_mode = "proxy"` to the table, which is what a sandbox needs where the sidecar cannot reach the providers' storage. `MountSettings.io_mode` is a `str`, not `str | None`, and `MountSettings` raises `ValueError` for a mode that is not `"proxy"` or `"direct"` or an extra argument that sets what the core declares, where it is built and again where the command is made. **The I/O mode is in the campaign fingerprint, for each mount level** (`PlatformConfig.fingerprint`, from `data_path_record`: `{"kind": "mount", "io_mode": "proxy"}`), as a REST level's listing mode is: a resume under another mode is refused, naming `platform.data_paths.<level>.io_mode`. A store started before this release recorded its mount levels with no mode, so it is refused a resume too, with that leaf "absent when the campaign started": it ran under oneclient's own detection, which is a different experiment, so start a new store (the old runs' records are unchanged, `mount.io_mode` null). Each run's sandbox metadata records the effective mode in `mount.io_mode`, the options the sandbox itself passed in `mount.options` (now ending in `--config /dev/null`), the study's `extra_args` by option name and the SHA-256 of the list in `mount.extra_args`, where a word that starts with a dash and is not an option of oneclient is recorded as `<value>`, never verbatim, and the SHA-256 and path in the image of the oneclient configuration file the image carries in `mount.image_config` (`unread` when the sidecar could not hash it). The other oneclient options stay at the client's fixed defaults, listed with their source in `docs/interfaces.md` ("The mount's I/O mode").
- **The sidecar's oneclient reads only its argv and the token (O36).** The command passes `--config /dev/null`, so the image's own configuration file is never parsed (an empty file gives the parser no options), and `env -u` removes `ONECLIENT_OVERRIDE` and `ONECLIENT_CONFIG` beside `ONECLIENT_INSECURE`; `ONECLIENT_ACCESS_TOKEN` stays, since the token arrives that way. The sidecar's entrypoint prints the SHA-256 of the file oneclient's `--help` names as its default configuration file before oneclient starts, which the sandbox records without creating a container or copying a file out of the image. A gated live check (`tests/live/test_mount_spaces.py`) reads oneclient's own start lines and the names of its environment and asserts that each logged option says what the argv sets, or oneclient's default, and that no variable but the token's is one oneclient reads an option from.
- **Breaking for a study that lists instruments in an order its `finish` depends on: instruments finish in the reverse of their list (C31).** `start_instruments` still starts them in list order, and `finish_instruments` now calls their `finish` last listed first, so an instrument listed earlier finishes later: setup and teardown nest, and an instrument listed after another starts after it and finishes before it. Before, both ran in list order, so no instrument could start after another and finish before it, and platform probes listed after a progressive ingest or a fault injector read the state it had already restored. List the instrument whose `finish` undoes something (the injector, the ingest) before the probes that must read the state before the undo. A study that listed an instrument first so that it finishes first, one that stamps the end of the run, lists it last. Only the calls are reversed: the record keeps its instruments in list order, so the keys of `instruments` keep the order they had (what an instrument reads can differ, which is the change's point), a `finish` that raises is recorded as failed and the others still finish, and `at_first_turn` and the post-run `check`s stay in list order, the first listed instrument that refuses being the refusal the record keeps. The fault injector's `running` clears the provider when its block exits, so a study that wants its probes to read before the restore calls `finish_instruments` inside the block. No in-repo executor lists more than one instrument, so no example or shipped instrument changes.
- **Breaking for a study whose `[faults] journal` lives outside a federation root: such a platform file is refused at load (O35).** The fault journal's parent is the federation's root, where every link check now writes the federation's `link-check.json` (`expcore_onedata.linkrecord`), whichever platform file the check ran through. `load_faults` therefore refuses a `[faults]` table whose `journal`'s parent holds no `platform.toml`, naming the key `journal`, rather than writing the record somewhere no reader looks. Every platform file of one federation already had to name the same journal to share the federation's lock, so a derived platform file kept in another directory names `<root>/fault-journal` in full, never the template's relative `fault-journal`.
- **Breaking for a study that keeps a platform file with a relative `[faults] journal` beside its `study.toml`: such a file is refused at load (O35).** The Compose federation's `platform.toml` copied beside `study.toml` resolved its relative `journal` into the study directory, whose parent held that very copy, so the check above passed vacuously and the federation's lock, journal and link-check record landed in the study, not in the federation's root. `load_faults` now refuses a relative `journal` in a platform file whose directory holds `study.toml`, whatever the relative path says, naming the key `journal` and the form to write, `journal = "<absolute path of the federation's root>/fault-journal"`. A relative journal still loads in the federation's own root, where no `study.toml` lies beside the file, and in a file derived in any other directory that holds no `study.toml`. The HPC federation's rendered file already states it in full, so its shards are unaffected.

### Fixed

- **`shards.py plan` and `lines`, and `fleet.py plan` for the shards it assigns, refuse shards whose campaign fingerprints differ, before any line is printed** (O63; `deploy/hpc-federation/shardresume.py`, `shards.py`, `fleetplan.py`, its README). Each shard's campaign fingerprint holds its federation's platform settings that shape a run (the faults sanction and targets, the placement bounds, the data identity), so shards on federations that rendered different ones merged only into a refusal, after they had run. Each is now taken beside its own federation's `platform.toml` by the function `resume-line` uses (the manifest's Python, `-I -B`, on a private copy of the study), and the refusal names the shards by label and the first differing top-level key of the fingerprint, never a host, a path or a value; a `platform.toml` that cannot be read or a fingerprint that cannot be taken is refused too.
- **`up` launches a service once more when its launch failed at launch, and launches the providers 1 s apart (O57; live 17 Nov 2026, twice).** srun's fan-out on the login node could not find the nodes' addresses: the five provider steps, launched in the same second, were accounted `CANCELLED` with exit `0:116` within 12 s, their run logs empty, so `up` failed and the whole federation had to be torn down, although a minute later every node resolved. A service is now launched once more only when both proofs hold: sacct shows that exact step, by the id srun's client log names, as exactly `CANCELLED` with exit `0:116`, the client log shows its task launch failing for want of an address, and its run log is a file of zero bytes. A log with a single byte, a step sacct cannot show, a second failure and a step still listed at its bound are never retried. The retry and its proofs (`services.<name>.launch_retries`: the step, its id, sacct's state and exit code, the run log's size, and a class of failure, never a host name) are written to the state file before the launch and printed in `up`'s output; the stagger (`launch.stagger_s`, `hpcdriver.LAUNCH_STAGGER_S`) is recorded too. The retry waits `launch_retry_backoff_s` first (60 s, `hpcdriver.LAUNCH_RETRY_BACKOFF_S`, recorded as `launch.retry_backoff_s`), since the live nodes resolved again a minute after the failure, counted from each step's own End as the same sacct row prints it (providers that failed together wait one backoff in all; an End that cannot be read or believed takes the whole backoff from now, never zero), and the run log is read again after the wait: a byte in it cancels the retry. See `deploy/hpc-federation/DESIGN.md`, section 5.
- **A budget too small for a campaign sharded by model named none of the shards it could not pay for (C32).** The refusal of a `[budget] max_usd` below one run's reservation for every priced shard together gave the count and the figure but no shard and no model, so with one priced model nothing in it said which. It now also lists each priced shard with its model, as the refusals of a shard's own part of a budget always did; its figures are unchanged.
- **A preflight's served-model check ignored a model's declared fingerprints, so it flagged what the campaign accepts (B18, #214).** `expcore.models.served.judge` had no way to take the `accepted_fingerprints` a model declares, so replies from two of its declared deployments, which one provider served within one session, came back as a `mismatch` while the campaign took them as the model. `judge` now takes `accepted_fingerprints=` and judges membership by the rule the campaign's check applies, now one definition (`expcore.models.served.outside_accepted`): a reply from any declared member is accepted, an undeclared fingerprint is still a mismatch, a mixed reply counts only when the set lists each of its fingerprints, and a model without a set is judged as before. The set replaces the baseline, as it does in the campaign, so an `expected_fingerprint` given with it is ignored. A study's preflight passes `accepted_fingerprints=spec.accepted_fingerprints`; nothing else in this repository called `judge`.
- **A launch-time git read no longer rewrites the clone it reads (O81).** The tool server's pin check ran `git status` in its checkout, and the code identity runs it in the study's tree. Git rewrites `.git/index` on a fresh copy's first status and again after a tracked file's mtime changes, so the check wrote the thing it checked and a digest of a directory holding `.git` changed after the first launch, which a later shard would have refused. Every git call in `src/` now takes its environment from `expcore.provenance.gitobjects.git_env`, the process's own plus `GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0`: the same answer, clean or dirty and the commit, with the index left as it was. The pin check also reads the user's global and the system git configuration as empty, so a global `core.fsmonitor` program does not run in a launch check, and a clone that relied on a global `safe.directory` is refused. A scan test fails a new git call in `src/` that does not pass `git_env`.
- **A reset that used to proceed beside another holder of the federation now waits for it, or refuses** (O66). Every reset, full or shortcut, now holds the federation's reset lock (`federation.reset`) exclusively for its whole run: `SpaceReset.run` and `SpacesReset.run` take the hold themselves after checking the plans and before sending anything, or use the caller's live `exclusive()` section. Until now only a shortcut reset inside a section took it, and only for `[placement] reset_exclusive_wait_s` (60 s): past that wait it did the full reads anyway, recording `shortcut_unavailable`, while another run's agent, a tool or a health check acted on the federation; a plain `run()`, a reset with `reset_shortcut` off (the default), a section that found a dead holder (it let the hold go at once) and one entered while a worker of the run held a share took no hold at all. **The wait is now bounded by `[faults] admission_timeout_s`** (an hour by default) and stops on the admission's cancel (`Admission.cancel`, the campaign's); at the bound it is refused as `resetgrant.ResetLockBusy`, a `FederationBusy` of kind `federation_busy`, which costs the run no attempt, naming the holders by pid and status, with nothing sent. The shortcut's own fallbacks (`off`, `holder_gone`, `holders_unread`, `shares_retained`, a reading outside the section) still give the full reads, now under the hold; `shortcut_unavailable` is no longer given, and `reset_exclusive_wait_s` is accepted and bounds nothing. **Also refused now:** on a platform with `[faults]`, a reset in a context that holds no admission (`AdmissionRefused`, before anything is sent, its message naming the remedy: wrap a reset outside a campaign in `shared_admission(config, reason=<code>)`, as `docs/interfaces.md` and the study guide show, and reset in the run's own context, which `asyncio.to_thread` carries and a thread pool's `run_in_executor` does not), one under an admission whose owner has left, and a reset of several spaces of two federations (`ValueError`); the hold keeps a share of the run's admission and lasts as long as its last user, so an attempt cancelled while its reset runs in a thread, or a caller's section that ends first, lets no lock go until the reset ends; a hold that lapses all the same fails the stage it lapsed in. A worker of the run's own already at work under a share goes on (`shares_retained`), as before. **While its reset holds the lock, nothing of the run may start on the federation**, whatever `reset_shortcut` says: its sandbox and tool server (`require_admission`), a tool reusing its admission and a health check in its context are refused or deferred, as they were only inside a shortcut section before; start them after the reset. Each space's record gains `reset_lock` (the grant's facts: mode, why, `wait_s`, holders), and its `seconds` count from the hold. **What it costs:** shared runs on one federation that each reset no longer overlap their attempts, since a reset waits for every other shared holder (another run's whole attempt, a tool, a health check) to let go; a campaign with several workers on one federation serialises at its resets. The gated live tests that reset outside a campaign now take `shared_admission`.
- **Every verb relayed from a compute node cost about 30 s, whatever its own time (O58).** The relay's client (`expcore_onedata.fault_relay.RelayClient`) polled `answers/<id>.json` by path. Its first lookup, made before the relay had answered, left a negative entry on the node's NFS client, which kept failing every later lookup of the name until the directory's attributes expired (the default `acdirmin`, 30 s). Measured on a live HPC federation: the relay saw a request within 0.04-0.14 s and ran the verb in a median of 0.9 s, but the answer reached the client 20-29 s later (median 28.7 s). The client now lists `answers/` with `os.scandir` on every poll (an `opendir` revalidates the directory) and reads the file only once the listing shows its exact name, never a stat or an open of the path first. Its guarantees are unchanged (the answer's checks against the request's id, the relay's nonce and the format, the timeout, the withdrawal, the cleanup), and a listing that fails is no answer yet, as a missing file was. The relay already listed `requests/`, and the heartbeat is read once per admission and per try, so nothing else polled a name another host creates. Tested against a model of the node's negative lookup cache (`tests/support/relay_support.py`); a live federation has not yet re-measured the latency.
- **A provider's restart failed its identity check on a service that had not changed, because op_worker said its domain was null for a moment (O58).** `finish.sh` read `domain` from op_worker's `/configuration`, which op_worker answers from the provider record it reads over its Onezone connection, so right after its REST listener answers it says null until that connection is up; `jsonget` printed the JSON null as the word `null`, and the check compared the word with the recorded domain (live, 17 Nov 2026, a restart after a fault-injected stop: the check ran 1.9 s before op_worker's connection came up, and a second start, where the connection came first, passed). A first start in the same window would have recorded the word as the provider's domain, and every restart after it compared against that. Both starts now wait, inside the readiness budget `wait_ready` already had (1800 s, `HPCOP_READY_SECONDS`), for a domain that is neither null nor empty, then check as before: a different non-null domain still fails as a changed identity, and a domain that stays null fails as that, not as a change. `jsonget` prints nothing and exits 3 for a JSON null (or gives its `--default`), so no script takes the word for an id, a domain, a user id or a token: `finish-zone.sh` and `mint-registration-token.sh` fail on a null user id or token instead of recording or delivering the word. The wait lengthens the restarted provider's down time, which a live slot measures, so it is reported: a ready service's status carries `domain_wait_s` (the seconds its start waited, 0 when it did not), and the injector records it beside `ready_after_s` in the restore's `verified` step and the injection, optional and absent for a driver or a status that reports none (Compose has no such wait), so no record holds a 0 nobody measured and existing records are unchanged. A Compose restore reads no `domain` and compares no recorded identity (its readiness is a container's health and one authenticated read, and its identity is the container's, compared for change), so it needed no wait.
- What an HPC shard step killed outright leaves on its nodes, and what a cancelled service leaves, are removed; and no Python the HPC drivers launch writes bytecode (O57, O63; live slot 5). `down`'s sweep and the next shard step on a node run the Apptainer run reaper over the sandbox's work root (a dead `fuse.oneclient` mount unmounted, `fusermount3 -u` then `-uz`, shown gone by the mount table, before the killed run's directory and record go; a run it cannot clear fails the sweep and refuses the step), the sweep removes the empty work root, and it runs on every node a shard step ran on (W1, W2). A service whose step `down` cancelled at the stop's bound (an outright kill on the site) has the work directory the state recorded for it removed and shown gone, so `down` finishes in one pass; a cancel's exit status (225 on a het job, while it ended the step) no longer aborts the wait (K1). Every Python the driver and `shards.py` launch, on a node or the login node, runs with `-I -B`: a node step had written a `.pyc` into the core's interpreter under the account's shared home (B1). `out_snapshot.restore` now also refuses a snapshot whose store names an ended attempt whose marker its `runs/` lacks.
- **The HPC render has no `[placement]`, so a tool that read it had to supply a default of its own (O79).** The platform layer requires the table and the pilot's template declared none; the ingest tool of live slot B's prep patched in a default table, and defaults supplied separately by each tool are how a setting drifts unseen. The core has no default for the data provider, so a template declares `[placement] holder` (the pilot's now does, with a comment saying why) and `hpcfed.py up` refuses one that does not, naming the key, before anything is submitted. The render keeps what the template declares and writes the core's default for every other setting beside it, read from `PlacementSettings` (never a copy of the numbers). `load_placement` still refuses a file without the table, and `shards.py step` still copies the file as it is. `deploy/hpc-federation/README.md` and `DESIGN.md` (section 6) describe the render.
- **Charging changed: an injection an earlier attempt left open in the fault journal charges no run that meets it (O80).** The first run tried after an attempt left a failed restore or an open injection, in a pre-run check that closed the gate or in another attempt's post-run check, was charged an infrastructure attempt for the failure of the attempt that made it, which had been charged already. The injector now writes an `owner` on each `injecting` line of the fault journal (`liveness.owner`: the process, and inside an attempt its campaign and attempt id), and `SettledInjections` and the fault gate charge only the attempt that owns the entry; every other check, a pre-run check included, holds uncharged and says the failure was left by another attempt (the gate's record has `charged: false`). A line with no owner, or one naming a process alone (a heal's container restart), is charged as before and says so. **A study that saw such a run charged sees it held instead; the attempt that made the injection is charged as before.** `Health.charge` keeps its default (`True`): a study's own marker-based health check must answer `charge=False` for a marker an earlier attempt left or compare its owner as `ingesthealth` does (`docs/interfaces.md`, the health check). `owner` is an optional field of a journal line, not of any run record.
- **Charging changed: an attempt whose executor raises after its model loop is charged what its call log measured, not its whole bound (C05).** A study's post-run guard (a leak found in a tool answer, say) raised after every request was answered, and the attempt was recorded with unknown usage and charged its bound, though its call log held each reply's counts. The recording now tells the attempt's spend guard when the loop returns (`expcore.models.spend.after_loop`, `AttemptSpend.loop_ended`), and `expcore.campaign.after_loop.settle_usage` gives an attempt that raises after that point the usage its log measured, `usage_complete` true, and `spend_basis` `measured` on its row and in its marker. The log must hold at least one request and every request in it closed with its counts (or by a failed last send, which its top-up pays for); a log that is unreadable, empty, or has a request with no end entry, a reply not read or a count missing leaves the usage unknown, charges the bound as before and records `bound`. A raise during the loop or before it, and any attempt without a budget (no guard, no log), keep their usage unknown and their bound, with no basis. **A study that ran such attempts sees a lower recorded charge for them, and a done run whose earlier attempt raised so now counts that attempt in its measured cost; rows already stored are not rewritten.** The marker's `spend_basis` is an optional field of schema 3, written only when set; no run record field changes.
- **The data gate reused an upstream connection after a request that carried a body, and the next request on it can stall (O85; `sandbox/gate.py`).** Measured on 17 Nov 2026 directly against two Onedata providers, with Python's `http.client` (the gate's own library) and no `Expect` header, not through the gate: after a request with a body of about 1.5 MiB (a PUT), the next request on the same kept connection (a GET, or the next POST) stalled in about half the tries until the client's timeout, while a new connection answered in milliseconds; 400 KiB and 4 MiB bodies never stalled the next request. The gate waits on an upstream for up to 14,400 s, so one request could have hung for hours. The gate now closes the upstream connection once the answer to a request that carried a body is read, whatever the status, and the next request opens a new one; the line says `upstream_closed` `"after_body"`. Only reuse changes: the request is sent once, and no retry, status, answer or timeout is different; a request without a body keeps its connection. The gate relays no write and refuses a body over 1 MiB, so a body of 1.5 MiB never reaches an upstream through it: the rule covers the bodies it does relay (the lookup and scope POSTs, an allowed call that declares one, each page of a complete listing that has one) and costs a connect and a handshake for the request after each. Docker and Apptainer run the same gate. Whether a body of 1 MiB or less can stall the platform is not measured.
- **The tool server could read a `.env` file nothing in its launch named (O84).** onedata-mcp reads a `.env` when its config module is imported, searching from its installed package up to `/`, so a `.env` in any ancestor of the package (on a cluster account, a home directory several studies share) could set any `ONEDATA_*` setting the launch left unset, `ONEDATA_MCP_NODE_NAMES` among them. `toolserver.server_environment` now gives the server `ONEDATA_MCP_DOTENV=0` on both platforms, the HPC launcher's included (a managed setting; onedata-mcp reads no `.env` given it), and the server starts in a new, empty, mode 0700 directory of its own for each start, removed when it stops, which also covers the `.env` FastMCP reads from the working directory. A relative path in `[toolserver] command` or `args` no longer resolves against the driver's working directory, and the pinned server must be an onedata-mcp commit that carries the guard (`docs/interfaces.md`, `docs/study-guide.md`).
- **A manifest could be frozen from a server the launch would not start (O84).** `python -m expcore.tools.manifest freeze` and `check` started the tool server outside the launch, with only the variables `--pass-env` named and in the driver's working directory, so a manifest (hashed into the campaign fingerprint) could freeze the tools another allow-list, host or `.env` showed, which no run saw. The new `python -m expcore_onedata.toolmanifest freeze|check PLATFORM CONDITION` starts the server from `toolserver.server_start`, the function the launch uses (the command and arguments, `server_environment` with the managed `ONEDATA_MCP_DOTENV=0`, the private CA copy, and a new, empty, mode 0700 directory for each start), writes the condition's declared manifest, verifies the pinned checkout first, as the launch does, refusing a clone at another commit or with a local change, and refuses `--pass-env` of a variable the launch sets itself; `load_platform` gains `require_manifests`, which only this command turns off, so one condition is frozen or checked before the others are frozen. The core command line keeps its form for servers a study starts itself and now starts them in such a directory too (`expcore.tools.workdir`). A study that froze the Onedata server's manifest with the core command line freezes it again with the new one: if the tools are the same nothing changes, and if not the condition's hash in the campaign fingerprint does, so a store started with the earlier manifest is not resumed with it. The server's environment also holds the managed `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1`, on both platforms: a platform without the resolve launcher started the server with neither `-B` nor the variable, and a `check` of its manifest (as a launch) wrote `.pyc` files into the clone, the virtualenv and the interpreter's library (one measured start wrote 22 into the clone and 1,083 into the virtualenv), which changes the digest of a study's frozen artifacts; a caller can neither set nor clear it, and `--pass-env` refuses it (`docs/interfaces.md`, `docs/study-guide.md`).
- On an HPC batch node a cell process that made itself non-dumpable (`prctl(PR_SET_DUMPABLE, 0)`) was hidden from the Apptainer backend's memory watch by `/proc`'s `hidepid=invisible`, so a run's record read as within its memory limit when it was not (O56; the HPC probes of 16 Nov 2026: such a process held 768 MiB under a 256 MiB limit while the watch saw a 12.7 MB peak and never killed). The watch now compares, at every poll, the anonymous memory the driver's own cgroup charges (`memory.stat` `anon`, which counts the hidden process) with the RssAnon of every process of the uid `/proc` shows in that cgroup (`apptainer/hidden.py`). Memory nothing visible explains that grows past `[sandbox.apptainer] hidden_memory_margin_bytes` (default 128 MiB) during a cell for two polls is taken as past the limit, as an unwalkable directory is taken as past its size: the cell is killed as `memory_limit` (`memory_hidden`). Memory that cannot be told the cell's (another run's cell in the cgroup, or a margin the start's own cells passed) makes the run's memory reading `unknown`, never a kill. A child that a visible cell process's `children` files list and `/proc` does not show is a `hidden_process`, recorded and never a kill; a double fork under the cell's hidden init escapes it, so it supplements the cgroup's check. Each run's `infrastructure_facts` holds the reading (`memory`: `within`, `over` or `unknown` with its reasons and figures), `within` only when the check held. The new instrument `expcore.visibility.ProcessVisibility(sandbox)` (namespace `process_visibility`) puts it in the record, and the report gains `## Process visibility (detected, not prevented)`: a run with a hidden process or hidden memory is flagged, one with an unknown reading is unknown and its limit condition unknown, and the analysis is run again without them (`analysis.json` `process_visibility`, the terminal's `process visibility` lines). Also: every cell binds a `resolv.conf` naming no name server and an `nsswitch.conf` whose hosts are files alone, and every run records `dns: none` (facts `dns_none`, `hidden_memory`); a completion cause on this backend names `apptainer exec` (`Completion.runner`); and the bundle's notes say an image without gcc takes shims built with the node's.
- **An Apptainer run's `infrastructure_facts` listed `mount_refused` for every run, a mount run included (O56, O60).** The key said a mount data path was refused until the mount's helper landed, and the helper has (hook c, `apptainer.hostmount`; #318's guard, #333's oneclient bundle). It is now listed only for a run whose sandbox refuses a mount (it is given no helper, or the platform declares no `[sandbox.mount]`), so a mount run never carries it and a sandbox that accepts one lists it for no run. Paths that still refuse a mount, so the key stays: no helper or no `[sandbox.mount]` (`MOUNT_REFUSED`, which now says what the sandbox lacks) and, at the helper's start, a direct mount: **HPC mounts are proxy-only until the direct-I/O comparison passes** (the study guide and `docs/storage-import.md` say so). `facts.record` takes `mount_refused` as a required keyword argument. Reading code that keyed on the key's presence in every Apptainer record should read the run's `data_path` and its `mount` metadata instead.
- **`down` could not stop the fault relay on a login node whose kernel has no `pidfd_open` (O58).** `fault_relay stop` signals the relay through a pidfd, and the raw call answered ENOSYS there, so the stop exited 1; `down` then ended the relay as a recorded client, but had already concluded it was not verified clean, and failed with "the held job is gone, but the fault relay could not be verified clean" on every run while a relay ran (a second `down` finished). On ENOSYS the stop now reads the relay's start tick and sends the signal with `os.kill` only while it still matches the heartbeat's (the pattern `end_client` follows), so a pid another process took is never signalled, and no other failure of the pidfd is worked round; a pid of 1 or below and a missing start tick are never signalled. On either path the stop also signals only a process whose command line holds `-m expcore_onedata.fault_relay serve --root <this root>` as consecutive arguments, in order: a heartbeat is a file, and a start tick that matches does not make another process of the user's, or another federation's relay, this root's relay. `down` checks the relay again after it has ended the recorded clients, and reports it unverified only if that second stop still shows a heartbeat. Without a pidfd the signal is not held across the check, so a pid reused in the instant between the read and the kill is not caught; a relay killed outright leaves a heartbeat that stays fresh for up to 30 s, and a `down` in that window still says unverified.
- **What the first live slot of the HPC federation found (O57).**
