  - **The zone's CA reaches the root as its certificates alone.** The image's test CA file holds the CA's private key before its certificate, and the core refuses a key in `ca_file`, so every `up` failed in the core's phase. `up` now keeps only the PEM `CERTIFICATE` blocks of the export (`deploy/hpc-federation/hpcca.py`), and refuses an export with no certificate or with a block that is not whole. The core's refusal stays as the backstop. The CA stays the image's own, since its digest is in the campaign fingerprint.
  - **Every step of a heterogeneous job names its component.** The site's Slurm refuses a step of a heterogeneous job with no `--het-group`, component 0 included, so `up` failed at its first step. A step on a service node now names `--het-group=0`, and a job of one component names none. The test fake refuses such a step as the site does.
  - **A step of the agent's component is found under that component's own id.** `squeue -s` lists it there alone, never under the leader's id, so the launcher's lookup by name and `down`'s wait for every step missed every step on the agent's node. Both now ask each id, and `down` cancels a step still listed there by its own id.
  - **A failed node facts step says why.** The error carries the step's status and the end of its stderr on one line.
  - **The default `HPC_STEP_PATH` holds `/usr/local/sbin:/usr/sbin`,** where the site's nodes keep `ss`; the zone's step failed in its first second without it. The bundle checks its tools up front (`lib.sh`'s `need_tools`), naming each missing one, and a missing or failing `ss` is an error in every reading of a node's listeners, never an empty listing read as none (a check counted no listener that way, a false pass).
  - **`down` removes its root on NFS.** Its lifecycle lock held inside the root became a silly-rename file, so the root was not empty, and a second `down` refused the unmarked root. The lock is now `<root>.lifecycle.lock` beside the root, never removed; the removal waits up to 30 s for silly-rename files, removes the marker last, and a second `down` finishes a removal cut short.
  - **A service that died before naming its work directory can be shown clean.** Its step must be shown ended, and a read-only check on its node (`bundle/leftovers.py`) must count no work directory of ours of its kind; otherwise it stays unverified.
  - **No step can fall back to the account's home,** which every job and agent of the account shares: every launcher step and shard step names `HOME`, the XDG directories, Apptainer's, uv's and pip's directories below `/dev/null`, a path nobody can make (a tool then fails rather than read what another user planted on the shared `/tmp`), and every bundle script that knows its work directory (`status.sh` and `exec.sh` among them) and a shard's dispatcher point them into their own 0700 directory on the node's tmpfs before any tool that reads one.
- **What the second live slot of the HPC federation found (O57): a site setting for a step's network option.** On a site whose switch plugin gives each job a virtual network identifier, every new step on component 0 of a heterogeneous job failed with `srun: error: task 0 launch failed: Error configuring interconnect` after the zone and five providers had started, so `up` failed at the first provider's registration; `--network=single_node_vni` failed the same way, `--network=no_vni` ran, a step on component 1 ran, and the same steps ran in a job of one component in two earlier runs. The cause (a count of steps per component, or exhausted identifiers) is not established. `site.env` takes an optional `HPC_STEP_NETWORK` (empty by default, so no step's argv changes): its value goes to every step the driver launches (`up`, `down` and the service verbs) as `srun --network=<value>`, and is recorded in the state file with the rest of the site. The value is one option string of letters, digits and `_ . , = : + -` (at most 128), refused with the variable named and the value not repeated, when `site.env` is read, when the state's record is read (a value that is no string is refused, never read as none) and when the argv is made. `sandbox_placement = "separate"` is documented as the placement to start from on such a site; no default changes. The line `shards.py lines` prints for a shard step does not carry the option. Unproven (live only): a whole `up` with either setting, and a step on component 1 with `no_vni`.
- `merge` and the check of a merged batch refused a side file a kill had left under its temporary name, as missing, where `check` run on the shard first would have finished its rename (#304, R15). A study that declares side files and merges shards copied back from other hosts, with a run killed between its record and the rename of its side file, could not merge them. `merge` now copies each shard's `runs/` into a private staging directory beside `OUT`, settles the copy as `check` does (the rename a record names is finished, a partial file no record names is dropped from the copy, each said on stderr as `merge: shard <i> (<label>): ...`), replays it, and moves the copies that passed into the batch, so what was replayed is what the batch holds and **a shard's `out/` is never written to, whether the merge succeeds or is refused**; a shard fetched read-only is merged as it is, and an `OUT` inside a shard's `out/` is refused before anything is made. `check` of a merged batch finishes a rename a record names in the batch too, without a lock file and without removing a file no record names. A directory that cannot be written is reported, never written to: `settle_side_files` returns what it could not do as `Settled(..., left=True)`. A temporary file of other bytes than its record's is never renamed, so it still fails the replay as before.
- The study guide said a shard's release step and release file are the host's own (#154). `campaign.toml` takes neither, so a budget hold of a study's campaign does not end. The guide now says what a shard whose share runs out does (it ends with its runs pending, exit status 3; the merge takes it and the analysis counts those runs as unfinished under it), that an attempt a kill interrupts is charged its run's bound against the shard's share, and that a `max_usd` a little above what the whole plan reserves gives every shard a part of `max_usd` that pays the reservation of every run it plans once (a `per_model_usd` ceiling, admission's probes, top-ups, retries and interrupted attempts aside). The rehearsal of a campaign of 4 models as 27 and as 21 shards (`tests/e2e/test_shard_scale.py`) shows the 27 shards merged give one driver's scores and analysis byte for byte, and the faults of a fleet of hosts handled at the merge.
- `check`, `merge` and `run --after` wrote beside a finished campaign store and failed on a read-only `out/` (R16). The store stays in WAL mode, and they opened it in place over a read-only connection, which makes `state.sqlite-shm` and `state.sqlite-wal` beside it and cannot remove them, and fails with `attempt to write a readonly database` where the directory is read-only, as shards copied back from other hosts may be. `--after` also made a `campaign.lock` beside whatever file it was given, a package's sealed snapshot included, which the package's checksums then refused. A finished store is now read from a private copy (`campaign.storecopy`): its file and its `-wal`, never its `-shm`, each signed before and after the copy by device, inode, size, modification and change times and the SHA-256 of its contents, with the bytes copied required to hash to both (file times move only on the kernel's coarse tick, tmpfs included, so a write in place can leave the rest unchanged), copied again on a change and refused, naming the file, after three attempts that each saw one; the copy is opened read-write in a temporary directory, so the `-wal` replays, and removed after. All three hold the store's lock as a reader (`lock.ReaderLock`) across the copy: the existing lock file opened read-only, never created, under a shared non-blocking `flock`, which the dispatcher's exclusive one refuses. A store a dispatcher holds is refused by `merge` and `--after`, and by `check` unless `--partial`, which backs the live store up in place, as before, since no copy of a store being written is one state. A store whose lock cannot be held is refused by all three, `--partial` included, naming the lock file: one missing ("no dispatcher ever ran there": copy `out/` whole), unreadable, or on a file system without `flock`. The merge now only reads a shard's `out/`, as the study guide says (`docs/interfaces.md`, "How a reader opens a store").
- **A connection failure on one of a reset's per-file reads no longer fails the whole reset (O29, O46, O51).** A live reset read 8,290 block maps at one provider, then one `GET` failed to connect: the map was unknown and the reset failed closed, 1,238 s lost, because the plumbing policy retried only timeouts. Every reading of the reset (the occupancy and quiet stages with the occupancy's counter, the shortcut's narrowed reads, the settle, the quiet stage's maps, the QoS scans with `summaries` mode's directory listings, and `SpaceReset.plan_scan`) now reads under a policy made for it, `Provisioner.reading_retry()`, and so do each stage's own transfer reads (listings, records, the waits for its jobs, the eviction stage's included, Onezone's clock), under one per stage tallied as `transfer_read_retry`: four attempts with pauses of 2, 5 and 15 s, a connect, read or write error or a remote protocol error tried again on a fresh connection as a timeout is, within the occupancy's `READ_BOUND_S`. An answer still decides at once (a 404 or a POSIX absence counts 0; any other error status, a failed storage entry or an error answer cut in its body fails; only a 200 cut short is asked again, its retry's whole answer deciding), and a read still failing after its fourth attempt fails the reading closed; every other call site keeps timeouts only (the next entry takes the reset's gate, listings, visibility wait, `verify_space` and root read under its policy). Each reading records its tally (`read_retry`, the quiet stage's maps `maps_read_retry`, `Holdings.facts()["read_retry"]`): reads, retries, failed attempts by error type, the reads that failed for good and the answers that were no plain success, per provider, a failed stage's included. `[placement] provider_concurrency` (16) bounds every read of a reset's readings in flight at one provider across spaces read at once (four spaces of 16 reads each put 64 at one shared provider), and the client's own pool keeps alive every connection it may open (100, past httpx's default 20), so a reading reuses its connections instead of reconnecting.
- **The reset's gate reads, its eviction listings and visibility wait, `verify_space` and the space root's first read retry a connection failure too, and a mutation whose answer was lost is never sent again (O09, O29, O46).** The previous fix left these timeouts-only, and the gate reads before every cancel, eviction and QoS deletion: one `ConnectError` in a large eviction's thousands of gates failed the reset. Each now reads under the policy of the stage it is made in (`Provisioner.mutate(gate_retry=)`, `schedule_transfer(gate_retry=)`, `verify_space(retry=)`, `root(retry=)`, `resolve_many(retry=)`, `walk(retry=)`, `await_visible(retry=)`, which only the reset passes), tallied in that stage's record, the driver's `verify_space` as the record's `verify_read_retry`; the listings, the root and the visibility wait's two reads per file before each eviction hold a provider slot. The gate still fails closed: each attempt decides from its own answer, and after the fourth failed read nothing is sent (`ScopeUnread`). The mutation is sent once: a cancel, an eviction or a QoS deletion whose answer is lost after it went out (`client.sent_unanswered`) may have been applied, so it is not sent again; the job's record, the block maps or the deletion barrier decide, failing closed at their bounds, and the stage records `unanswered`. No read a reset makes is left on timeouts only.
- The tool server's check threads took no share of the run's federation admission (O11). The checks before the spawn (`_prepare`) and after the stop (`_recheck`) read Onezone in worker threads, and a launch cancelled while one ran stopped waiting for it, so its run could leave its admission while the thread still read Onezone for a launch already cancelled (the result is discarded). The effect was nil in practice, but it broke the guarantee that nothing of a run acts on the federation after its admission ends. Each check thread now takes a share of the admission before it starts (`expcore_onedata.admission_share.ThreadShare`, the share the sandbox's start thread already kept, now one class, taken beside the thread's count as the tool server's step in `toolserver._in_counted_thread`; a check whose share cannot be taken is refused, as one without the run's admission is) and gives it up when it ends, on success, on an error and on a cancellation; a thread that never began has its share given up for it. A platform without `[faults]` has no admission and is unchanged.
- The default `[faults] heal_timeout_s` was refused by the loader's own check for a common federation size (O35). It was a fixed 4,200 s, and the load check refuses a `heal_timeout_s` too small for one round of the declared mode's steps, which with the other defaults and the container fallback is 3,780 s for four providers and 4,290 s for five, so a five-provider study that declared `heal = "all_links"` and left the bound at its default could not load. An undeclared `heal_timeout_s` is now derived from the same computation the check uses (`fault_settings.heal_round_s`, through `default_heal_timeout_s`): that least for the declared providers, mode and bounds, plus 10 %, rounded up to a whole minute, so the two cannot drift. With the other defaults `"all_links"` derives 4,200 s for four providers (unchanged) and 4,740 s for five, and 2,460 s and 3,060 s without the container fallback; with the heal off there is no bound (`heal_timeout_s` is 0, and nothing read it). A declared value is kept and still refused below the least, and the refusal now names the value declared as well as the least. `heal_token_margin_s`, the long margin with which a heal runs while a step of the run that uses its tokens still runs, counts the effective bound, so it follows it (11,934 s for five providers in `"all_links"`, 10,854 s for four); `margin_s` itself stays the study's to declare, at least `restore_token_margin_s`. `DEFAULT_HEAL_TIMEOUT_S` is removed.
- **A declared heal no longer makes every run's token outlive its ceiling by about three hours (O35, O06, O11).** The load rule of the heal demanded `[tokens] margin_s` of 900 s plus two heals and a drain, though a heal endangers the token only while a step that examines it after the heal still runs. The restore now always runs, as its container start and its session gate use no run token, and **a token's lifetime is now the study's explicit choice**. A platform with `[faults]` must declare `[tokens] margin_s`, at least what the restore, its gate and the session drain need (`restore_token_margin_s`, 2,134 s with the defaults). The drain counts because a clear that holds no exclusive admission takes the federation itself and waits out a drain record still standing before it restores (`session_drain_s`), and its own drain after the restore adds what outlasts the restore's bound; `session_drain_s = 0` adds neither. A missing margin and a short one are both refused at load, naming the least figure, its derivation term by term from the declared `[faults]` values, the drain's terms included, and the line to write (`[tokens] margin_s = 2134`). No default is derived, and a platform without `[faults]` is unchanged. **Breaking for every platform.toml with `[faults]` that declares no margin, or the 900 s default:** add the line the refusal names. The heal is checked at its own start: the tool server's launch (to the end of its stop's re-verification) and the sandbox's (to the end of its stop) count themselves on the run's admission, worker threads included, and with one still running and no long margin declared (`heal_token_margin_s`) the heal is skipped, recorded in the injector's reading with the steps and both margins, and a stuck pair leaves the restore unverified, never scored. **In the documented order, where the launches run until after the restore, a stuck pair is then healed only by a start with no step of its run running, by the long margin, or by hand,** and until then the health gate, whose recovery never heals, holds the campaign. An executor that stops its sandbox and tool server before `running`'s exit heals with the default margin.
- **The token's window holds one restore, and every step that uses a token runs under the run's admission (O35, O11).** Three gaps were left in the token gating. A token step that began without a run admission counted nothing, so the heal-skip could not see it; an injector that took an admission of its own could wait for the lock after its run minted a token; and when the clear at `running`'s exit failed, `finish` tried the restore again while the tool server still ran, putting two restores before the token check, which the least margin does not count (it broke when `ready_timeout_s` or `gate_timeout_s` dominated). On a platform with `[faults]`, the tool server's two checks and the sandbox's mint and start now start only under the run's admission held then, and are otherwise refused before they start (`admission.TokenStepRefused`, naming the step), failing closed. A failed restore is now tried again only when no step of the run that uses its tokens still runs, decided at once and never waited for: in an executor that stops its sandbox and tool server first, `finish` retries and the provider is back. While a step still runs, as at `finish` in the documented order, the injection is handed to recovery instead: it stays open in the journal, the run is set aside as `injector_failure` and never scored (#245), a later `clear` reports it still down, and its target's lock goes with the run's admission (an admission's endings now all run when one is interrupted), so nothing of the run restores it inside the window; then the next start's recovery, or the health gate's, restores it. The reading's `restore_retry` step records the path (`after_steps` or `left_open`), why, and the steps that ran. The least margin is unchanged, 2,134 s with the defaults: it counts one restore, which is now exact. A run whose admission ends while its tool server still runs now meets a harness defect at the stop's check, which ran uncounted before; launches outside the admission were refused already.
- The reset's occupancy stage trusted the space's directory-statistics counter alone (O46). After a good eviction the space root went on crediting the mirror 1,048,576 B that no file held, and the reset failed with "hold(s) bytes of the space beyond what the reset allows" and no figure; the counter also under-counts, reading 0 for 9.534 s while a placement of 1,572,864 B was held. The occupancy stage and the quiet stage now read every regular file's and hard link's block map, as any provider's listing showed them, at each provider other than the data provider, whatever the counter says, and judge the sum (a provider no declaration fits that supports the space is refused); the counter skips only a provider the caller declares never holds the space's bytes (`SpaceReset.run(never_holds=)`) while it reads 0. A refusal states the bytes held, the figure allowed and the counter, per provider and by file position; each disagreement is an `occupancy_counter_disagrees` event in the stage's record; the reads are bounded (`occupancy.READS_MAX`, `occupancy.READ_BOUND_S`) and fail closed; and a block map whose provider or storage entry is marked failed now reads as unknown, never as empty.
- The link check and a stuck restore named the wrong provider to restart (O35). Since the check tests every ordered (reading provider, source) pair, the provider whose rtransfer link is stuck is the pair's reader, which is not always the platform's local provider; two texts still spoke as if it were, and a study misread which provider to restart. The `links_stuck` failure of a fault injector's recovery now says to restart the rtransfer link on the reading provider each failed pair names (or that provider's container alone, one at a time, re-checking every pair after each) and carries the link check's detail, which names each failed pair by position. `PairResult.local` and the record's `"local"` key meant the pair's reader since the check tested every pair; the field is now `PairResult.reader` and the record has a `"reader"` key with the same value, with `local` kept in both as a deprecated alias for records and code that read it (an attribute and a key; a `PairResult` is built with `reader`, positionally or by keyword, and `local=` is no longer a keyword). The `{local}` placeholder of `linkcheck.REMEDY` is `{reader}` (a study that formats it passes `reader=`). Neither `PairResult` nor `REMEDY` has been in a release, so this renames them before any study could depend on the old spelling. The failure of a pair, "no transfer from providers[j] to providers[i] within N s" and its remedy, is unchanged in meaning: it names the reader.
- A progressive ingest's snapshot was bound to its plan by the files' labels alone (#262, O27, O44). The first study's record checker matched a run's snapshot to its arrival plan by the arriving files' labels, so a plan with the same files on another schedule (every part 300 s later) passed, and an analysis reading arrivals relative to the plan could pair a run with the wrong plan unnoticed. The core keeps no such reader, so a study's checker (its `recorded_arrival`) calls the new `expcore_onedata.ingestbind.check_recorded_plan(plan, snapshot)`, which binds by content and raises `PlanMismatch` (`.label`, `.field`) for any difference: the plan's hash (`snapshot["plan"]["sha256"]`, which the snapshot has recorded since its first version, so no field is added), the mode, each file's `bytes`, `sha256`, `at_s`, `parts`, `over_s`, `baseline` and `prefix`, and each reading's part, part count, due time and end byte, with every part planned once the start set its state. A snapshot that records no plan, no hash, a file's field or no steps is refused as unverifiable, never passed; one from before the mode existed reads as an arrival snapshot and binds to its arrival plan alone.
- A provider outage could be scored as the treatment without checking which provider it was (C34). A study cut its schema to one condition by rebuilding it with the treatment kind names alone, which dropped the outage condition's target (`"providers[1]"`); `Outcome.is_treatment` then counted any provider's outage as the treatment without asking the injector, and the store compared the record to the cut schema, so nothing caught it. A kind that is a fault on one target is now registered with `register_infra_kind(..., requires_target=True)` (`targeted_kinds`; the Onedata layer's `provider_outage` is the one, and the core holds that name to a target whoever registers it, a study's own kind of that name in `[study.kinds]` included), and the factor schema refuses it declared by its plain name, in `study.toml` and in code, naming the file, the condition and the kind: write `{ kind = "provider_outage", target = "providers[1]" }`. No targetless form is offered (nothing in the repository declared one, and the injector needs the target to inject). `Outcome.is_treatment` scores such a kind only when the failure names the declared target and the injector's snapshot shows that target held down in the attempt, and never without a declared target, whatever rule the record's version has. `build_record` and `validate` refuse a record that declares the kind with no target, and an outage run's admission refuses to take the federation without one. A study that declared `provider_outage` by its plain name fails to load until it names the target, and a record that declared it so, which `check` read as treatment before, is refused. The record's version and the schema digest of a condition that names its target are unchanged.
- The per-call tool bound capped every agent code cell (#235, L17). The loop handed every tool call `min(tool_call_bound_s, the agent's time left)`, the code tool's included, and the code tool passed it to `sandbox.exec`, so a study that declared the bound to classify a tool server's hang also cut every cell at it and told the model "the code did not finish within the time limit". That changed what the agent experienced, which was never the intent: the bound exists so that a hung tool server is `knowledge` infrastructure and not the agent's time. It now applies to calls to a tool server (an MCP server) only. A code cell, and any non-MCP tool, is handed the agent's time left whatever is declared, its "did not finish" text is the one it has without the key, and a cell the loop cuts is never judged by the bound. Which tools the bound reached is in the record (`agent_config.tool_server_tools`).
- A run's bounded wait for the federation that ran out consumed an attempt (O11, C33). The wait's end was `FederationBusy` of kind `injector_failure`, which is charged, so a run waiting behind an outage run (its restore and heal hold the federation exclusively, over an hour with a heal) lost an attempt inside its attempt each time: the in-call admission (`FederationLock.admitted`), or an injector whose run holds no admission and waits for the federation itself, exhausted `max_attempts` with piled-up time-outs. It is now the hold `federation_busy` (`expcore_onedata.sandbox.kinds`, untreatable and `charged=False`, as `ingest_held`), and the injector raises it as it is instead of as its own failure. So is a wait that runs out while the federation stays closed for an outage run's session drain, whether the admission waited or the injector's start did after its journal read. The campaign's run admission already left such a run unstarted with no attempt used and asked again; it still does, and the refusal now has the hold's kind. A cancelled wait, a holder record that cannot be written and an unsettled injection under a shared admission stay `injector_failure`, and a real injector failure is charged as before. A start that waits behind an injector whose restore failed is the hold too: the failure is that run's own, in its snapshot, and the health gate fails for it. The fault gate's own wait (`FaultGate`, the pre-run check) had two charged paths too: a check made during an outage run's heal read the heal's marker (`clear_failed` with `healing`) as a failed restore and charged the run that found it, and a deferral behind a live holder that outlasted `max_defer_s` (as an outage run with a heal can) closed the gate charging that run. The marker as an injection's latest line is now a heal under way, contention, and contention answers `charge=False`, so past the bound the gate closes blocking and charges no one.
- The sandbox's teardown had no deadline, and a cell's output had no cap (#200, O42, O43). `SandboxPlatform` now waits for the stop of a sandbox at most `teardown_deadline_s` (600 s by default) and records how it ended under `teardown` in the sandbox's metadata: `completed`, `failed`, or `cut` with the phase and counts it had left; a cut is `InfraError("sandbox")`, infrastructure and never the agent's doing, and the key stays quarantined until the sandbox is shown gone. A command the host runs that does not end after its kill is given up on after 10 s instead of waited for for ever (`hostops.run_bounded`). A cell's stdout and stderr keep their first 1 MiB each (`cells.OUTPUT_CAP_BYTES`), the rest read and dropped with bounded memory; `ExecResult.output_cut` and the cell's record say how many bytes each cut stream wrote and kept, and the code tool ends its text with a notice of it and flags `source_truncated`; the agent's `output_cap_chars` then cuts that whole text last, from its end, so a cap below it removes the notice and the model sees the loop's own, bar two edges: a cap a few characters below the text leaves a piece of the notice, and a cap too short for the loop's notice cuts plain (the order, the edges and the exact strings are in the study guide and `interfaces.md`). `HostOps.docker` takes `output_cap`, and `CmdResult` has `stdout_count` and `stderr_count`; a `HostOps` of a study's own that does not take `output_cap` must accept the keyword.
- Large HTTP bodies stayed alive in httpx reference cycles until a full collection (#259, O47). httpx binds every answer to a stream that points back at it, so each answer, its request and both bodies lived until the cyclic collector ran: eight files of 8 MiB uploaded and read back on the public fake left 128 MiB alive, and a long batch or a re-ingest grew by everything it moved until a collection came, so failures were intermittent. `OnezoneClient` now releases every answer as soon as it is read, however the read ends (a body cut short and a failure inside httpx's own send included), on every path that sends or receives a body (its requests, the ranges of `read_content`, the head `content_answer` reads), keeps no answer, request or body, and raises a transport failure with its handler over, so a `PlatformError` has no httpx error as its context. It also follows no redirect any more, whatever the HTTP client it was given does (httpx follows one inside its send, out of reach of the release, and carries the `X-Auth-Token` header to another host), and no 3xx answer is a result: the client takes any status below 400 for success, so with no redirect followed a bodyless 3xx read as `{}`, an empty success and a false pass for a write as much as for a read. Every 3xx, 300 to 399, on every path (a read, a write, a mutation, an upload, a ranged read, a streamed read and the head `content_answer` reads) is now a `PlatformError` that names the status and says whether a `Location` came, never its value, and its body is never read; the client's read retry and a probe's do not repeat it (`converge` and `converge_each` count it as a failed read, as they do every status, and end as not converged within their bound). The fakes' request logs (`Request`, of `FakeData`, `FakeFederation` and `MockOnedata`) kept every body too: `body` is now kept up to `BODY_KEPT_BYTES` (64 KiB) and larger bodies are recorded by `size` and `sha256`, so a test that read `len(r.body)` of a large write reads `r.size`. As a fallback, not the fix, `OnezoneClient(collect_after_bytes=)` (128 MiB by default, `None` for none) runs a full collection before the next exchange once that many bytes of bodies, sent and received, were moved.
- A REST read exposed no HTTP status and no `Content-Range` to its caller, so a study recorded every read as a 200 with no range, even a ranged read answered 206 or one clipped at the file's end (#198, O37). `OnezoneClient.read_range` and `read_content`, and `Provisioner.fetch` and `read_file` through them, take `answers` and report each accepted answer as a `streamed.ReadOutcome` (status, `Content-Range` as sent, body bytes); the day-0 outage check records them; and the data gate's line for every answer of a file's content carries `content_range` (null on a 200). A range read also refuses a 206 whose range ends past the end of the file it names, or ends before it starts, which it used to accept as an empty answer at the end of the file.
- The first `up` of a live slot failed on one panel read answered 500 ("GET <provider> panel storages"), and the retry passed. `deploy/compose-federation`'s panel reads are now tried again, at most five times in all, after waits of 1, 2, 4 and 8 s (no wait over 10 s), when the panel answered a server error or nothing; a refusal fails at once, and a panel write (the support call) is never tried again, since one that failed with a server error may have been received.
- The reset could certify its quiet check before an eviction's change had shown in the occupancy (O46). The occupancy stage read the space root's physical size right after the eviction, taking an eviction's change to show at once, but the 14 Nov live slot saw a mirror's root size drop 20.03 s after an eviction at depth 3 was scheduled, as late as a placement's bound. The figure then still held the evicted bytes, and the quiet stage, held to it, passed a growth up to their size; with nothing allowed, a good eviction failed the occupancy stage instead. The occupancy stage now waits `resetqos.occupancy_wait_s` from the eviction stage's end, the placement's lag 10 + 5 x (L - 1) s for the deepest regular file of the space plus the 5 s margin, counted from that end (at or after every eviction job's finish, the conservative reading) and on every reset, since an earlier run's eviction or transfer may still be showing. It then requires no transfer running, reads, and keeps the reading only when no transfer ran or ended since the wait began (the first page of each provider's ended listing, read at the eviction stage's end and after the reading, and the waiting and ongoing listings); otherwise it waits again from when none runs, up to `reset.OCCUPANCY_ROUNDS` (3) waits, and then fails. **The quiet stage now fails when a transfer ran or ended since the occupancy stage's wait began**, since an eviction then can hide a growth in its own reading. A listing shows a job up to `transfer_settle_s` late (O07), so the look that must see every job is made that long after the reading it vouches for: the quiet stage's, or the occupancy stage's own when the quiet stage is skipped. The reset's own jobs (its evictions, whose ids `eviction.evict_ids` and `evict_tree` now hand a new `scheduled` callback, and its cancels) never count as new. The occupancy record adds `waited_s`, `depth`, `rounds` and `listings_after_s`, and the quiet record `listings_after_s`. **Every reset takes that much longer:** 20 s for files one directory below the root, 5 s more per further level, and `transfer_settle_s` (30 s by default) for the late look. The test fake's directory size now lags an eviction as it lags a placement.
- A replication that completed short of the file is scheduled again (O01, O44). On 14 Nov the live slot's preallocated reset replicated the completed file to its mirror and got `completed` with the mirror holding the prefix alone, 1,577,178 B short. Nothing scheduled the job again, so the restore failed at the 300 s settle bound with the mirror's replica not restored. On the 25.0 development build (op-worker f118ed9) a replication runs from the target's own dbsync copy of the other providers' file locations. `GET /data/{id}/distribution` through any provider gathers each provider's own map instead, so a replication scheduled before the copy holds the last write fetches only what the copy shows, and no REST call shows the lag (`deploy/compose-federation/day0/2026-11-14-federation-facts-replication-lag.md`). `Provisioner.replicate_file`, and `replicate` through it, now schedule the replication again while the target's own block map is short after a job ended. The pauses are 5 s, then 10 s, then 15 s (`REPLICATION_RETRY_PAUSES_S`), with each check waiting up to `REPLICATION_CHECK_S`, 10 s, and at most `REPLICATION_ATTEMPTS`, five tries, all within `settle_timeout_s` from the first job's end: one deadline cuts a later job's wait and every check, and no job is scheduled once a check and its pause leave no time. A map that never covers the file is still `NotPlaced`, and the message names the tries; a later try still running at the deadline is `WaitTimeout`. The record adds `tries`, `transfer_ids` (every job's id, each also in the change log) and `converged_s`, the seconds from the first job's end until the map covered the file; `transfer_states` counts every try's job. A preallocated reset's `restore.files[label]` adds `replications`: the `tries`, `transfer_ids` and `converged_s` of each provider replicated to, by position. Every caller gets the retry: the reset's mirror replica, provisioning, and the day-0 outage check's seeding. The other paths are safe as they are. The arrival mode restores no replica. `reset.py` schedules no replication. `place_ranges` places by range reads judged by the receiver's own map, so a lag there fails closed as `NotPlaced` and never passes. The fake models the lag (`FakeData.replication_view_lag_s`), with the block map read fresh through any provider.
- A start a preallocated ingest's baseline mark refused consumed an attempt (O45, C33). The refusal was `InfraError("ingest_failure")` inside the attempt, which is charged, so every refused start used one of the run's `max_attempts`; and an `open` mark held by a live process, a run in progress or an instrument dropped without `restore()` in the campaign's own process, read healthy, so the gate stayed open while every start was refused and charged until the runs were exhausted. A held start is now `ingest_held`, a hold kind that costs no attempt (`register_infra_kind(..., charged=False)`, `expcore.errors.uncharged_kinds`), and the post-run check after it refuses the same way. Each mark names its owner (`expcore.campaign.liveness.owner()`: the attempt's campaign and row, and the process by boot id, pid and start time, as the lease pool names a holder), and the start takes the baseline's hold (`expcore_onedata.ingesthold`, an exclusive `flock` beside the journal) before it marks the baseline open, letting it go after the restore's `whole` mark, at the end of its attempt (`liveness.on_end`), when its instrument is collected, or with its process. An `open` mark is orphaned, as unhealthy as an `unhealthy` one, the moment the store holds its attempt's result, its process is gone, or its hold is free (an attempt of another process that ended, an instrument dropped), with no wait for the run's ceiling. Given the journals, the health check defers while a live attempt holds a baseline, in a check outside any attempt, so the campaign holds its runs and asks again every `recheck_s`, and it fails on an orphaned or unhealthy baseline with `Health(..., charge=False)`, a new field: the gate closes and holds every run, the one that found it included, charging none, until `recover()`; a post-run check that meets such a failure sets its attempt aside uncharged (`outage_discarded`). Only the attempt that wrote the failing mark is charged, in its own post-run check, which reports its own failure first and also fails on its own open mark, its ingest having ended without its reset. An instrument's refusal of a hold kind decides over whatever else set the attempt aside, an unverified fault restore included (it is never scored either way), the exit of a fault injector's session or run whose restore failed keeps a held start as it was, and a hold that cannot be read refuses a recovery. An attempt is live from its 'running' row until the store holds its result (`liveness.running`, which the attempt runner enters and the scheduler registers its store for).
- A fault injector's restore that was not verified was passed silently by every caller that handles only a raising exit (O11). `FaultInjector.session` and `running` cleared in `finally` and returned normally when the restore failed (the provider never served again, its identity did not change, the start failed), with the answer only in `clear()` (False) and the snapshot's `ok` (false), and the instrument route (`start_instruments`, then `finish_instruments`) recorded the failed snapshot without refusing the attempt. Three things now stop it. The exit of `session` raises `RestoreNotVerified`, an `InfraError` of kind `injector_failure` naming the provider's label, how the restore ended and what was checked (the identity changed, the provider served), never a token; it is raised from the exception the start or the body raised, or, for an interrupt or a cancellation, that is raised as it was with the failure in its notes. The exit of `running` returns, so an executor that records the run inside the `async with` keeps its record, and the record carries the failure itself: the injector's snapshot, which is the record's instrument reading, holds `restore_verified` (an optional field of the reading, so `expcore-record/3` is unchanged) as `true` for a verified restore and as `false`, with `how`, `identity_changed` and `serving`, for one that was not. A `running` whose body raised has no record to carry it and still raises the restore failure from the body's exception. The campaign refuses to score a record that carries `restore_verified` as anything but `true` (`expcore.records.injections.restore_refusal`): `RunRecord.set_aside` is true for it, so re-scoring skips it, and the scheduler sets the attempt aside as `injector_failure` in the place it applies an instrument's refusal, whatever the attempt's outcome was, with or without an instrument check, and whatever the post-run health check decided (an attempt the health check or an outage already set aside keeps that outcome, and is not scored either); the attempt is run again, charged as an infrastructure failure. A process that has not registered `injector_failure` (the core alone, with a reading filed by hand) sets it aside as `post_check_unverified` instead, so the refusal never fails for an unknown kind. `FaultInjector.check`, the post-run check `finish_instruments` calls, refuses the same restore as before, and the journal still marks the injection open (`clear_failed`), so the health gate fails for it and the next injector's start is refused until a later clear verifies it. A study that wired `session` and caught only the exit now sees a failed restore as an `injector_failure`: an attempt it ran on such a federation is set aside, as for a failed start. `INJECTOR_FAILURE` is now named in `expcore.records.injections`, which the platform's kinds module imports, so the core can set an attempt aside as that kind.
- `exec(code, timeout_s, record=False)` reports a kill by the sandbox's own bound (#220, O39). A harness step starts no timer, so its only limit is the container's `timeout -s KILL`, a second after `timeout_s`, and a step killed there returned exit 137 with `timed_out` false, as a step that killed itself does: a caller could not tell a hung read from a crash. `ExecResult.timed_out` is now true for it, and the new `ExecResult.bound` names the bound (`cell_timer`, `container_timeout` or `host_call`; None exactly when `timed_out` is false), also in the `harness_steps` record, which gains a `bound` field. The time tells them apart, since the backstop leaves nothing else: exit 137 or 124 after the whole of the limit is the bound's kill, and a step that exits so sooner is its own crash. A cell of the agent's (`record=True`) is unchanged.
- `McpToolProvider.execute` ignored its `timeout_s` (#204, L16), so a tool call made outside the agent loop (a pre-run check, the day-0 outage check, a health probe) to a server that was up and never answered hung the attempt for ever, holding the lease and the federation, with nothing recorded. The call now ends at `timeout_s` with `CallTimedOut`, an `InfraError` of kind `knowledge` that names the tool and the bound (`tool`, `bound_s`); a non-positive bound raises `ValueError` before the call. Inside the loop `timeout_s` is the agent's time left, so the bound is the loop's own and never longer, and the watch judges the call's expiry at that bound as the loop's own cut (a time cap, or the ceiling's infrastructure, the call recorded `cut`), whichever the event loop runs first, so a hung call at the cap is never set aside as `knowledge` infrastructure. `CallTimedOut` lives in `expcore.errors` (re-exported by `expcore.tools.mcp_client`). The day-0 outage check records the client's own expiry at its bound as `blocked` and timed out, as it records its own wait running out.
- A leased slot no longer stays quarantined for its tokens' whole lifetime after every run (C19). The probe held a slot until its tokens' journalled expiry plus 60 s, the run's whole-time ceiling plus 1,260 s with the default `[tokens]` (about 25 h at an 88,200 s ceiling), however soon the run ended, because no token was ever revoked. A launch that held a lease now revokes the slot's tokens on its way out: after the sandbox's stop and the tool server's, and only when no mint or start thread of the key runs, each of the slot's two users revokes all its own temporary tokens with its own credential (`DELETE /user/tokens/temporary`, no privilege, nothing of another slot or of the fixed principals). The revocation counts only when each user's generation (`GET /user/tokens/temporary`) has risen and every token the run held answers `POST /tokens/verify_access_token` with 400 `tokenRevoked`; then the token journal records a revoked line whose `settle_until` is `[tokens] revocation_settle_s` later (new, 330 s by default: the worst case of a provider purging its cache after a lost connection to Onezone, 300 s, plus a session's 15 s recheck; a settle below that bound of 315 s is refused, in platform.toml and when passed directly). A revoked line caps every line of its slot before the revocation's start line, which is journalled before the first bump, an indeterminate one included; a line after the start line, a mint that may hold a token of the new generation, keeps its own end, so a process that dies right after the cap leaves no valid token capped. The probe compares a settle end with the clock with no margin; the release rule is the sandbox shown gone and the tokens expired, or revoked, confirmed and settled, so a slot frees about 6.5 min after its run. Any failure journals no revoked line and the expiry rule stands, which the sandbox's metadata records under `token_revocation`; a sandbox not shown gone stays quarantined. `python -m expcore_onedata.lease revoke --platform platform.toml --leases DIR --slot NAME` runs the same revocation for a slot held from before. It holds the slot's lease lock throughout, so no pool can lease, reap or free the slot meanwhile, and refuses a slot whose lock a live process holds (a run, or a running campaign keeping it quarantined: stop the campaign first) or whose sandbox is not shown gone. The three calls are in `UNVERIFIED_ENDPOINTS`, with a live contract test that runs with `EXPCORE_LIVE_REVOKE=1`. The study guide's slot planning gains the sizing rule that follows.
- The mount hung on the first read of a second space (O36). oneclient 25.0 started with neither `--force-proxy-io` nor `--force-direct-io` detects each storage's access type at the first read of each space, and for a second space on a storage already detected as proxied it takes a plain mutex and calls itself again with the mutex held, which locks it twice on one thread (`src/cache/helpersCache.h`, `performAutoIOStorageDetection`, oneclient tag `bb8a63e3`). The helper's promise was never fulfilled, so the read waited for ever with nothing logged: the first space a mount read worked and the first read of any other hung (seen live on 13 Nov). The sandbox now always starts oneclient with the declared mode's force option, so the detection never runs; `"direct"`, which takes a different path with no such lock pattern, is refused at load until the storage mapping exists (#223).
- A cell's hard deadline (#212, O34) had no bound on the cell's own wait for its outcome: an escalation that stalled held the cell's `exec`, and the worker thread under it, for ever, and so `asyncio.run`'s shutdown. Every wait for the outcome now lasts at most the margin and 30 s, and an outcome not decided by then is `survived_kill`, the attempt `cell_unkillable`.
- At a cell's deadline, a kill whose `HostOps` call raised fell back on the quiesce, ignored what it found and recorded nothing, so the cell was the agent's own `time_limit` and later cells still ran. The escalation now records the step that raised and its error, and the cell is `killed` only when the quiesce shows every process gone; otherwise it is `survived_kill`, `cell_unkillable`, the run is ended and the sandbox's stop ordered (O34).
- An escalation's outcome at a cell's deadline never gets less severe than a step it already took (`killed` < `mount_aborted` < `sandbox_stopped` < `survived_kill`, O34). A kill that raised after a successful abort, with a quiesce that then found nothing left, was `killed` again: the agent's own `time_limit`, with the run's `ended` unset and later cells allowed on the dead mount. It is now `mount_aborted` at least, `cell_unkillable`, with the run ended and later cells refused.
- The fake's tombstone (`FakeData`, #201) counted its `tombstone_s` from the clock of the read that settled a delayed delete, not from the delete's scheduled landing time, so a read after a long jump of the clock still answered from a tombstone that had expired: with `delete_delay_s` 5 and `tombstone_s` 10, a delete at 1000 and a `size` read at 1016 answered 200 where the tombstone, up at 1015, must answer 400 `posix`/`enoent`. The tombstone of a delete now starts at its scheduled landing time (and of one landed early by `land_deletes`, when it landed), and so do the tombstones of what was under a deleted directory.
- `PlacementSettings` (#196, O25) took `fresh_connection_bytes` before `probe_attempts`, so a caller passing the settings positionally had every later argument shift by one field: `PlacementSettings("data", 2.0, 3600.0, 300.0, 2, 900.0, 16 << 20, 3, 1.0)` set `probe_attempts=1.0` and a probe then failed with a `TypeError`. The field is now the last of the class, the order from before #196 is restored, and a field added later goes last.
- `deploy/compose-federation/federation.py down` (#202) reported "down (already down)" whenever the root was gone and no container or network of the project remained, without looking at the project's volumes. A `down` that removed the root and then found a volume it could not remove raised, and its retry reported success while that volume was still there. With the root gone, `down` now lists the volumes labelled with the project and fails, naming them, if any remain; Compose still runs only over a marked root, so they are removed with `docker volume rm`.
- The day-0 outage check (#178, O28) prepared the scenario's files before it built the fault injector, so a process that had died with the data provider stopped made the preparation fail against that provider and return before the journal's recovery ran: the provider stayed down, no restore was recorded, and a multi-space run went on to the next space. The injector is now built first and its recovery runs before anything is prepared; a provider an earlier run left stopped is restarted and the run goes on, with what was settled in the space's `earlier_injection` facts, and a recovery that cannot be done prepares nothing and halts the spaces after it.
- **The link-check record's lock is taken on an NFS root (O35).** The lock file, `link-check.lock`, was opened read-only, and NFS implements `flock` as a POSIX lock, which needs a descriptor open for writing to be exclusive, so on an HPC federation's NFSv4 root the exclusive lock failed with EBADF: `up` said the record's lock could not be taken, and a campaign's routine, restore and heal link checks there would have left no record, since a check that cannot be ordered publishes no verdict. The file is now opened for writing (still created 0644 by its owner), and read-only only when this process cannot write it (EACCES, EROFS, EPERM: another user's file, a read-only mount, an immutable file), which a local filesystem locks all the same and NFS refuses, so on NFS a check by a user who cannot write the lock file still runs unordered and publishes nothing, as before. No other exclusive `fcntl.flock` in `src/` or `deploy/` is taken on a read-only descriptor.
- **A link check whose record was not written says so in the run's record, not only in a log line (O35).** A check that could not be serialized (the record's lock not taken) or whose record could not be written leaves no record and removes the older one, and until now only a warning said so: the run whose restore, heal or routine check it was had nothing of it in its record, so a study could not tell a run whose link verdict was never published from one whose verdict was. The report of such a check now says `lock_not_taken` or `write_failed` (`LinkReport.not_written`, set by `fault_serves.recorded`, never part of the record file, whose bytes are unchanged), the matrix of a restore's and a heal's reads carries it, and it reaches the run as an optional fact, `link_record: "not_written"` with `link_record_reason`: at the top of the fault injector's snapshot (`instruments.fault_injector`, with a `link_record` step, in a `recovered` step for a start's recovery, and in each of the heal's matrices), in the day-0 outage check's `restore` facts and so in its report, and, for a routine check, in `PlatformHealth.link_record` and in the try's note (`prerun.current()`, key `linkrecord.LINK_RECORD_NOTE`) for the run whose pre-run check it was, which an executor puts in that run's record. Additive: both keys are absent, never null, when the record was written, the record schema is unchanged, and a restore, a heal or a gate's verdict is what it was.
- The same check read both mount files after the restore but kept only the owner-only one, so a replicated file that came back with other bytes, or failed with an errno, was dropped and the check passed (#178, O28). Both are kept (`after.mount_owner_only` and `after.mount_replicated`), are read again until each is served or the settle timeout, and a `differs` or an error on either fails the check.
- This entry said the day-0 outage check had never run live, and that the fault injector's, the sandbox's and the tool server's tests had not either. The check passed live on 12 Nov with the injector, a real sandbox in mount mode and the real tool server (`deploy/compose-federation/day0/2026-11-12-federation-facts-provider-outage-rerun.md`). The lede now points to the facts files for what each live run established, and the known limit names that run (#178).
- A cell's deadline decided only after the cell's bounded wait had given up on it, and so ended the run as `survived_kill`, replaced that end with its own outcome (#226, O34): an abort that freed the cell turned the run's end into `mount_aborted` after the attempt was classified, out of step with the cell's own record, and the collection, refused until then, went ahead. The first end published now stands; the late outcome is kept among the cell's deadlines.
- **A proxy mount's start lines were not in oneclient's log when the collection read it (O54).** oneclient buffers INFO lines for up to 30 s unless `--disable-log-buffering` is passed, and only a direct mount passed it, so `MountIoMode` could read a proxy run's log before its start lines were written, take it as `unknown` and set the attempt aside as `io_mode_integrity` infrastructure. The sidecar now passes the switch for every mount, once. It changes when INFO lines are written and nothing else: no data path, capability, device or AppArmor change, and neither the campaign fingerprint (the I/O mode word) nor the run record's schema or its `mount_io_mode` snapshot changes. A proxy run's sandbox metadata `mount.options` gains `--disable-log-buffering`, as a direct run's always had. **Breaking for a study's platform file:** a `[sandbox.mount] extra_args` that carries `--disable-log-buffering` (or an abbreviation of it) is now refused at load in either mode, where it was refused with direct I/O only, since oneclient refuses a switch given twice; drop it. Not run live: that a proxy run's start lines are in the file at collection (O54 in `docs/regressions.md`).
- **An ended replication or eviction read `conflicting`, never final, so a poll for its end never ended and the probes' transfer bounds were unknown for every run that replicated (O32).** A record's state was the set of its status fields, any disagreement conflicting, but every 25.0 record carries both halves and marks the one its job does not do `skipped`: an ended replication reads `transferStatus` and `replicationStatus` `completed` beside `evictionStatus` `skipped`. The first federation study's run at core `39bba713` found all 1,080 answers of its 20 replications so: the window carried 23 earlier jobs over, `transfer_not_ended` and `transfer_carried_over` left the bound into every replication's target unknown though the bytes reconciled exactly, the ended listing's walk never stopped at an earlier job, and the study's persona, polling `job_of(...).state` for a final state, ran 54 rounds more, to its token cap. The state is now read per kind (`transferrecord.state_of`, from op-worker `f118ed9`): a replication's from its replication half, an eviction's from its eviction half, and a migration's from the half it is in, final only once both are. `skipped` on the unused half contradicts nothing, and fields that do contradict each other still read `conflicting`: a summary other than the one op-worker derives from the halves, an unused half that is not `skipped`, a migration whose replication failed and whose eviction completed, a migration half its state rests on missing, or provider ids that name another kind than the record's `type`, which op-worker derives from them. `consistency.transfer_state`, which the waits for a transfer's end and the day-0 outage check read, and the probes' `transfers[].state` read the same rule; the first field present used to win there, which on a record without `transferStatus` read an active eviction's `replicationStatus: skipped` as ended. `effectiveJobStatus`, the state of the job's newest rerun, is not read. The fakes (`FakeData`, and the tests' reset and outage fakes) answered one half alone, which is why no test met the shape; they now answer it as op-worker does, both halves with the unused one `skipped` and its provider id null, and `transferStatus` and `effectiveJobStatus` derived from the halves (a test that sets a half through `provider_view` gets the summary that follows from it, unless it sets one too).
- **An exec that did not complete raised nothing and read as exit 0 with no output (O55).** A live Day-0 read cell came back as exit 0 with nothing printed. The core passed `docker exec`'s exit code and bytes through, and two things below it give that result: a program lost on its way to `python -`'s stdin runs as an empty program, and the docker client reports exit 0 for an exec whose exit the daemon never recorded (docker/cli v29.2.1, `getExecExitStatus`). Both reach every cell, the agent's included. Each cell now runs under a completion bootstrap that checks the program whole before it runs it and, after the interpreter exits, writes a record with the exec's nonce and exit status on both streams; the sandbox takes the record out of the output, so what the model sees and the record keeps is the program's own output, byte for byte. An exec that no timeout or kill explains and that is not verified raises `completion.ExecIncomplete` (kind `sandbox`, also a `SandboxError`), naming the cause: the agent's attempt is set aside as retried infrastructure, and a Day-0 check records a gap. A program that exits 0 and prints nothing stays a success. Each code call in the run record gains the optional `exec_check` (`source_whole`, `exit_status`, `nonce_matched`), additive in version 3 with fixture `07-exec-check.json`; `HostOps.docker` takes `marker=` and `CmdResult` gains `stdout_marks`, `stderr_marks` and `input_error`. **For a `HostOps` of a study's own:** pass `marker` through, or every cell raises. Not run live: which mechanism the live run met, and the bootstrap under a real `docker exec` (O55 in `docs/regressions.md`).
- **A transfer the harness scheduled before the run, a reset's eviction, made every run's transfer bounds unknown (O08, O32).** The probes flagged `harness_transfer_not_listed` whenever a job of the provisioner's log was missing from the accepted listing, but since O32 the ended walk never keeps a job that ended before the window's cutoff, and every reset evicts before its window: a live smoke run's split into the local provider was unknown though its gained bytes equalled its transfer bytes in exactly. A harness job the listing lacks now flags nothing when a record of it, read on every tracked provider, says it ended (a final state) with a finish time after 0 and before the cutoff and no readable final record gives another finish time; `space_transfers.harness_before_window` counts those. A copy that has not ended contradicts nothing, and an unreadable record neither confirms nor contradicts; with no such record, or a final one that gives another finish time, none, 0, or one at or after the cutoff, the job still leaves every bound unknown.
- **A shard step's snapshot thread read the first attempt's beginning as a run's end (O62).** The thread starts before the dispatcher has made `out/runs/`, so its first reading was None; at its first look the first attempt had begun and the count of ended attempts was 0, which it compared with None and read as a run's end, so a snapshot held an attempt in flight, against the rule that a step snapshots after a run's end or at the clock's. No reading now counts as 0. The HPC rehearsal had exposed it: its kill was timed by the snapshot that holds the third attempt in flight, and its wait accepted any snapshot that held any running attempt, so with the first attempts slower than the 30 s poll the kill followed that early snapshot, and the export listed three lost attempts that had ended and one in flight, where it expects one and one. The spend recorded was right, nothing counted twice; the test's expectation was not. The rehearsal now holds the second attempt over the first look, so every run takes the early snapshot, accepts only the snapshot of the first two attempts ended and the third in flight, and checks that what the export lists as lost with what the restored snapshot holds is exactly what the killed life journaled.
- **Two federations of one account could be placed on one node, and the second one's `up` then failed on Couchbase's fixed port (O57, #398).** Slurm packs held jobs by cores and memory, and the services have no network namespace, so nothing kept two federations' services of one account (a fleet's members, parallel slots) apart. `up` now reads the account's live jobs read-only (`squeue --me`) before it submits and excludes the nodes of every job whose name starts with `hpcop-` from each component and held job (the agent's sandbox never runs beside a service, another federation's included), records and says the count and never a name, and refuses when the read fails. A second read once the job runs and before any step refuses a node that another live federation holds (two ups that raced), cancels the held jobs and names the role (`zone`, `providers[i]`, `agent`). A job the exclusion keeps pending ends at the queue bound `up` always had and says why. What the site's `sbatch` does when the exclusion leaves no node that fits is not seen live.
- **A shard on a core that names no code reached its node before it was refused (C06, P01).** A core installed by `git archive` (no `.git`, no `expcore/_build_identity.json`) has an unknown commit, and `expcore run` refuses to start on one, so a shard's step started on its node and failed there; the first real-model probe on the HPC allocation found it. `shards.py plan` and `lines` now ask the manifest's `python` which core it imports (a process of its own, `-I`, under the step's PATH and no home), and `step` reads its own interpreter's, before anything is made or printed: an unknown core, or an installed one edited since its build (stale), is refused, saying to use a git checkout of the core or a wheel built with its build identity (`hatch build` writes `expcore/_build_identity.json`). A dirty core is left to the run's own refusal. `fleet.py plan` (before it records anything) and `fleet.py step` (when it has a line to launch) ask the same, since the fleet builds its lines itself. The HPC README's note on the core now gives that recipe, a checkout or a wheel and never a copy, and the study guide's first-start notes say that a model on an endpoint that may answer from more than one deployment needs `accepted_fingerprints` in `models.toml`, or a run whose served identity changes is set aside as endpoint infrastructure (C16).

### Security

- Every Python the HPC federation's driver launches runs isolated and writes no bytecode, and every step it launches starts in `/` (O58, O57). The login node's fault relay ran as `python -B -m expcore_onedata.fault_relay serve|alive|stop`, so Python put the directory `up` or `down` was launched from first on `sys.path` and read every `PYTHON*` variable. On 16 Nov a `down` launched from the login node's `/tmp`, which held 49 `.py` files of other users, had the relay's `stop` import another user's `/tmp/inspect.py` in place of the standard library's; it crashed on that file's first line, so `down` listed the relay unverified. An `up` launched there would have run another user's module as our uid in the relay, which serves the federation's root, its secrets included, for the federation's whole life, and in every verb it runs. Now every Python argv the driver, its bundle, its run harness, `shards.py` and the test runner build takes `-I -B`: the relay's three commands, the verbs' argv prefix (the state file's `command` and the `[faults] command` `up` renders), the shard step, its dispatcher and `expcore run`, `shards.py collect`'s merge line (which `--run` now runs in the 0700 directory it collected into, refusing one others may enter), the bundle's helpers and in-container probes, and the test runner's pytest; and in the core's own `src/`, the five launches that run an interpreter on a node (the sandbox's gate, the mount watcher, the egress collector, the completion check of an exec and the copy-out), which had `-I` alone, so `-I` and `-B` always come together there too (a scan of every `-I` display of `src/` says so). `-I` also ignores every `PYTHON*` variable and the user's site-packages, and `-B` keeps bytecode out of a shared interpreter or checkout (another lane's finding: a node step wrote `.pyc` into the shared interpreter); and the relay works in the federation's root, a 0700 directory of ours that no symbolic link reaches (it is not started in any other, and `up` refuses a root a link reaches in its preflight, before anything is submitted), never in the directory `up` was launched from. Separately, a step srun launched without a working directory started in the login node's, or, where that does not exist on the node, in the node's shared `/tmp` (slurmstepd falls back to it; another study's audit): the service, control, teardown, core and shard steps, and the run harness's, now name `--chdir=/`, as the held job's `sbatch` and the test runner's steps already did, and a relative interpreter (`HPCOP_PY`, a shard manifest's `python`) or a relative `B`, `SH`, `L` or `GATE_DIR` in the run harness's `site.env`, which would now resolve under `/`, is refused. `tests/deploy/test_hpc_isolated_python.py` plants modules named like the standard library's and the core's where the relay is launched, in its root and under `PYTHONPATH` and runs the real relay over them; it scans every Python argv and every `srun` and `sbatch` of `deploy/hpc-federation` and `deploy/hpc-tests` for `-I -B` and `--chdir=/`, and the fake Slurm refuses either missing in every driver test. Launch `up`, `down` and the verbs as `python -I -B deploy/hpc-federation/hpcfed.py`, from a directory of ours all the same: `uv run` takes the project of the working directory when it has a `pyproject.toml`. Not changed here: the two lines the bundle bakes into the Onedata rootfs (the Couchbase guard's, `python3 -I /root/hpcop-cb-init.py ...` in the patched start script and the launcher's wait) keep `-I` alone, since a change would alter what the baked image's check expects (`check-defaults.sh` finds them there verbatim).
- Collecting a run's output no longer follows or keeps a link (#129, O19). `DockerSandbox.collect` copied what the agent left with `cp -r` and `chown -R` under sudo, so a symbolic link the agent made still pointed into the host from the copy, and the study's collector, the record builder and a package build would follow it; a hard link to a file outside the output, a device, a FIFO or a socket was as dangerous. Collection now lists the output as root without following anything and refuses all of these before anything is copied, as it does another file system, a path too long to copy and more bytes than the working disk holds (which only a sparse file reaches). A FIFO, socket, device or mount on the way to an output is refused as well. The agent's container is frozen through its cgroup while its outputs are listed and copied (`docker pause`, confirmed by the cgroup's own state), so nothing that survived the kills can change the tree, and a root helper copies name by name from each parent directory's descriptor with `O_NOFOLLOW`, checking every object against the listing, so a directory swapped for a link after the listing is never read through; the copy is the driver's, and verified by lstat to hold only its directories and files of one link. What the agent made is its own doing: none of the outputs is copied, and the collection names each object by its path under the working directory and what it is, never by a link's target, under `refused_output` in its result and the run's metadata, for the study's scorers; the attempt is not set aside. A collection that fails for the harness's own reasons (a container that cannot be frozen, a copy command or the disk failing, a copy that is not what was listed, a gate log that is not a plain file) is infrastructure of the platform's new kind `output_collection`, which no condition may declare as its treatment, and keeps the sandbox's disks; a copy command that failed raised `SandboxError` before. The driver reads the gate log without following a link. The reaper's copy of a dead run's output follows the same rule; a refused reap keeps that run's disks, and so its lease key, until an operator acts.
- A writing condition's tool-server token has no `api` caveat, so within the tool-server plane's spaces it is a full REST session of its user for every call the tool server makes: what the agent can ask for is bounded by the condition's tool allow-list and its frozen manifest alone. Onedata's `api` caveat could narrow the token to named operations without limiting it to data access, but which operations the tool server's calls are is not in Onedata's caveat source, and one missing entry would refuse a tool, so the token has none (#177).
- The sandbox binds every secret with `--mount type=bind,...,readonly`, which refuses a source that is not there, and its teardown removes an empty directory left at a token's path (O05). On 13 Nov a study's operator ran `docker cp` on the log directory of a running oneclient sidecar; it failed, and the teardown then raised "1 of the run's copies of the data-path token could not be removed" and quarantined the slot. The likely cause, inferred and not shown: `docker cp` re-mounts a running container's binds, and `-v` makes a missing bind source a directory owned by root, so an empty directory stood where the removed token's file had been, and `rm -f` cannot remove one. The sidecar's and the gate's token files and the directory of the gate's TLS keys are now `--mount` binds; the gate's `gate.json`, the CA copies, the gate's log and the agent's binds, which hold no secret, stay `-v`. What `rm -f` leaves is examined without being read, and `metadata()["teardown"]` records each path in the run's directory, its kind, size and owner's uid, and what was done (the run's directory by its id and hash, as every record names one). An empty directory is removed with `rmdir` and does not fail the teardown; a file, a link or a directory with an entry in it still does, and its error names the path and the kind, never a content. `sidecar_run_args` takes `log_dir`, a required keyword argument.
- Collecting a run's evidence no longer needs `docker cp`. oneclient is always started with `--log-dir /var/log/oneclient` (`[sandbox.mount] extra_args` may not give one, in any form), and the run's `oneclient-log` directory, owner-only, is bound there, as the gate's log is bound to `gatelog`, so the log is read from the host, survives its container and is kept with a kept run. `docker cp` from a running sidecar or gate is forbidden (study guide, "Collecting a run's evidence"). The directory is not redacted: oneclient's INFO log held start-up settings and no token on 11 Nov, and the token reaches oneclient through its environment, but the directory stays owner-only.

### Documentation

- A live comparison of the HPC host mount with the Compose mount sidecar, in forced proxy (#179, O60; `deploy/hpc-federation/facts/2026-11-16-slot-7-proxy-equivalence.md`, with its record `2026-11-16-slot-7-proxy-equivalence.json`). The same six files, read by the same script from the same before-maps, came out equal on both halves in bytes, SHA-256 and every provider's block maps before and after, with the same effective I/O mode and the same oneclient build; the file states the differences it explains (the Compose mount's `allow_other`, the token lifetime's margin, read and start times) and what it does not show (direct I/O, timing, files beyond six small ones).

### Known limits

- A complete listing is complete only for a directory that does not change while the gate reads it: Onedata gives a listing no snapshot. An entry another client inserts during the read makes another come twice, and the listing is refused; an entry removed during the read can shift another out of the pages unseen, and the answer lacks it with nothing to show. Entries that carry neither a file id nor a name cannot be told apart, so a repeat among them is not found.
- Threat model of the replication-package checks: they catch accidental leaks of private names and listed terms in text and in common encodings of it (UTF-16, escapes, entities, base64 and hex runs, compressed and archived content, SQLite databases). They do not defend against deliberate concealment, and they never read text inside images.
- The redaction of the package and provenance commands covers the core's own messages, not every form a name can take: a listed name of more than twelve words, or one that ends in punctuation, is not looked for as a phrase in a message; a name a message splits across a line break is not joined; and what a study's own plugin prints while it is run to regenerate the outputs is the study's code, not a core message, and is not redacted. A listed name that is an ordinary phrase can hide a whole message line, which errs on the safe side.
- A package's wheels are those of the build's platform and Python, so a reader needs the same. Every text member of every wheel is read with the study-token list, except in a wheel the study's `uv.lock` proves a pypi.org release, and in an anonymised build with the anonymisation list; a member that is not text is attested by its hash. They add about 65 MB to the example's package. An editable install cannot be packed from its installed files and needs `--wheels`; files a distribution installs outside its site directory are left out of a packed wheel, apart from the console scripts the installer writes again.
- An anonymised package still shows the distribution name `papers-experiments-core`, the module name `expcore` and the core's code; it withholds the vendored code's provenance headers. A release from an index is read as it is, without the decoded views the author's own code gets. With the core's public token list the example's package builds, plain and anonymised, with no wheel exempt and no member attested. A library whose code holds a word of the list is refused when it is packed again from its installed files, since a re-packed wheel never has the bytes of the published artifact the lock records; supplying its published wheel with `--wheels` (the file whose URL and hash `uv.lock` records) exempts it from that list, and attesting the member in `package.toml` works too.
- The package's study-token check uses the hashes whose scope is `all`, the names private everywhere (hosts, addresses, users, spaces, providers). A study's own condition, dataset and model names are expected in its own package, so code-scope hashes are not applied. `--hashes` takes another hash file, for example a study's own, in place of the core's list rather than in addition to it, and the build prints how many hashes it checks with. A hash file with a line in any other format is refused, a bare `sha256sum` digest by name, but one whose lines have the format and whose digests were made some other way cannot be told from a correct one; the study guide gives the format, the hash rule and the command that makes the file.
- Anonymisation relabels only whole words in the prose and metadata files its list names (JSON lexically, string by string, TOML and text as text; YAML is refused, since without a parser its rewrite cannot be checked), and fails the build on a listed term anywhere else, escaped or encoded forms included; it never rewrites code. The scanner and canaries of earlier packages are not in this release.
- The package's checks fail closed. A file goes in only if they can read all of it: UTF-8 text without NUL bytes, UTF-16 text with a byte-order mark, an SQLite database (its values read too), or a gzip, bzip2, xz, zip or tar archive of such files; base64 and hex runs in text are decoded and read the same way. Anything else (a PDF or PostScript file, even in ASCII, a PNG, a raw zlib stream, unknown binary, a member that fails to decompress) is refused unless `package.toml` attests that exact file by its SHA-256 with a reason in an `[[opaque]]` entry; it then goes in unscanned, on the author's word that it was inspected by hand.
- A prompt template nested in another, rendered first and passed as a slot's value, is recorded as that slot's text: the record holds the outer template's hash, and neither the inner template's hash nor its slot values ([`docs/interfaces.md`](docs/interfaces.md#prompttemplate)).
- A file an agent writes during a run has no place in the batch unless the study declares it as a side file: the replay check takes an undeclared `.json` file beside a record as a record and fails it, and hashes no undeclared file of another kind. What the scorer needs of such a file goes into the record as an instrument's snapshot ([`docs/study-guide.md`](docs/study-guide.md#files-an-agent-writes)); the original bytes are not carried.
- A study cannot register an estimator through its plugin: the preregistration is validated when the study is loaded, before the plugin is imported, so a name only the plugin registers is refused as unknown. An installed module named in `[study] platforms`, which is imported first, can register one, and the analysis then uses it ([`docs/interfaces.md`](docs/interfaces.md#estimator)).
- A ratio contrast (S13) under the default estimation unit has no estimate by model, since each cluster's sums pool its models, and it weighs runs, not tasks or models, within a cluster; estimated per model's cell, each model's estimate is a breakdown with no test of its own, and a value's cells weigh the same whatever runs each holds. Its `equivalence_margin` is a natural-log ratio.
